Unrestricted quantum moduli algebras, III: Surfaces of arbitrary genus and skein algebras
Stéphane Baseilhac 1 , Matthieu Faitg 2 , Philippe Roche 3

Abstract
. We prove that the quantum moduli algebra associated to a possibly punctured compact oriented surface and a complex semisimple Lie algebra 𝔤 \displaystyle\mathfrak{g} is a Noetherian and finitely generated ring; if the surface has punctures, we prove also that it has no non-trivial zero divisors. Moreover, we show that the quantum moduli algebra is isomorphic to the skein algebra of the surface, defined by means of the Reshetikhin-Turaev functor for the quantum group U q ​ ( 𝔤 ) \displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) , and which coincides with the Kauffman bracket skein algebra when 𝔤 = 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 2 \displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2} .



1,3
IMAG, Univ Montpellier, CNRS, Montpellier, France.

2
Institut de Mathématiques de Toulouse, 118 route de Narbonne, F-31062 Toulouse, France.

1
stephane.baseilhac@umontpellier.fr,
2
matthieu.faitg@univ-tlse3.fr,
3
philippe.roche@umontpellier.fr

Keywords: quantum groups, skein algebras, invariant theory

AMS subject classification 2020: 17B37, 20G42, 57R56

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Context

The quantum moduli algebras have been introduced in the mid
{}^{\prime}90
s by Alekseev-Grosse-Schomerus
[
AGS95
,
AGS96
,
AS96b
]
and Buffenoir-Roche
[
BR95
,
BR96
]
. They are associative, non commutative algebras defined over the ground field
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
, quantizing the algebras of functions on the moduli spaces of flat
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
-connections on surfaces, where
\displaystyle q
is a formal variable,
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
is a complex simple Lie algebra, and
\displaystyle D
is the corank of the root lattice of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
in the weight lattice (see §
2.3
for the generalization to semisimple Lie algebras).

The main purpose of this paper is to prove that the quantum moduli algebras are Noetherian and finitely generated rings, with no non trivial zero divisors when the surface has punctures, and that they are isomorphic to skein algebras. Theses results should allow one to study the prime and primitive spectra of the skein algebras similarly as those of quantized coordinate rings (see eg.
[
BG02
]
, Part II).

Denote by
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}
the oriented surface with genus
\displaystyle g
and
\displaystyle n
punctures (
\displaystyle n\in\mathbb{N}
), and by
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
the surface with one boundary component, obtained from
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}
by removing an open
\displaystyle 2
-disk (these surfaces are pictured in §
6.1
). To
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
and
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
one can associate an algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
, endowed with an action of the Drinfeld-Jimbo (simply-connected) quantum group
\displaystyle U_{q}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
making
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
a module-algebra. The subalgebra of invariant elements
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}(\mathfrak{g})
is the quantum moduli algebra of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
and
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
. The case
\displaystyle g=0
was studied in
[
BR22
,
BR21
]
.

There are at least two areas of good motivations to study the quantum moduli algebras. On one hand, the definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
relies on Hopf algebra and quantum group theory. It is built by a twisting procedure from
\displaystyle 2g+n
copies of the quantum coordinate algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)
associated to a complex semisimple algebraic group
\displaystyle G
with Lie algebra
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
, and it is therefore a natural problem to study the algebraic structure and representation theory of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}(\mathfrak{g})
.

On another hand, connections with quantum topology are well-established: for instance,
[
AS96a
]
showed that the Witten-Reshetikin-Turaev representations of the mapping class groups of surfaces can be recovered from certain representations of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
,
[
BFK98a
,
BFK98b
]
proved (with a slightly different formalism) that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
is isomorphic to the Kauffman bracket skein algebra of
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
, and recently these results have been extended in
[
Fai19a
,
Fai20a
]
and
[
Fai20b
,
Kor23
]
respectively. It is a natural problem to extend the isomorphism of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}(\mathfrak{g})
with a skein algebra beyond
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
. By using such an isomorphism one can naturally expect that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
, which has an algebraic flavour by definition, provides good tools to study the skein algebras.

Our results in this paper make advances on these two families of problems. Firstly, we show that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}(\mathfrak{g})
are Noetherian, finitely generated rings, and have no non-trivial zero divisors. Such properties are well-known for
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)
and the subalgebra of invariant elements under the coadjoint action of
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
(see eg.
[
VY20
, Prop. 3.117]
). In our context we use also tools, like filtrations, which are standard for quantum groups, and a version of the Hilbert–Nagata theorem in invariant theory. The similar result when
\displaystyle g=0
was obtained in
[
BR21
]
. The present genus
\displaystyle g>0
case is substantially more complicated.

Our second main result is the construction of an (explicit) isomorphism of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}(\mathfrak{g})
with a skein algebra associated to the surface
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
. That skein algebra is defined by means of the Reshetikhin-Turaev functor for the quantum group
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, applied to certain ribbon graphs in
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}\times[0,1]
. When
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, the isomorphism recovers the one obtained in
[
BFK98b
]
. In order to define it, the main tool is a “holonomy map”, which is an isomorphism relating
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
with an algebra of “stated skeins” on
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
, the surface obtained from
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
by removing a point on the boundary component, in the sense of
[
Lê18
,
CL22
]
,
[
LS21
]
and
[
CKL
]
for
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
,
\displaystyle\mathfrak{sl}_{m+1}
and any
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
, respectively. The holonomy map generalizes a construction in
[
Fai20b
, §5]
in the case
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
. For
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{m+1}
, the existence of an isomorphism between
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
and a stated skein algebra was obtained in
[
LS21
]
by using results from factorization homology
[
BBJ18
]
.

Combining our two results we see that the skein algebra of
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
associated to an arbitrary semisimple Lie algebra
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
is a Noetherian and finitely generated ring, and has no non-trivial zero divisors. These facts were proved in the case
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
in
[
Bul99
]
and
[
PS19
]
(including also the case of closed surfaces), and
[
FS22
]
proved finite generation for
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{3}
. For stated skein algebras of any surface and when
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, this was proved in
[
LY22
]
. These papers use geometric techniques based on curves on surfaces. The paper
[
LY23
]
, which appeared online after the first version of the present work, studies the skein algebras for
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{m+1}
.

Finally, the quantum moduli algebra of
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}
, including the case
\displaystyle n=0
, can be defined by using the notion of quantum reduction developed by several authors and applied to
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
. The topological counterpart of this notion is the operation of gluing a
\displaystyle 2
-disk along the boundary component of
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
. We describe quantum reduction in detail, especially for
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
, and show that the resulting algebra is a Noetherian and finitely generated ring, indeed isomorphic to the skein algebra of
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}
. For closed surfaces
\displaystyle(n=0)
, whether or not it has no non trivial zero divisors is still an open question at this stage.

We can formulate most of our constructions for general quasitriangular Hopf algebras
\displaystyle H
, thus obtaining a
\displaystyle H
-module algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
and a subalgebra of
\displaystyle H
-invariant elements
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
. We do so in the text, and then make the required adaptation to handle the case of
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, which is quasitriangular only in a categorical completion. Our results are described with more details in the next subsection.

We note that the algebras
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}(\mathfrak{g})
have integral forms, which are subalgebras
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A})^{U_{A}^{\mathrm{res}}}(\mathfrak{g})
defined over the ground ring
\displaystyle A=\mathbb{C}[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}]
, and such that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A})^{U_{A}^{\mathrm{res}}}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}(\mathfrak{g})
, where
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\mathrm{res}}
is Lusztig’s restricted quantum group associated to the adjoint quantum group
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
. It is because of integrality properties of the
\displaystyle R
-matrix of
\displaystyle U_{A}^{\mathrm{res}}
that the twists involved in the definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
yield a well-defined algebra structure on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Trivially our first main result implies that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A})^{U_{A}^{\mathrm{res}}}(\mathfrak{g})
have no non-trivial zero divisors. By using the Kashiwara-Lusztig theory of canonical basis, we have shown in
[
BR21
]
that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,n}^{A}(\mathfrak{g})
is a Noetherian and finitely generated algebra. We expect that these results still hold true in genus
\displaystyle g>0
. In
[
BFR
]
we study the algebraic properties and representations of these algebras when the parameter
\displaystyle q
is specialized to a root of unity.

1.2.
Summary of results

We first give an overview of the definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
. Let
\displaystyle H
be a quasitriangular Hopf algebra with
\displaystyle R
-matrix
\displaystyle R\in H^{\otimes 2}
, and let
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
be its restricted dual (see §
2
). For
\displaystyle g,n\in\mathbb{N}
, the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is the vector space
\displaystyle(H^{\circ})^{\otimes(2g+n)}
with a product “twisted by
\displaystyle R
”. There is a coadjoint action
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
, which gives it the structure of a right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra (§
4.1
). In particular we have the subalgebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
of
\displaystyle H
-invariant elements for this action.

The definitions and some results will be given for general
\displaystyle H
but we are mainly interested in the case where
\displaystyle H
is a quantum group. So let
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
be a complex simple Lie algebra and
\displaystyle G
be the simply-connected algebraic Lie group with Lie algebra
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
; for the extension to semisimple
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
, see §
2.3
. Fix a formal variable
\displaystyle q
and denote by
\displaystyle U_{q}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
the simply connected Drinfeld-Jimbo quantum group defined over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
, by
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}\subset U_{q}
the adjoint quantum group, and by
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}=\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)
the associated quantized coordinate algebra (see §
2.4
, §
2.6
). In this situation we denote the resulting algebra by
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
or simply
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
, which is
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)^{\otimes(2g+n)}
as a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
-vector space (see §
2.3
for the definition of the integer
\displaystyle D
), and by
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}(\mathfrak{g})
or simply
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}
the subalgebra of
\displaystyle U_{q}
-invariant elements.

The definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
relies on the special cases
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
:

(1)

\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)^{\widetilde{\otimes}g}\>\widetilde{\otimes}\>\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)^{\widetilde{\otimes}n}

where
\displaystyle\widetilde{\otimes}
is the braided tensor product in the braided tensor category of right
\displaystyle H
-modules, as defined in
[
Maj92
, Lem 9.2.12]
and recalled before Proposition
4.3
. Hence the algebras
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
play a special role and have to be examined first. The papers
[
BR22
,
BR21
]
were focused on
\displaystyle g=0
and in particular
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
. Here we are interested in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
for arbitrary
\displaystyle g
. So we start with
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
in §
3
. This algebra is very different from
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
; for instance
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
is strongly related to
\displaystyle H
while
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
is strongly related to the Heisenberg double of
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
(§
3.2
, §
3.3
).

Here is our first main result for the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}:=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
:

Theorem 1

(Theorems
4.11
,
4.17
,
5.8
)
.

1. The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is Noetherian and does not have non-trivial zero divisors.

2. The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}
is Noetherian and finitely generated.

We note that it is not difficult to prove that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is finitely generated (Prop.
4.7
) and that it follows of course from item 1 that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}
does not have non-trivial zero divisors.

Let us discuss a bit how we prove Theorem
1
. The main ideas of the proof are similar to those for
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,n}
in
[
BR22
,
BR21
]
; however when
\displaystyle g>0
the presence of the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
requires many non-trivial generalizations and new computations.

To prove that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is Noetherian, we use filtrations. A filtration for
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
has been introduced in
[
VY20
, §3.14.4]
. In §
3.4
we modify it in order to define a filtration of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
and show that the associated graded algebra is Noetherian, which implies that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
is Noetherian. Then in §
4.3
we first define a filtration of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
whose associated graded algebra transforms the braided tensor product (
1
) into a “quasi-commutative” tensor product; this allows us in a second step to use tensor products of the filtrations of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
in order to get an associated graded algebra which is Noetherian.

The proof of the item 2 of Theorem
1
is based on a generalization of the Hilbert–Nagata theorem. Let
\displaystyle G
be a group acting on a graded algebra
\displaystyle A
in such a way that the action is compatible with the multiplication and the grading. The Hilbert–Nagata theorem gives sufficient conditions for the subalgebra of
\displaystyle G
-invariant elements of
\displaystyle A
to be Noetherian and finitely generated. In §
4.4
we generalize this theorem to the case where
\displaystyle A
is a graded module-algebra over a Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H
. We then apply this general result to the case where
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
and
\displaystyle A
is a “graded truncation” of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
.

The fact that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
does not have non-trivial zero divisors is not proven directly but through a morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
called the Alekseev morphism, which we now discuss. In §
5
, relying on the formulas given in
[
Ale94
]
, we define for any quasitriangular Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H
a morphism of algebras

\Phi_{g,n}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\to\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}.

Here
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
is the “two-sided Heisenberg double”, an algebra which we introduce in §
5.1
and which extends the usual Heisenberg double
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
recalled in §
3.2
. For
\displaystyle g>1
it is necessary to use
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
instead of
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
to make sense of the formulas in
[
Ale94
]
if
\displaystyle H
is not finite-dimensional (this point is explained at the end of §
5.2
). It is important to define and analyze
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
first since their properties are used in the proofs for
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
. The morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
is well-known in quantum group theory (see eg.
[
Bau00
]
), while
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}:\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\to\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
is defined in §
3.2
following
[
Ale94
]
.

Let us return to the case
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
. In that situation
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
takes values in
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(\mathcal{O}_{q})^{\otimes g}\otimes U_{q}^{\otimes n}
. We prove that
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(\mathcal{O}_{q})^{\otimes g}\otimes U_{q}^{\otimes n}
does not contain non-trivial zero divisors (Prop.
5.7
) and that
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
is injective (Th.
5.8
), which implies that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
does not have non-trivial zero divisors. In
[
Bau00
]
it was already proved that
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}\to U_{q}
is injective; moreover, that paper showed that the image of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
is
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}}
, namely the subspace of locally finite elements of
\displaystyle U_{q}
for the adjoint action. In Theorem
3.11
we show a similar result for
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
, that is,
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}:\mathcal{L}_{1,0}\to\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{O}_{q})
is injective and its image is the subspace of locally finite elements for an action of
\displaystyle U_{q}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{O}_{q})
that we introduce in §
3.2
.

In §
6
we relate the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
and its subalgebra of
\displaystyle H
-invariant elements
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
to skein theory. Let
\displaystyle H
be a ribbon Hopf algebra over a field
\displaystyle k
, and let
\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{RT}}:\mathrm{Rib}_{H}\to H\text{-mod}
be the Reshetikhin–Turaev functor, which to an
\displaystyle H
-colored oriented ribbon graph associates some
\displaystyle H
-linear morphism
[
RT90
]
. Let
\displaystyle\Sigma
be an oriented surface, possibly with boundary. The skein algebra of
\displaystyle\Sigma
associated to
\displaystyle H
, denoted by
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma)
, is the
\displaystyle k
-vector space generated by the isotopy classes of
\displaystyle H
-colored oriented ribbon links (with coupons) modulo the skein relations:

The
\displaystyle T_{i}
are any ribbon graphs and the
\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\in k
are any scalars such that the linear equation on the left holds. The right hand-side represents a linear combination of links which are equal outside of the cube in
\displaystyle\Sigma\times[0,1]
which is depicted in grey. The product of two links
\displaystyle L_{1},L_{2}
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma)
is obtained by putting
\displaystyle L_{1}
below
\displaystyle L_{2}
in
\displaystyle\Sigma\times[0,1]
.

The stated skein algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}^{\mathrm{st}}(\Sigma)
is a generalization of
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma)
where one uses ribbon graphs in
\displaystyle\Sigma\times[0,1]
instead of links. The endpoints of these ribbon graphs are required to be in
\displaystyle\partial\Sigma\times[0,1]
and are labelled by “states”
i.e.
vectors in
\displaystyle H
-modules. Each boundary circle of
\displaystyle\Sigma
must contain at least one puncture. Stated skein algebras have been introduced and studied for
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
in
[
Lê18
,
CL22
,
Kor23
]
and for
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{m+1})
in
[
LS21
]
. The general definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}^{\mathrm{st}}(\Sigma)
for any ribbon Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H
will appear in
[
CKL
]
. In the present paper we will deal with stated skeins for the surface
\displaystyle\Sigma=\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
obtained by removing one point
\displaystyle\bullet
on the circle
\displaystyle\partial(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
. The definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}^{\mathrm{st}}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
is explained in §
6.1
, as a particular case of
[
CKL
]
.

In
[
Fai20b
, §4.1]
a “holonomy map” hol has been defined, which to a
\displaystyle H
-colored oriented ribbon graph
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
in
\displaystyle(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})\times[0,1]
associates a tensor
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}(\mathbf{T})
with coefficients in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
. The type of the tensor
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}(\mathbf{T})
depends of the orientation and the number of endpoints of
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
. The map hol generalizes the Reshetikhin–Turaev functor to the surfaces
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
. In §
6.2
we refine it to a “stated holonomy map”
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{S}_{H}^{\mathrm{st}}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
, which is a morphism of algebras due to the properties of hol. Then in §
6.3
we note that there is a natural algebra morphism
\displaystyle I:\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\to\mathcal{S}_{H}^{\mathrm{st}}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
simply obtained by seeing a link as a ribbon graph without boundary points. When restricted to links,
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}
are equal and give a morphism
\displaystyle W=\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}\circ I:\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
(§
6.3
). In this way we recover the “Wilson loop map”
\displaystyle W
already defined and studied in
[
BR96
,
BFK98a
]
(the latter paper also defined holonomy for certain tangles called
\displaystyle q
-nets). Our second main result is:

Theorem 2

(Theorems
6.5
and
6.9
)
.

1.
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{S}_{H}^{\mathrm{st}}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is an isomorphism of algebras.

2. If the category
\displaystyle H\text{-}{\rm mod}
of finite dimensional
\displaystyle H
-modules is semisimple, then
\displaystyle W:\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
is an isomorphism of algebras.

This result is a generalization of
[
Fai20b
, §5]
for the first item and of
[
BFK98b
]
(see
[
BR22
, §8.2]
in our context) for the second item, which proved it for
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. If
\displaystyle H\text{-}\mathrm{mod}
is not semisimple the second item fails because in general
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
is bigger than
\displaystyle\mathrm{im}(W)
, as explained in §
6.4
. To sum up:

When
\displaystyle H\text{-}\mathrm{mod}
is semisimple we get in particular that the morphism
\displaystyle I
is injective (Corollary
6.12
). This is an interesting consequence, because it seems more difficult to prove this fact by using only skein theory.

Take
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
. The category
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
of type
\displaystyle 1
finite dimensional
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules is semisimple. Write
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
for the stated skein and skein algebras of the respective surfaces
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
,
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
and
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}
, where the ribbon graphs are colored by objects and morphisms in
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
. They are
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
-algebras. For
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
these algebras are respectively the Kauffman bracket skein algebra and the stated skein algebra of
[
Lê18
,
CL22
]
. For
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{m+1}(\mathbb{C})
they can be described in terms of special ribbon graphs called webs
[
Sik05
,
LS21
]
.

The conjunction of Theorems
1
and
2
finally gives:

Corollary 3
.

1. The stated skein algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
is finitely generated, Noetherian and does not have non-trivial zero divisors.

2. The skein algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
is finitely generated, Noetherian and does not have non-

trivial zero divisors.

In §
7
we extend our results to the surface
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}
. It is a fact that the skein algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n})
is a quotient of
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
(see §
7.4
). Then the item 2 of Corollary
3
implies

Corollary 4
.

The skein algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
is finitely generated and Noetherian.

We consider the quantum reduction
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{qr}}(H)
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
associated to the counit
\displaystyle\varepsilon\colon H\rightarrow k
(see below), and we show that, under suitable hypothesis satisfied eg. in the case of
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
, we have:

Theorem 5

(§
7.3
and §
7.4
)
.

1. There is a surjective algebra morphism
\displaystyle\pi\colon\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{qr}}(H)
.

2. The isomorphism
\displaystyle W:\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
descends to an algebra isomorphism
\displaystyle W^{\rm qr}:\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{qr}}(H)
.

The notion of quantum reduction of module algebras over quantum groups has been introduced in
[
Lu93
]
as an analog of Hamiltonian reduction in symplectic geometry
[
MaWe74
]
(see
[
AM98
]
for the broader notion of quasi-Hamiltonian reduction, well-suited to character varieties of surface groups).

In that setup, one considers a symplectic space
\displaystyle X
endowed with a Hamiltonian action of a Lie group
\displaystyle G
generated by a moment map
\displaystyle\mu^{cl}\colon X\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, and the reduction procedure describes symplectic leaves in
\displaystyle X/G
as quotients
\displaystyle(\mu^{cl})^{-1}(\mathcal{C})/G
, where
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
is a coadjoint orbit in
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.

In the quantum setup one works dually, and considers a module algebra
\displaystyle A
over a Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H
. The quantum moment map is a morphism of algebras
\displaystyle\mu\colon H^{\prime}\rightarrow A
, where
\displaystyle H^{\prime}\subset H
is a coideal subalgebra, and
\displaystyle\mu
satisfies an equation which guarantees that it generates the action of
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
on
\displaystyle A
. The reduction procedure describes algebras of invariant elements in certain quotients of
\displaystyle A
, defined by means of
\displaystyle\mu
and characters of
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
.

An ansatz of quantum reduction was implemented in
[
Ale94
]
and
[
BNR02
]
. Its proper definition and fundamental properties were settled in
[
VV10
]
, which applied it to double affine Hecke algebras (which are closely related to
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{1,0}(\mathfrak{g})
). Quantum reduction was also applied to quivers in
[
Jor14
]
and
[
GJS19
]
, where it was axiomatized in a categorical setting.

In the case
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\rm ad}(\mathfrak{g})
, the quantum reduction in Theorem
5
is obtained from a quantum moment map
\displaystyle\mu\colon U_{q}^{lf}(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(\mathfrak{g})
, where
\displaystyle U_{q}^{lf}(\mathfrak{g})\subset U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is the subalgebra of locally finite elements (which is known to be isomorphic to
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)
), and one uses the counit
\displaystyle\varepsilon
as a character of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\rm lf}
. In the case
\displaystyle g=1
,
\displaystyle\mu
is essentially the quantum moment map denoted by
\displaystyle\partial_{\lhd}
in Proposition 1.8.2 of
[
VV10
]
, and for
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}_{m+1}
and arbitrary genus
\displaystyle g
, it coincides with the quantum moment map of
[
Jor14
]
.

For the reader’s convenience, before we prove Theorem
5
we recall the general setup of quantum reduction (§
7.1
), and we describe the quantum moment map
\displaystyle\mu
leading to
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\rm qr}(H)
, providing all arguments we did not find in the litterature (§
7.2
). In particular there is a subtlety in the normalization of
\displaystyle\mu
(see Remark
7.15
).

We thank F. Costantino who explained us the definition of stated skein algebras to appear in
[
CKL
]
, which is used in §
6
, and T.T.Q. Lê for a question which led us to Corollary
6.12
. We are grateful to them and to J. Korinman for discussions during the workshop “Skein algebras in Toulouse” in March 2023, funded by the LabEx CIMI.

M.F. is supported by the ANR LabEx CIMI
within the French State funding “Investissements d’Avenir”. Part of this work was done when M.F. was a postdoc in the University of Hamburg, supported by the DFG under Germany’s Excellence Strategy - EXC 2121 “Quantum Universe” - 390833306.

2.
Preliminaries

The notations and conventions used in this paper agree with those from
[
BR21
]
.

2.1.
Hopf algebras

Let
\displaystyle H
be a Hopf algebra over a field
\displaystyle k
(see e.g.
[
Kas95
, Chap. III]
or
[
CP94
, §4.1]
). We denote by

\Delta:H\to H\otimes H,\qquad\varepsilon:H\to k,\qquad S:H\to H

the coproduct, counit and antipode of
\displaystyle H
. The unit of
\displaystyle H
is denoted by
\displaystyle 1
. We occasionally write
\displaystyle\Delta_{H}
,
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{H}
,
\displaystyle S_{H}
,
\displaystyle 1_{H}
if there is an ambiguity. We assume that the antipode
\displaystyle S
is invertible.

For an element
\displaystyle X\in H^{\otimes n}
we use the notation
\displaystyle\textstyle X=\sum_{(X)}X_{(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes X_{(n)}
as a substitute for
\displaystyle\textstyle X=\sum_{i}X_{(1),i}\otimes\ldots\otimes X_{(n),i}
.

For the coproduct we write
\displaystyle\textstyle\Delta(h)=\sum_{(h)}h_{(1)}\otimes h_{(2)}
(Sweedler’s notation) instead of
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{(\Delta(h))}\Delta(h)_{(1)}\otimes\Delta(h)_{(2)}
. The opposite coproduct, denoted by
\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
, is defined by
\displaystyle\textstyle\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}(h)=\sum_{(h)}h_{(2)}\otimes h_{(1)}
. The co-opposite Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H^{\mathrm{cop}}
is the algebra
\displaystyle H
endowed with the coproduct
\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
, the counit
\displaystyle\varepsilon
and the antipode
\displaystyle S^{-1}
.

In §§
3
,
4
and
5
,
\displaystyle H
is a quasitriangular Hopf algebra as defined e.g. in
[
CP94
, §4.2]
or
[
Kas95
, §VIII.2]
. We write the
\displaystyle R
-matrix of
\displaystyle H
as
\displaystyle\textstyle R=\sum_{(R)}R_{(1)}\otimes R_{(2)}\in H^{\otimes 2}
. In §
6
we moreover assume that
\displaystyle H
is a ribbon Hopf algebra, which means that it contains an element
\displaystyle v
with the properties listed e.g. in
[
Kas95
, §XIV.6]
or
[
CP94
, §4.2.C]
; in particular

(2)

v\text{ is central and }\Delta(v)=(v\otimes v)(R^{\prime}R)^{-1}\ ,

where
\displaystyle R^{\prime}=\textstyle\sum_{(R)}R_{(2)}\otimes R_{(1)}
. Let
\displaystyle\textstyle u=\sum_{(R)}S(R_{(2)})R_{(1)}
be the Drinfeld element, then
\displaystyle g=uv^{-1}
is the pivotal element of
\displaystyle H
. It satisfies
\displaystyle\Delta(g)=g\otimes g
and
\displaystyle S^{2}(h)=ghg^{-1}
for all
\displaystyle h\in H
.

Let
\displaystyle H^{*}=\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(H,k)
be the dual vector space of
\displaystyle H
. For a finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-module
\displaystyle V
and
\displaystyle v\in V
,
\displaystyle f\in V^{*}
, we define
{}_{V}\phi^{f}_{v}\in H^{*}
by
{}_{V}\phi^{f}_{v}(h)=f(h\cdot v)
, where
\displaystyle\cdot
is the action of
\displaystyle H
on
\displaystyle V
. The linear form
{}_{V}\phi^{f}_{v}
is called a
matrix coefficient of
\displaystyle V
. When a basis
\displaystyle(v_{j})
of
\displaystyle V
is given we allow ourselves to write
{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}
instead of
{}_{V}\phi^{v^{i}}_{v_{j}}
for a better readability, where
\displaystyle(v^{i})
is the dual basis. The
restricted dual of
\displaystyle H
, denoted by
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
, is the subspace of
\displaystyle H^{*}
spanned by the matrix coefficients of finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-modules. Let us set

(3)

\begin{array}[]{c}{}_{V}\phi^{f}_{v}\,{{}_{W}}\phi^{l}_{w}={{}_{V\otimes W}\phi^{f\otimes l}_{v\otimes w}},\quad\Delta(_{V}\phi^{i}_{j})=\sum_{k=1}^{\dim(V)}{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{k}}\otimes{{}_{V}\phi^{k}_{j}},\\[13.00005pt]
1_{H^{\circ}}={{}_{k}\phi^{1}_{1}},\quad\varepsilon(_{V}\phi^{i}_{j})=\delta_{i,j},\quad S(_{V}\phi^{i}_{j})={{}_{V^{*}}\phi^{j}_{i}}\end{array}

or in other words

\varphi\psi=(\varphi\otimes\psi)\circ\Delta,\quad\Delta(\varphi)(x\otimes y)=\varphi(xy),\quad 1_{H^{\circ}}=\varepsilon,\quad\varepsilon(\varphi)=\varphi(1),\quad S(\varphi)=\varphi\circ S

for all
\displaystyle\varphi\in H^{\circ}
. Then
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
is a Hopf algebra. The above formula for
\displaystyle\Delta(\varphi)
is not well-defined for a general
\displaystyle\varphi\in H^{*}
, which explains the relevance of
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
. If
\displaystyle H
is finite-dimensional then
\displaystyle H^{\circ}=H^{*}
because
\displaystyle\varphi={{}_{H}\phi^{\varphi}_{1}}
for all
\displaystyle\varphi\in H^{*}
.

Let
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
be a full subcategory of
\displaystyle H
-mod (the category of finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-modules) such that the trivial module
\displaystyle k
is in
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
and if
\displaystyle V,W\in\mathcal{C}
then
\displaystyle V\otimes W\in\mathcal{C}
and
\displaystyle V^{*}\in\mathcal{C}
. Let
\displaystyle H^{\circ}_{\mathcal{C}}
be the subspace of
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
spanned by the matrix coefficients of the objects in
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
. It is clear from (
3
) that
\displaystyle H^{\circ}_{\mathcal{C}}
is a subalgebra of
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
.

2.2.
Filtrations of algebras

Let
\displaystyle(S,\leq)
be an ordered abelian monoid and let
\displaystyle A
be an associative
\displaystyle k
-algebra (
\displaystyle k
is a field). If
\displaystyle F=(F^{s})_{s\in S}
is an algebra filtration of
\displaystyle A
indexed by
\displaystyle S
, we denote by
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{gr}_{F}(A)=\bigoplus_{s\in S}\mathrm{gr}_{F}(A)_{s}
the associated graded algebra which is defined by
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{F}(A)_{s}=F^{s}/F^{<s}
, where
\displaystyle\textstyle F^{<s}=\sum_{r<s}F^{r}
, and endowed with the product

(a+F^{<s})(b+F^{<t})=ab+F^{<(s+t)}

for
\displaystyle a\in F^{s}
and
\displaystyle b\in F^{t}
.

Assume now that
\displaystyle A
has a decomposition
\displaystyle\textstyle A=\bigoplus_{s\in S}X_{s}
as a
\displaystyle k
-vector space, where the family of subspaces
\displaystyle X=(X_{s})_{s\in S}
is such that
\displaystyle\textstyle X_{r}X_{s}\subset\bigoplus_{t\leq r+s}X_{t}
for all
\displaystyle r,s
. Assume moreover that the partial order
\displaystyle\leq
on
\displaystyle S
satisfies

(4)

\forall\,r,s\in S,\quad\exists\,m\in S,\quad r\leq m\text{ and }s\leq m

or in other words that any finite subset of
\displaystyle S
has an upper bound. Define
\displaystyle\textstyle\Sigma^{s}(X)=\bigoplus_{r\leq s}X_{r}
. Then
\displaystyle\Sigma(X)=\bigl(\Sigma^{s}(X)\bigr)_{s\in S}
is a filtration of the algebra
\displaystyle A
. The condition
\displaystyle\textstyle A=\bigcup_{s\in S}\Sigma^{s}(X)
follows from (
4
). Moreover,
\displaystyle\textstyle\Sigma^{<s}(X)=\bigoplus_{r<s}X_{r}
so that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\Sigma(X)}(A)
can be identified with
\displaystyle\textstyle\bigoplus_{s\in S}X_{s}
as graded vector spaces, and under this identification the product
\displaystyle\circ
in
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\Sigma(X)}(A)
is

x\circ y=\pi_{r+s}(xy)

where
\displaystyle x\in X_{r}
,
\displaystyle y\in X_{s}
,
\displaystyle xy
is the product in
\displaystyle A
and
\displaystyle\textstyle\pi_{r+s}:\Sigma^{r+s}(X)\to X_{r+s}
is the canonical projection. All the filtrations used in the subsequent sections are of this form.

Recall that an order relation
\displaystyle\leq
is called
well-founded
if any decreasing chain
\displaystyle s_{1}\geq s_{2}\geq\ldots
is eventually constant. The relevance of filtered algebras in the present paper comes from the following criterion :

Lemma 2.1
.

Assume that the order relation
\displaystyle\leq
on
\displaystyle S
is well-founded and let
\displaystyle F=(F^{s})_{s\in S}
be a filtration of
\displaystyle A
. If
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{F}(A)
is Noetherian, then
\displaystyle A
is Noetherian.

See e.g.
[
VY20
, Lem. 3.130]
for the proof. We note that the converse statement is false in general (see
[
MCR01
, §1.6.9]
for a simple counter-example).

A strategy to prove the Noetherianity of some algebra
\displaystyle A
is then to find a filtration
\displaystyle F
of
\displaystyle A
such that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{F}(A)
is simpler to analyze. For the algebras
\displaystyle A
considered in this paper, we will define
\displaystyle F
in such a way that the following criterion from
[
BG02
, Prop. I.8.17]
applies to
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{F}(A)
:

Lemma 2.2
.

If an associative
\displaystyle k
-algebra is generated by elements
\displaystyle u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}
such that

\forall\,1\leq j<i\leq m,\quad u_{i}u_{j}=q_{ij}u_{j}u_{i}+\sum_{s=1}^{j-1}\sum_{t=1}^{m}\left(\alpha^{ij}_{st}u_{s}u_{t}+\beta^{ij}_{st}u_{t}u_{s}\right)

for certain scalars
\displaystyle q_{ij}\in k^{\times}
,
\displaystyle\alpha^{ij}_{st},\beta^{ij}_{st}\in k
, then it is Noetherian.

Finally, we record two classical transfer results:

Lemma 2.3
.

Assume that the order relation
\displaystyle\leq
on
\displaystyle S
is well-founded and let
\displaystyle F=(F^{s})_{s\in S}
be a filtration of
\displaystyle A
. If
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{F}(A)
is finitely generated, then
\displaystyle A
is finitely generated as well.

Proof.

The easy proof by well-founded induction is left to the reader.
∎

We say that
\displaystyle F=(F^{s})_{s\in S}
is
locally bounded below
if for each nonzero element
\displaystyle a\in A
the set of all
\displaystyle s\in S
such that
\displaystyle a\in F^{s}
has a minimal element. We have (see e.g.
[
MCR01
, §1.6.6]
or
[
VY20
, Lem. 3.130]
):

Lemma 2.4
.

Assume that
\displaystyle(S,\leq)
is totally ordered and
\displaystyle F=(F^{s})_{s\in S}
is a filtration of
\displaystyle A
which is locally bounded below. If
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{F}(A)
has no non-trivial zero divisors, then
\displaystyle A
has no non-trivial zero divisors as well.

2.3.
Semisimple Lie algebras

Let
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
be a finite-dimensional complex simple Lie algebra; one can more generally adapt the notations to handle the case of finite-dimensional complex semisimple Lie algebras, by replacing
\displaystyle D
below with the lowest common multiple of the corresponding integers for the simple components of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
. Here we fix the notations regarding
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
.

We denote by
\displaystyle m
the rank of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
and its Cartan matrix by
\displaystyle(a_{ij})
. We fix a Cartan subalgebra
\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
and a basis of simple roots
\displaystyle\alpha_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}_{\pm}
be the associated Borel subalgebras. We denote by
\displaystyle N
the number of positive roots of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
.

Let
\displaystyle d_{1},\ldots,d_{m}
be the unique coprime integers such that the matrix
\displaystyle(d_{i}a_{ij})
is symmetric. There is an inner product
\displaystyle(-,-)
on
\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}
defined by
\displaystyle(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})=d_{i}a_{ij}
on the simple roots. The simple coroots are
\displaystyle\alpha_{i}^{\vee}=d_{i}^{-1}\alpha_{i}
for every
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq m
, so that
\displaystyle(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}^{\vee})=2
.

The root lattice is
\displaystyle\textstyle Q=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}\subset\mathfrak{h}^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}
. The weight lattice
\displaystyle P
is the
\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}
-lattice formed by all the
\displaystyle\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying
\displaystyle(\lambda,\alpha_{i}^{\vee})\in\mathbb{Z}
for every
\displaystyle i
. The fundamental weights
\displaystyle\varpi_{i}
,
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq m
, are defined by
\displaystyle(\varpi_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee})=\delta_{ij}
for all
\displaystyle j
. Then
\displaystyle\textstyle P=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}\varpi_{i}
. We have
\displaystyle Q\subset P
and we denote by
\displaystyle D
the smallest positive integer such that
\displaystyle DP\subset Q
. Note that
\displaystyle D
is also the corank of
\displaystyle Q
in
\displaystyle P
.

The cone of dominant integral weights is
\displaystyle\textstyle P_{+}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{N}\varpi_{i}
. We also put
\displaystyle\textstyle Q_{+}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{N}\alpha_{i}
. Note that
\displaystyle Q_{+}\not\subset P_{+}
. But
\displaystyle P_{+}\subset D^{-1}Q_{+}
, which is due to the classical fact that the inverse of the Cartan matrix has coefficients in
\displaystyle D^{-1}\mathbb{N}
.

The standard partial order
\displaystyle\leq
on
\displaystyle P
is defined by
\displaystyle\lambda\leq\mu
if and only if
\displaystyle\mu-\lambda\in Q_{+}
. We will need another partial order
\displaystyle\preceq
on
\displaystyle P
, defined by
\displaystyle\lambda\preceq\mu
if and only if
\displaystyle\mu-\lambda\in D^{-1}Q_{+}
. Note that
\displaystyle\lambda\leq\mu
implies
\displaystyle\lambda\preceq\mu
. The advantage of
\displaystyle\preceq
is that it satisfies the condition (
4
), contrarily to
\displaystyle\leq
.

2.4.
Quantum group
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})

Let
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
be a semisimple finite-dimensional Lie algebra of rank
\displaystyle m
and let
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
be the field of fractions of
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[q]
, where
\displaystyle q
is an indeterminate. The
simply connected quantum group

\displaystyle U_{q}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is the
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
-algebra generated by
\displaystyle E_{i},F_{i},L_{i}^{\pm 1}
for
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq m
modulo the relations

\displaystyle\displaystyle L_{i}^{\pm 1}L_{i}^{\mp 1}=1,\qquad L_{i}L_{j}=L_{j}L_{i},\qquad L_{i}E_{j}=q_{i}^{\delta_{i,j}}E_{j}L_{i},\qquad L_{i}F_{j}=q_{i}^{-\delta_{i,j}}F_{j}L_{i},

\displaystyle\displaystyle E_{i}F_{j}-F_{j}E_{i}=\delta_{i,j}\frac{K_{i}-K_{i}^{-1}}{q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle q\text{-Serre relations (see e.g. \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{VY}{}{}, Def. 3.13]}})

where
\displaystyle q_{i}=q^{d_{i}}
and for
\displaystyle\textstyle\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{m}n_{i}\varpi_{i}\in P
we set
\displaystyle\textstyle K_{\mu}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}L_{i}^{n_{i}}
and
\displaystyle\textstyle K_{i}=K_{\alpha_{i}}=\prod_{j=1}^{m}L_{j}^{a_{ji}}
. The Hopf algebra structure on
\displaystyle U_{q}
is given by

\begin{array}[]{lll}\Delta(E_{i})=E_{i}\otimes K_{i}+1\otimes E_{i},&\Delta(F_{i})=F_{i}\otimes 1+K_{i}^{-1}\otimes F_{i},&\Delta(L_{i})=L_{i}\otimes L_{i},\\
S(E_{i})=-E_{i}K_{i}^{-1},&S(F_{i})=-K_{i}F_{i},&S(L_{i})=L_{i}^{-1},\\
\varepsilon(E_{i})=0,&\varepsilon(F_{i})=0,&\varepsilon(L_{i})=1.\end{array}

We denote by
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})
,
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})
and
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})
the subalgebras of
\displaystyle U_{q}
generated respectively by
\displaystyle(K_{\mu})_{\mu\in P}
,
\displaystyle(E_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m}
and
\displaystyle(F_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m}
.

The
adjoint quantum group

\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
is the Hopf subalgebra of
\displaystyle U_{q}
generated by the elements
\displaystyle E_{i},F_{i},K_{i}
. In particular
\displaystyle K_{\alpha}\in U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
for all
\displaystyle\alpha\in Q
.

Fix a reduced expression
\displaystyle s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{N}}
of the longest element
\displaystyle w_{0}
of the Weyl group of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
, where as usual
\displaystyle s_{i}:\mathfrak{h}^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathfrak{h}^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\displaystyle\alpha_{j}\mapsto\alpha_{j}-a_{ij}\alpha_{i}
. This expression induces a total ordering of the positive roots:

\beta_{1}=\alpha_{i_{1}},\quad\beta_{k}=s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{k-1}}(\alpha_{i_{k}})

for all
\displaystyle 2\leq k\leq N
. The
root vectors
of
\displaystyle U_{q}
associated to this ordering are

E_{\beta_{1}}=E_{i_{1}},\>E_{\beta_{k}}=T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k-1}}(E_{i_{k}}),\qquad F_{\beta_{1}}=F_{i_{1}},\>F_{\beta_{k}}=T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k-1}}(F_{i_{k}})

for all
\displaystyle 2\leq k\leq N
, where the
\displaystyle T_{i}
are Lusztig’s algebra automorphisms whose defining formulas can be found e.g. in
[
VY20
, Th. 3.58]
. We record that for any
\displaystyle\mu\in P
,

(5)

K_{\mu}E_{\beta_{j}}=q^{(\mu,\beta_{j})}E_{\beta_{j}}K_{\mu},\qquad K_{\mu}F_{\beta_{j}}=q^{-(\mu,\beta_{j})}F_{\beta_{j}}K_{\mu}.

For
\displaystyle\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{N})\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
,
\displaystyle\mu\in P
and
\displaystyle\mathbf{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{N})\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
consider the monomial

(6)

X(\mathbf{t},\mu,\mathbf{s})=F_{\beta_{N}}^{t_{N}}\ldots F_{\beta_{1}}^{t_{1}}K_{\mu}E_{\beta_{N}}^{s_{N}}\ldots E_{\beta_{1}}^{s_{1}}.

It is a theorem that these monomials form a basis of
\displaystyle U_{q}
, called the
Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt basis
(PBW basis). In particular the elements
\displaystyle X(\mathbf{0},0,\mathbf{s})
form a basis of
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})
(where
\displaystyle\mathbf{0}=(0,\ldots,0)
), the elements
\displaystyle X(\mathbf{t},0,\mathbf{0})
form a basis of
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})
and the elements
\displaystyle K_{\mu}=X(\mathbf{0},\mu,\mathbf{0})
form a basis of
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})
.

Recall that the
height
of an element in
\displaystyle Q
is
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{ht}\left(\sum_{i}k_{i}\alpha_{i}\right)=\sum_{i}k_{i}
, and let

\mathrm{ht}\bigl(X(\mathbf{t},\mu,\mathbf{s})\bigr)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(t_{i}+s_{i})\mathrm{ht}(\beta_{i}).

Then the
degree
of
\displaystyle X(\mathbf{t},\mu,\mathbf{s})
is defined as

d\bigl(X(\mathbf{t},\mu,\mathbf{s})\bigr)=\bigl(s_{N},\ldots,s_{1},t_{N},\ldots,t_{1},\mathrm{ht}\bigl(X(\mathbf{t},\mu,\mathbf{s})\bigr)\bigr)\in\mathbb{N}^{2N+1}.

The set
\displaystyle\mathbb{N}^{2N+1}
is a totally ordered additive monoid for the lexicographic order given by

(7)

(1,0,\ldots,0)<(0,1,0,\ldots,0)<\ldots<(0,\ldots,0,1,0)<(0,\ldots,0,1).

For
\displaystyle\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{2N+1}
, denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{\mathbf{m}}
the subspace of
\displaystyle U_{q}
spanned by the monomials
\displaystyle X(\mathbf{t},\mu,\mathbf{s})
of degree
\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{m}
. It was shown in
[
DCK90
, Prop 1.7]
that
\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{\mathbf{m}})_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{2N+1}}
is an algebra filtration of
\displaystyle U_{q}
and that the graded algebra
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}}(U_{q})
is quasi-polynomial, generated by the cosets

\overline{E_{\beta_{i}}}=E_{\beta_{i}}+\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{<d(E_{\beta_{i}})},\quad\overline{F_{\beta_{i}}}=F_{\beta_{i}}+\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{<d(F_{\beta_{i}})},\quad\overline{K_{\nu}}=K_{\nu}+\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{<d(K_{\nu})}

for
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq N
and
\displaystyle\nu\in P
(note that
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{<d(K_{\mu})}=0
since
\displaystyle d(K_{\mu})=\mathbf{0}
), modulo the relations

(8)

\begin{array}[]{lll}\overline{E_{\beta_{i}}}\,\overline{E_{\beta_{j}}}=q^{(\beta_{i},\beta_{j})}\overline{E_{\beta_{j}}}\,\overline{E_{\beta_{i}}},&\overline{F_{\beta_{i}}}\,\overline{F_{\beta_{j}}}=q^{(\beta_{i},\beta_{j})}\overline{F_{\beta_{j}}}\,\overline{F_{\beta_{i}}},&\overline{E_{\beta_{i}}}\,\overline{F_{\beta_{j}}}=\overline{F_{\beta_{j}}}\,\overline{E_{\beta_{i}}},\\[5.0pt]
\overline{K_{\mu}}\,\overline{E_{\beta_{j}}}=q^{(\mu,\beta_{j})}\overline{E_{\beta_{j}}}\,\overline{K_{\mu}},&\overline{K_{\mu}}\,\overline{F_{\beta_{j}}}=q^{-(\mu,\beta_{j})}\overline{F_{\beta_{j}}}\,\overline{K_{\mu}},&\overline{K_{\mu}}\,\overline{K_{\nu}}=\overline{K_{\mu+\nu}}.\end{array}

The following facts describe the effect of the coproduct
\displaystyle\Delta
on the filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}
; this will be useful later.

Proposition 2.5
.

We have

\Delta(E_{\beta_{j}})=E_{\beta_{j}}\otimes K_{\beta_{j}}+\sum_{k}c_{k}X(\mathbf{0},0,\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{k})\otimes X(\mathbf{0},\mu_{k},\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}_{k})+1\otimes E_{\beta_{j}}

with
\displaystyle\mathrm{ht}\bigl(X(\mathbf{0},0,\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{k})\bigr)<\mathrm{ht}(\beta_{j})
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{ht}\bigl(X(\mathbf{0},\mu_{k},\mathbf{s}_{k}^{\prime\prime})\bigr)<\mathrm{ht}(\beta_{j})
for all
\displaystyle k
, and
\displaystyle c_{k}\in\mathbb{C}(q)
. Similarly

\Delta(F_{\beta_{j}})=F_{\beta_{j}}\otimes 1+\sum_{k}d_{k}X(\mathbf{t}^{\prime}_{k},\nu_{k},\mathbf{0})\otimes X(\mathbf{t}_{k}^{\prime\prime},0,\mathbf{0})+K_{-\beta_{j}}\otimes F_{\beta_{j}}

with
\displaystyle\mathrm{ht}\bigl(X(\mathbf{t}^{\prime}_{k},\nu_{k},\mathbf{0})\bigr)<\mathrm{ht}(\beta_{j})
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{ht}\bigl(X(\mathbf{t}_{k}^{\prime\prime},0,\mathbf{0})\bigr)<\mathrm{ht}(\beta_{j})
for all
\displaystyle k
, and
\displaystyle d_{k}\in\mathbb{C}(q)
.

Proof.

Let

U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})_{\lambda}=\bigl\{x\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})\,\big|\,\forall\,\nu\in P,\>K_{\nu}xK_{\nu}^{-1}=q^{(\nu,\lambda)}x\bigr\}

which is the space of vectors in
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})
with weight
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
for the adjoint action of
\displaystyle U_{q}
. Due to (
5
) we have
\displaystyle E_{\beta_{j}}\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})_{\beta_{j}}
. By
[
Jan96
, Lem 4.12]
(see also
[
Krä03
, Prop. 1]
for the exact statement used here) there exist elements
\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{i}\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})_{\gamma_{i}}
and
\displaystyle x^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})_{\beta_{j}-\gamma_{i}}
with
\displaystyle\gamma_{i}\in Q
satisfying
\displaystyle 0<\gamma_{i}<\beta_{j}
such that

\Delta(E_{\beta_{j}})=E_{\beta_{j}}\otimes K_{\beta_{j}}+\sum_{i}x^{\prime}_{i}\otimes x^{\prime\prime}_{i}K_{\gamma_{i}}+1\otimes E_{\beta_{j}}.

Since the monomials
\displaystyle X(\mathbf{0},0,\mathbf{s})
form a basis of
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})
we can rewrite this as

\Delta(E_{\beta_{j}})=E_{\beta_{j}}\otimes K_{\beta_{j}}+\sum_{k}c_{k}X(\mathbf{0},0,\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{k})\otimes X(\mathbf{0},\mu_{k},\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}_{k})+1\otimes E_{\beta_{j}}

where for all
\displaystyle k
:
\displaystyle c_{k}\in\mathbb{C}(q),

\displaystyle X(\mathbf{0},0,\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{k})\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})_{\mu_{k}}
and
\displaystyle X(\mathbf{0},\mu_{k},\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}_{k})\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})_{\beta_{j}-\mu_{k}}
for some
\displaystyle\mu_{k}\in Q
such that
\displaystyle 0<\mu_{k}<\beta_{j}
. Write
\displaystyle\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{k}=(s^{\prime}_{k,1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{k,N})
and note by (
5
) that

K_{\nu}X(\mathbf{0},0,\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{k})K_{\nu}^{-1}=q^{(\nu,s^{\prime}_{k,1}\beta_{1}+\ldots+s^{\prime}_{k,N}\beta_{N})}X(\mathbf{0},0,\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{k})

for all
\displaystyle\nu\in P
. Hence
\displaystyle s^{\prime}_{k,1}\beta_{1}+\ldots+s^{\prime}_{k,N}\beta_{N}=\mu_{k}
and we get

\mathrm{ht}\bigl(X(\mathbf{0},0,\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{k})\bigr)=\mathrm{ht}(s^{\prime}_{k,1}\beta_{1}+\ldots+s^{\prime}_{k,N}\beta_{N})=\mathrm{ht}(\mu_{k})<\mathrm{ht}(\mu_{k})+\mathrm{ht}(\beta_{j}-\mu_{k})=\mathrm{ht}(\beta_{j}).

We obtain similarly that
\displaystyle\mathrm{ht}\bigl(X(\mathbf{0},\mu_{k},\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}_{k})\bigr)<\mathrm{ht}(\beta_{j})
. The proof for
\displaystyle F_{\beta_{j}}
is completely analogous.
∎

Corollary 2.6
.

For all
\displaystyle\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{2N+1}
we have
\displaystyle\Delta(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{\mathbf{m}})\subset\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{\mathbf{m}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{\mathbf{m}}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.5
and by definition of the degree
\displaystyle d
and of the order
\displaystyle<
on
\displaystyle\mathbb{N}^{2N+1}
we have
\displaystyle\Delta(E_{\beta_{j}})\in\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{d(E_{\beta_{j}})}\right)^{\otimes 2}
and
\displaystyle\Delta(F_{\beta_{j}})\in\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{d(F_{\beta_{j}})}\right)^{\otimes 2}
. Hence

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Delta\bigl(X(\mathbf{t},\mu,\mathbf{s})\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\Delta(F_{\beta_{N}})^{t_{N}}\ldots\Delta(F_{\beta_{1}})^{t_{1}}\Delta(K_{\mu})\Delta(E_{\beta_{N}})^{s_{N}}\ldots\Delta(E_{\beta_{1}})^{s_{1}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\in\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{\sum_{j=1}^{N}t_{j}d(F_{\beta_{j}})+s_{j}d(E_{\beta_{j}})}\right)^{\otimes 2}=\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{d(X(\mathbf{t},\mu,\mathbf{s}))}\right)^{\otimes 2}

and the result follows.
∎

2.5.
Categorical completion
\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{q}
and
\displaystyle R
-matrix

The quantum group
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
is not quasitriangular in the usual sense. We quickly recall from
[
BR22
]
how to overcome this issue.

Let
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
be the full subcategory of type
\displaystyle 1
finite-dimensional modules in
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
-mod (see e.g.
[
CP94
, §10.1.A]
or
[
BG02
, §I.6.12]
). Note that
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
is equivalent to the full subcategory of type
\displaystyle 1
finite-dimensional
\displaystyle U_{q}
-modules.

The
categorical completion of
\displaystyle U^{\mathrm{ad}}_{q}
is the subalgebra
\displaystyle\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{ad}}_{q}
of
\displaystyle\textstyle\prod_{V\in\mathcal{C}}\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q)}(V)
consisting of the sequences
\displaystyle(a_{V})_{V\in\mathcal{C}}
which satisfy
\displaystyle f\circ a_{V}=a_{V^{\prime}}\circ f
for any
\displaystyle f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V,V^{\prime})
,
i.e.
natural transformations
\displaystyle a:\mathcal{U}\Rightarrow\mathcal{U}
where
\displaystyle\mathcal{U}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}(q)}
is the forgetful functor. It admits a “generalized” Hopf algebra structure such that the map

(9)

\iota:U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}\to\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{ad}}_{q},\quad h\mapsto(h_{V})_{V\in\mathcal{C}}

is a morphism of Hopf algebras, where
\displaystyle h_{V}
is the representation of
\displaystyle h
on
\displaystyle V
. The morphism
\displaystyle\iota
is known to be injective and thus
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
can be seen as a Hopf subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{ad}}_{q}
. If moreover we let
\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{q}=\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
be the extension of scalars to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
, then
\displaystyle\iota
can be extended to an embedding of the simply connected quantum group:

\iota:U_{q}\to\mathbb{U}_{q}.

There is an analogous notion of categorical completion of
\displaystyle U_{q}\otimes U_{q}
, denoted by
\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{q}\otimes\mathbb{U}_{q}
, see
[
BR22
, §2, §3.2]
. The
\displaystyle R
-matrix lives in
\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{q}\otimes\mathbb{U}_{q}
; this means that it is a family of
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
-linear maps

(10)

R=\bigl(R_{V,W}:V\otimes W\to V\otimes W\bigr)_{V,W\in\mathcal{C}}

which is natural (
i.e.
it commutes with the
\displaystyle U_{q}
-morphisms of the form
\displaystyle f\otimes g
). The definition of
\displaystyle R
is derived from the
\displaystyle R
-matrix of
\displaystyle U_{h}(\mathfrak{g})

[
CP94
, Th. 8.3.9]
: we have
\displaystyle R=\Theta\hat{R}
, where

•

for all
\displaystyle V,W\in\mathcal{C}
, if
\displaystyle v\in V
and
\displaystyle w\in W
are weight vectors of weights
\displaystyle\mu
and
\displaystyle\nu
we put

(11)

\Theta(v\otimes w)=q^{(\mu,\nu)}v\otimes w.

Since any module in
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
has a basis of weight vectors, this defines a linear map
\displaystyle\Theta_{V,W}:V\otimes W\to V\otimes W
and
\displaystyle\Theta=(\Theta_{V,W})_{V,W\in\mathcal{C}}
is an element of
\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\otimes 2}
.

•

\displaystyle\hat{R}
is written formally as
\displaystyle\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}z_{i,n}E_{\beta_{i}}^{n}\otimes F_{\beta_{i}}^{n}
, where the
\displaystyle z_{i,n}
are coefficients in
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
such that
\displaystyle z_{i,0}=1
for all
\displaystyle i
. Since the actions of
\displaystyle E_{\beta_{i}}
and
\displaystyle F_{\beta_{i}}
are nilpotent on any finite-dimensional module, the action of these infinite sums on
\displaystyle V\otimes W
gives a well-defined linear map
\displaystyle\hat{R}_{V,W}:V\otimes W\to V\otimes W
for all
\displaystyle V,W\in\mathcal{C}
and
\displaystyle\hat{R}=(\hat{R}_{V,W})_{V,W\in\mathcal{C}}
is an element of
\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\otimes 2}
.

Since
\displaystyle\Theta_{V,W}\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q)}(V\otimes W)=\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q)}(V)\otimes\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}(q)}(W)
, we can write
\displaystyle\textstyle\Theta_{V,W}=\sum_{i}\Theta^{V}_{(1),i}\otimes\Theta^{W}_{(2),i}
. From this observation we allow ourselves to use the notation
\displaystyle\Theta=\Theta_{(1)}\otimes\Theta_{(2)}
, which will be very convenient in later computations. Let
\displaystyle v
be a vector of weight
\displaystyle\mu\in P
in some
\displaystyle V\in\mathcal{C}
; then we have

(12)

\Theta_{(1)}v\otimes\Theta_{(2)}=v\otimes K_{\mu},\qquad\Theta_{(1)}\otimes\Theta_{(2)}v=K_{\mu}\otimes v.

Indeed,
\displaystyle\Theta_{V,-}=\bigl(\Theta_{V,W}:V\otimes W\to V\otimes W\bigr)_{W\in\mathcal{C}}
is a family of linear maps indexed by
\displaystyle W
, so by definition
\displaystyle\Theta_{V,-}\in\mathrm{End}(V)\otimes\mathbb{U}_{q}
. Thus
\displaystyle\Theta_{(1)}v\otimes\Theta_{(2)}=\Theta_{V,-}(v\otimes?)\in V\otimes\mathbb{U}_{q}
. If
\displaystyle w\in W
is a vector of weight
\displaystyle\nu
we have
\displaystyle\Theta_{V,W}(v\otimes w)=q^{(\mu,\nu)}v\otimes w=v\otimes K_{\mu}w
, which means that
\displaystyle\Theta_{V,-}(v\otimes?)=v\otimes K_{\mu}
as desired. We also note that

(S\otimes\mathrm{id})(\Theta)=(\mathrm{id}\otimes S)(\Theta)=\Theta^{-1}

and we allow ourselves to write these elements as
\displaystyle S(\Theta_{(1)})\otimes\Theta_{(2)}
and
\displaystyle\Theta_{(1)}\otimes S(\Theta_{(2)})
. Finally it is obvious that

(13)

K_{\nu}\Theta_{(1)}\otimes\Theta_{(2)}=\Theta_{(1)}K_{\nu}\otimes\Theta_{(2)},\qquad\Theta_{(1)}\otimes K_{\nu}\Theta_{(2)}=\Theta_{(1)}\otimes\Theta_{(2)}K_{\nu}

for all
\displaystyle\nu\in P
.

Using the PBW basis (
6
) to rewrite
\displaystyle\hat{R}
, we get

(14)

R=\Theta_{(1)}\otimes\Theta_{(2)}+\sum_{k\geq 1}z_{k}\,\Theta_{(1)}X(\mathbf{0},0,\mathbf{s}_{k})\otimes\Theta_{(2)}X(\mathbf{s}_{k},0,\mathbf{0})

where
\displaystyle z_{k}\in\mathbb{C}(q)
and for each
\displaystyle k\geq 1
we have
\displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{k}\neq\mathbf{0}
. This expression of
\displaystyle R
will be abridged as a formal sum

R=\sum_{(R)}R_{(1)}\otimes R_{(2)}.

Let
\displaystyle R_{(1)}\otimes R_{(2)}
be one of the summands in (
14
); we see from (
5
) and (
13
) that

(15)

K_{\nu}R_{(1)}\otimes R_{(2)}=q^{(\nu,\gamma)}R_{(1)}K_{\nu}\otimes R_{(2)}\qquad R_{(1)}\otimes K_{\nu}R_{(2)}=q^{-(\nu,\gamma)}R_{(1)}\otimes R_{(2)}K_{\nu}

for some
\displaystyle\gamma\in Q_{+}
. In particular
\displaystyle\gamma=0
if and only if
\displaystyle R_{(1)}\otimes R_{(2)}
is the first summand of
\displaystyle R
, namely
\displaystyle\Theta_{(1)}\otimes\Theta_{(2)}
.

2.6.
Quantized coordinate algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)

Recall that
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is the full subcategory of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
-mod whose objects are the (finite-dimensional) modules of type
\displaystyle 1
. The subcategory
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
contains the trivial module and is stable by tensor product and dual.

Let
\displaystyle G
be the connected, complex, semisimple algebraic group
\displaystyle G
with Lie algebra
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
. The
quantized coordinate algebra

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}=\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)
is the subspace of
\displaystyle(U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}})^{\circ}
spanned over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
by the family of all matrix coefficients of objects in
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}

[
BG02
, Chap. I.7]
. Note that
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}=(U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}})^{\circ}_{\mathcal{C}}
in the notations of §
2.1
and hence
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
is a Hopf algebra. We denote by
\displaystyle\star
the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
. Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
is semisimple,
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
is spanned over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
by the matrix coefficients of the irreducible finite-dimensional
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
-modules of type
\displaystyle 1
.

Let
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})=\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
be the extension of scalars to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
, where
\displaystyle D\in\mathbb{N}
is defined in §
2.3
. There is a non-degenerate pairing

(16)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})\otimes\mathbb{U}_{q}\to\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D}),\quad\bigl\langle{{}_{V}\phi^{w}_{v}},(a_{X})_{X\in\mathcal{C}}\bigr\rangle=w(a_{V}v)

which extends the evaluation pairing
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}\to\mathbb{C}(q)
.

For each
\displaystyle\mu\in P_{+}
let
\displaystyle V_{\mu}
be the irreducible
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
-module of type
\displaystyle 1
with highest weight
\displaystyle\mu
.
We denote by
\displaystyle C(\mu)
the subspace of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
spanned by the matrix coefficients of
\displaystyle V_{\mu}
. We have
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathcal{O}_{q}=\bigoplus_{\mu\in P_{+}}C(\mu)
. From the decomposition

V_{\mu}\otimes V_{\nu}=V_{\mu+\nu}\oplus\bigoplus_{\lambda<\mu+\nu}N_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}V_{\lambda}

where the
\displaystyle N_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}\in\mathbb{N}
are multiplicities and
\displaystyle\lambda\leq\kappa
means that
\displaystyle\kappa-\lambda\in Q_{+}
, we deduce that

(17)

C(\mu)\star C(\nu)=C(\mu+\nu)\oplus\bigoplus_{\lambda<\mu+\nu}\delta_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}\,C(\lambda)

where
\displaystyle\delta_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}
is
\displaystyle 0
or
\displaystyle 1
, depending if
\displaystyle N_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}=0
or
\displaystyle N_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}>0
.

3.
The handle algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}

Let
\displaystyle H
be a quasitriangular Hopf algebra with an invertible antipode. In the first papers on combinatorial quantization like
[
Ale94
,
AGS95
,
BR95
]
, the
handle algebra

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
was defined by matrix relations which describe the commutation relations in the algebra. Here we give a more intrinsic definition, as a twist of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)^{\otimes 2}
. The seminal definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
based on matrix relations will be recovered in Proposition
3.4
.

3.1.
Definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)

Recall that an invertible element
\displaystyle J\in H^{\otimes 2}
satisfying

\begin{array}[]{l}(J\otimes 1)\,(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})(J)=(1\otimes J)\,(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)(J),\\[1.99997pt]
(\varepsilon\otimes\mathrm{id})(J)=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varepsilon)(J)=1.\end{array}

is called a
twist
for
\displaystyle H
. We can define a new Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H_{J}
, called the
twist of
\displaystyle H
by
\displaystyle J
, which is
\displaystyle H
as an algebra but whose coproduct, counit and antipode are (for
\displaystyle h\in H
)

\Delta_{H_{J}}(h)=J\Delta_{H}(h)J^{-1},\quad\varepsilon_{H_{J}}(h)=\varepsilon_{H}(h),\quad S_{H_{J}}(h)=uS_{H}(h)u^{-1}

with
\displaystyle\textstyle u=\sum_{(J)}J_{(1)}S(J_{(2)})
if we write
\displaystyle\textstyle J=\sum_{(J)}J_{(1)}\otimes J_{(2)}
. If
\displaystyle(A,m_{A},\cdot)
is a right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra we can define a right
\displaystyle H_{J}
-module-algebra
\displaystyle A_{J}
, called the
twist of
\displaystyle A
by
\displaystyle J
, which is
\displaystyle A
as a vector space and whose product is

(18)

m_{A_{J}}(x\otimes y)=\sum_{(J)}m_{A}\bigl(x\cdot J_{(1)}\otimes y\cdot J_{(2)}\bigr)

(where
\displaystyle m_{A}
is the product in
\displaystyle A
). The right action
\displaystyle\cdot:A_{J}\otimes H_{J}\to A_{J}
is equal to the original action
\displaystyle\cdot:A\otimes H\to A
.

Following the conventions of
[
BR22
,
BR21
]
recalled in §
2.1
, we write the
\displaystyle R
-matrix of
\displaystyle H
as

R=\sum_{(R)}R_{(1)}\otimes R_{(2)}\in H^{\otimes 2}.

We first recall from
[
BR22
, §4]
the construction of the
loop algebra

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
as a twist of the restricted dual
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
(see §
2.1
for the definition of
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
). Denote by
\displaystyle\rhd
and
\displaystyle\lhd
the left and right coregular actions of
\displaystyle H
on
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
respectively:

(19)

x\rhd\varphi=\sum_{(\varphi)}\varphi_{(1)}\langle\varphi_{(2)},x\rangle,\quad\varphi\lhd x=\sum_{(\varphi)}\langle\varphi_{(1)},x\rangle\varphi_{(2)}

where
\displaystyle x\in H
,
\displaystyle\varphi\in H^{\circ}
and we use Sweedler’s notation
\displaystyle\textstyle\Delta_{H^{\circ}}(\varphi)=\sum_{(\varphi)}\varphi_{(1)}\otimes\varphi_{(2)}
. Let
\displaystyle H\otimes H^{\mathrm{cop}}
be endowed with the usual Hopf algebra structure on tensor products. Introduce

(20)

F=R_{32}R_{42}=\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2})}(1\otimes R_{(2)}^{1}R_{(2)}^{2})\otimes(R_{(1)}^{1}\otimes R_{(1)}^{2})\in(H\otimes H^{\mathrm{cop}})^{\otimes 2}.

Here we write
\displaystyle\textstyle R_{32}=\sum_{(R)}1\otimes R_{(2)}\otimes R_{(1)}\otimes 1
and
\displaystyle\textstyle R_{42}=\sum_{(R)}1\otimes R_{(2)}\otimes 1\otimes R_{(1)}
, which are embeddings of
\displaystyle R\in H^{\otimes 2}
into
\displaystyle(H\otimes H^{\mathrm{cop}})^{\otimes 2}
, while
\displaystyle R^{1}
and
\displaystyle R^{2}
denote two copies of
\displaystyle R
. It is straightforward to check that the element
\displaystyle F
is a twist for
\displaystyle H\otimes H^{\mathrm{cop}}
, so we can consider the twisted Hopf algebra
\displaystyle(H\otimes H^{\mathrm{cop}})_{F}
, which we denote by
\displaystyle A_{0,1}(H)
.

Note that
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
is a right
\displaystyle(H\otimes H^{\mathrm{cop}})
-module-algebra for the action

(21)

\varphi\cdot(x\otimes y)=S(y)\rhd\varphi\lhd x\qquad(\text{with }\varphi\in H^{\circ},\,x\in H,\,y\in H^{\mathrm{cop}}).

Then
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
is defined to be the twist
\displaystyle(H^{\circ})_{F}
. Explicitly, it is the vector space
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
with the product

(22)

\varphi\psi=\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2})}\bigl(R_{(2)}^{2}S(R_{(2)}^{1})\rhd\varphi\bigr)\star\bigl(R_{(1)}^{2}\rhd\psi\lhd R_{(1)}^{1}\bigr)

where
\displaystyle\star
is the usual product on
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
, given by
\displaystyle\eta\star\gamma=(\eta\otimes\gamma)\circ\Delta_{H}
. The formula (
22
) is derived from (
20
) using the general definition of the twisted product in (
18
) together with the fact that
\displaystyle S
is an antimorphism of algebras and that
\displaystyle(S\otimes S)(R)=R
.

By construction,
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
is a right
\displaystyle A_{0,1}(H)
-module-algebra for the action (
21
). The coproduct
\displaystyle\Delta_{H}
, viewed as a map
\displaystyle H\to A_{0,1}(H)
, is a morphism of Hopf algebras. It follows that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
is a right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra for the right coadjoint action, given by

(23)

\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(\varphi)=\sum_{(h)}S(h_{(2)})\rhd\varphi\lhd h_{(1)}.

In order to define
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
, let

\gamma=R_{32}R_{31}R_{42}R_{14}^{-1}=\sum_{(R^{1}),\ldots(R^{4})}\!\!\!\bigl(R_{(2)}^{2}S(R_{(1)}^{4})\otimes R_{(2)}^{1}R_{(2)}^{3}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(R_{(1)}^{1}R_{(1)}^{2}\otimes R_{(1)}^{3}R_{(2)}^{4}\bigr)\in A_{0,1}(H)^{\otimes 2}

where
\displaystyle R^{1},\ldots,R^{4}
are four copies of
\displaystyle R
and we used that
\displaystyle R^{-1}=(S\otimes\mathrm{id})(R)
.

Lemma 3.1
.

The element
\displaystyle\gamma
is a bicharacter of
\displaystyle A_{0,1}(H)
, which means that

(\Delta_{A_{0,1}(H)}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\gamma)=\gamma_{23}\gamma_{13},\quad(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta_{A_{0,1}(H)})(\gamma)=\gamma_{12}\gamma_{13}

where the subscripts denote embeddings of
\displaystyle\gamma\in A_{0,1}(H)^{\otimes 2}
into
\displaystyle A_{0,1}(H)^{\otimes 3}
. It follows that

\Gamma=1\otimes 1\otimes\gamma\otimes 1\otimes 1\in A_{0,1}(H)^{\otimes 2}\otimes A_{0,1}(H)^{\otimes 2}

is a twist for
\displaystyle A_{0,1}(H)^{\otimes 2}
.

Proof.

The two equalities are obtained by straightforward computations using the definition of the coproduct in
\displaystyle D_{F}
and the defining properties of the
\displaystyle R
-matrix given e.g. in
[
CP94
, §4.2]
; this is left to the reader. The last claim is an easy general fact.
∎

It follows that we have the twisted Hopf algebra
\displaystyle\bigl(A_{0,1}(H)^{\otimes 2}\bigr)_{\Gamma}
, which we denote by
\displaystyle A_{1,0}(H)
.

Definition 3.2
.

We define
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
as the twist by
\displaystyle\Gamma
of the right
\displaystyle A_{0,1}(H)^{\otimes 2}
-module-algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)^{\otimes 2}
:

\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)=(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\otimes\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H))_{\Gamma}.

Explicitly,
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
is the vector space
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\otimes\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
with the product

(24)

(\beta\otimes\alpha)(\beta^{\prime}\otimes\alpha^{\prime})=\sum_{(R^{1}),\ldots,(R^{4})}\beta\bigl(R_{(2)}^{4}R_{(1)}^{3}\rhd\beta^{\prime}\lhd R_{(1)}^{1}R_{(1)}^{2}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(R_{(2)}^{3}S(R_{(2)}^{1})\rhd\alpha\lhd R_{(2)}^{2}R_{(1)}^{4}\bigr)\alpha^{\prime}

where we used that
\displaystyle(S\otimes S)(R)=R
. In particular the maps

(25)

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathfrak{i}_{A}:&\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\\
&\alpha&\longmapsto&1\otimes\alpha\end{array},\qquad\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathfrak{i}_{B}:&\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\\
&\beta&\longmapsto&\beta\otimes 1\end{array}

are embeddings of algebras and we have

(26)

\beta\otimes\alpha=\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\beta)\,\mathfrak{i}_{A}(\alpha).

The choice of the subscripts
\displaystyle A
and
\displaystyle B
to denote the embeddings will be explained below.

Lemma 3.3
.

The iterated coproduct
\displaystyle\textstyle\Delta^{(3)}_{H}:h\mapsto\sum_{(h)}h_{(1)}\otimes h_{(2)}\otimes h_{(3)}\otimes h_{(4)}
, viewed as a map
\displaystyle H\to A_{1,0}(H)
, is a morphism of Hopf algebras.

Proof.

Straightforward computations left to the reader.
∎

Since by definition
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
is a right
\displaystyle A_{1,0}(H)
-module-algebra, the lemma implies that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
is a right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra with action

(27)

\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(\beta\otimes\alpha)=\sum_{(h)}\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(1)})(\beta)\otimes\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(2)})(\alpha)

where we use the
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
from (
23
). In particular the maps
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{A},\mathfrak{i}_{B}
in (
25
) are embeddings of
\displaystyle H
-module-algebras.

We now recover the definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
by matrix relations, which is very helpful in certain proofs. To introduce the notations, let us first recall the case of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
, which has been treated in
[
BR22
, Prop. 4.6]
. Let
\displaystyle V
be a finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-module,
\displaystyle(v_{i})
be a basis of
\displaystyle V
and
\displaystyle(v^{i})
be its dual basis. Denote by
{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}
the matrix coefficients in this basis:
{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}(h)=v^{i}(h\cdot v_{j})
for
\displaystyle h\in H
. Let
\displaystyle E_{ij}
be the basis elements of
\displaystyle\mathrm{End}(V)
given by
\displaystyle E_{ij}(v_{k})=\delta_{jk}v_{i}
. We define

(28)

\overset{V}{M}=\sum_{i,j}{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}\otimes E_{ij}\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\otimes\mathrm{End}(V)

which can be seen as a matrix of size
\displaystyle\dim(V)
with coefficients in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
. Note that
\displaystyle\overset{V}{M}
does not depend on the choice of a basis in
\displaystyle V
. By its very definition, the collection of matrices
\displaystyle M
satisfies
naturality
:

(29)

(\mathrm{id}\otimes f)\overset{V}{M}=\overset{W}{M}(\mathrm{id}\otimes f)

for any
\displaystyle H
-linear morphism
\displaystyle f:V\to W
.
Consider the embeddings

\begin{array}[]{rcll}j_{1}:&\mathrm{End}(V)&\longrightarrow&\mathrm{End}(V)\otimes\mathrm{End}(W)\\
&X&\longmapsto&X\otimes\mathrm{id}_{W}\end{array},\quad\begin{array}[]{rcll}j_{2}:&\mathrm{End}(W)&\longrightarrow&\mathrm{End}(V)\otimes\mathrm{End}(W)\\
&Y&\longmapsto&\mathrm{id}_{V}\otimes Y\end{array}

where
\displaystyle V
and
\displaystyle W
are any finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-modules. We define

\overset{V}{M}_{1}=(\mathrm{id}\otimes j_{1})\bigl(\overset{V}{M}\bigr),\>\>\overset{W}{M}_{2}=(\mathrm{id}\otimes j_{2})\bigl(\overset{W}{M}\bigr)\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\otimes\mathrm{End}(V)\otimes\mathrm{End}(W).

Note that
\displaystyle\overset{V\otimes W}{M}
can be viewed as an element of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\otimes\mathrm{End}(V)\otimes\mathrm{End}(W)
since
\displaystyle\mathrm{End}(V\otimes W)=\mathrm{End}(V)\otimes\mathrm{End}(W)
. Finally
\displaystyle x_{V,W}
denotes the representation of some
\displaystyle x\in H^{\otimes 2}
on
\displaystyle V\otimes W
and we implicitly identify
\displaystyle x_{V,W}
with
\displaystyle 1\otimes x_{V,W}\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\otimes\mathrm{End}(V)\otimes\mathrm{End}(W)
. Then
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
is spanned by the coefficients of the matrices
\displaystyle\overset{V}{M}
for all
\displaystyle V
in
\displaystyle H\text{-}\mathrm{mod}
and (
22
) is equivalent to the set of all
fusion relations
:

(30)

\overset{V\otimes W}{M}=\overset{V}{M}_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{M}_{2}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}^{-1}

where
\displaystyle\textstyle R^{\prime}=\sum_{(R)}R_{(2)}\otimes R_{(1)}
. Thanks to the braiding in
\displaystyle H\text{-}\mathrm{mod}
and naturality (
29
), it is equivalent to

(31)

\overset{V\otimes W}{M}=R_{V,W}^{-1}\,\overset{W}{M}_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{M}_{1}.

From the fusion relation one can check that
\displaystyle\overset{V}{M}
is invertible,
see e.g.
[
Fai20b
, Prop. 3.3]
. Moreover, the equivalence of (
30
) and (
31
) gives the so-called reflection equation:

(32)

R_{V,W}\overset{V}{M}_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{M}_{2}=\overset{W}{M}_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{M}_{1}(R^{\prime})_{V,W}.

For
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
, consider the matrices

\overset{V}{A}=(\mathfrak{i}_{A}\otimes\mathrm{id})\bigl(\overset{V}{M}\bigr),\>\>\overset{V}{B}=(\mathfrak{i}_{B}\otimes\mathrm{id})\bigl(\overset{V}{M}\bigr)\in\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\otimes\mathrm{End}(V).

Since the matrix
\displaystyle\overset{V}{M}
is invertible, so are
\displaystyle\overset{V}{A}
and
\displaystyle\overset{V}{B}
. In mathematical physics,
\displaystyle\overset{V}{A}
and
\displaystyle\overset{V}{B}
are seen as quantum holonomy matrices associated to the usual generators
\displaystyle a,b
of the fundamental group of the torus with an open disk removed.

Proposition 3.4
.

The following relations hold true for any finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-modules
\displaystyle V
,
\displaystyle W
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\overset{V\otimes W}{A}=\overset{V}{A}_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{A}_{2}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}^{-1}\quad\text{(fusion relation)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\overset{V\otimes W}{B}=\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{B}_{2}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}^{-1}\quad\text{(fusion relation)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{A}_{2}=\overset{W}{A}_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}\quad\text{(exchange relation)}

where
\displaystyle\textstyle R^{\prime}=\sum_{(R)}R_{(2)}\otimes R_{(1)}
. These matrix equalities entirely describe the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
.

Proof.

We see from (
24
) and (
26
) that the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
is entirely described by the following formulas:

(33)

\begin{array}[]{rl}\mathfrak{i}_{A}(\varphi)\mathfrak{i}_{A}(\psi)&=\mathfrak{i}_{A}(\varphi\psi),\\
\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\varphi)\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\psi)&=\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\varphi\psi),\\
\mathfrak{i}_{A}(\varphi)\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\psi)&=\sum_{(R^{1}),\ldots,(R^{4})}\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathfrak{i}_{B}\bigl(R_{(2)}^{4}R_{(1)}^{3}\rhd\psi\lhd R_{(1)}^{1}R_{(1)}^{2}\bigr)\mathfrak{i}_{A}\bigl(R_{(2)}^{3}S(R_{(2)}^{1})\rhd\varphi\lhd R_{(2)}^{2}R_{(1)}^{4}\bigr).\end{array}

Assume that
\displaystyle\varphi
,
\displaystyle\psi
are matrix coefficients
\displaystyle{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}
,
\displaystyle{{}_{W}\phi^{k}_{l}}
in some bases of
\displaystyle V
and
\displaystyle W
. We have already recalled above that the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
for matrix coefficients is equivalent to (
30
). Hence the two first equalities in (
33
) for matrix coefficients are equivalent to the fusion relations for the matrices
\displaystyle\overset{V}{A}
and
\displaystyle\overset{V}{B}
. The exchange relation is equivalent to the third equality in (
33
) for matrix coefficients, as we now show. Note that by the very definition of a representation we have

\sum_{i,j}(h\rhd{{}_{V}\phi}^{i}_{j})\otimes E_{ij}=\sum_{i,j}{{}_{V}\phi}^{i}_{j}\otimes(E_{ij}\,h_{V}),\qquad\sum_{i,j}({{}_{V}\phi}^{i}_{j}\lhd h)\otimes E_{ij}=\sum_{i,j}{{}_{V}\phi}^{i}_{j}\otimes(h_{V}E_{ij})

where
\displaystyle h_{V}\in\mathrm{End}(V)
is the representation of
\displaystyle h\in H
on
\displaystyle V
.
Hence

\displaystyle\displaystyle\overset{W}{A}_{2}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}=\sum_{i,j,k,l}\mathfrak{i}_{A}({{}_{W}\phi}^{k}_{l})\,\mathfrak{i}_{B}({{}_{V}\phi}^{i}_{j})\otimes E_{ij}\otimes E_{kl}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j,k,l\\
(R^{1}),\ldots,(R^{4})\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{i}_{B}\bigl(R_{(2)}^{4}R_{(1)}^{3}\rhd{{}_{V}\phi}^{i}_{j}\lhd R_{(1)}^{1}R_{(1)}^{2}\bigr)\,\mathfrak{i}_{A}\bigl(R_{(2)}^{3}S(R_{(2)}^{1})\rhd{{}_{W}\phi}^{k}_{l}\lhd R_{(2)}^{2}R_{(1)}^{4}\bigr)\otimes E_{ij}\otimes E_{kl}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=
