\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j,k,l\\
(R^{1}),\ldots,(R^{4})\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{i}_{B}({{}_{V}\phi}^{i}_{j})\,\mathfrak{i}_{A}({{}_{W}\phi}^{k}_{l})\otimes\bigl(R_{(1)}^{1}\bigr)_{V}\,\bigl(R_{(1)}^{2}\bigr)_{V}\,E_{ij}\,\bigl(R_{(2)}^{4}\bigr)_{V}\,\bigl(R_{(1)}^{3}\bigr)_{V}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\otimes\bigl(R_{(2)}^{2}\bigr)_{W}\,\bigl(R_{(1)}^{4}\bigr)_{W}\,E_{kl}\,\bigl(R_{(2)}^{3}\bigr)_{W}\,S\bigl(R_{(2)}^{1}\bigr)_{W}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),\ldots,(R^{4})}\bigl(R_{(1)}^{1}\bigr)_{V1}\,\bigl(R_{(1)}^{2}\bigr)_{V1}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,\bigl(R_{(2)}^{4}\bigr)_{V1}\,\bigl(R_{(1)}^{3}\bigr)_{V1}\,\bigl(R_{(2)}^{2}\bigr)_{W2}\,\bigl(R_{(1)}^{4}\bigr)_{W2}\,\overset{W}{A}_{2}\,\bigl(R_{(2)}^{3}\bigr)_{W2}\,S\bigl(R_{(2)}^{1}\bigr)_{W2}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R)}\bigl(R_{(1)}\bigr)_{V1}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{A}_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,S\bigl(R_{(2)}\bigr)_{W2}.

Using that
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2})}R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(1)}\otimes S(R^{2}_{(2)})R^{1}_{(2)}=1\otimes 1
, we get

R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{A}_{2}=\sum_{(R)}\bigl(R_{(1)}\bigr)_{V1}\,\overset{W}{A}_{2}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,\bigl(R_{(2)}\bigr)_{W2}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}=\overset{W}{A}_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}

as claimed.

As a result the equalities in (
33
), which determine the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
, are equivalent to the matrix equalities when
\displaystyle\varphi
and
\displaystyle\psi
are matrix coefficients. But the matrix coefficients span
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
as a vector space, so if we know the formulas (
33
) for matrix coefficients we can actually deduce the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
, which proves the last claim.
∎

We will need the following result when discussing the quantum moment maps (see Section
7.2
). Assume now
\displaystyle H
has a ribbon element
\displaystyle v
. For every finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-module
\displaystyle V
define matrices in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\otimes{\rm End}(V)
by:

(34)

\overset{V}{X}=v_{V}\overset{V}{B}\overset{V}{A}{}^{-1}\ ,\quad\overset{V}{Y}=v_{V}\overset{V}{B}{}^{-1}\overset{V}{A}

where
\displaystyle v_{V}\in\mathrm{End}(V)
is the representation of
\displaystyle v
on
\displaystyle V
and is identified with
\displaystyle 1_{\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)}\otimes v_{V}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

The matrices
\displaystyle\overset{V}{X}
,
\displaystyle\overset{V}{Y}
, and
\displaystyle\overset{V}{X}\overset{V}{Y}=v_{V}^{2}\overset{V}{B}\overset{V}{A}{}^{-1}\overset{V}{B}{}^{-1}\overset{V}{A}
satisfy the fusion relation (
30
).

Proof.

Recall first that
\displaystyle v
is central. It follows that
\displaystyle v_{V}
is a
\displaystyle H
-linear endomorphism so by naturality (
29
) we have
\displaystyle v_{V}\overset{V}{M}=\overset{V}{M}v_{V}
. Moreover
\displaystyle(v_{V})_{1}\,x_{V,W}=x_{V,W}\,(v_{V})_{1}
and
\displaystyle(v_{W})_{2}\,x_{V,W}=x_{V,W}\,(v_{W})_{2}
for all
\displaystyle x\in H^{\otimes 2}
. Let us show the claim for
\displaystyle\overset{V}{X}
; explanations are below the computation:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\overset{V\otimes W}{X}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(v_{V})_{1}\,(v_{W})_{2}\,(R^{\prime}R)_{V,W}^{-1}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{B}_{2}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}^{-1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{A}{}^{-1}_{2}(R^{\prime})_{V,W}^{-1}\,\overset{V}{A}{}^{-1}_{1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(v_{V})_{1}\,(v_{W})_{2}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}^{-1}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{B}_{2}\,\overset{W}{A}{}^{-1}_{2}(R^{\prime})_{V,W}^{-1}\,\overset{V}{A}{}^{-1}_{1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(v_{V})_{1}\,(v_{W})_{2}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}\,\overset{W}{B}_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}\,\overset{W}{A}{}^{-1}_{2}(R^{\prime})_{V,W}^{-1}\,\overset{V}{A}{}^{-1}_{1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(v_{V})_{1}\,(v_{W})_{2}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}\,\overset{W}{B}_{2}\,\overset{W}{A}{}^{-1}_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,\overset{V}{A}{}^{-1}_{1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=R_{V,W}^{-1}\,\overset{W}{X}_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{X}_{1}=\overset{V}{X}_{1}\,R^{\prime}_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{X}_{2}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}^{-1}.

For the first equality we used (
2
) and the fusion relations for the matrices
\displaystyle B
and
\displaystyle A
, for the third equality we used a variant of (
32
), for the fourth equality we used the exchange relation in Proposition
3.4
, for the fifth equality we used the remarks made at the begining of the proof and for the sixth equality we used the equivalence of (
31
) and (
30
).

The same arguments apply to
\displaystyle\overset{V}{Y}
. In order to prove the claim for
\displaystyle\overset{V}{X}\overset{V}{Y}
we first show that
\displaystyle\overset{V}{X}
and
\displaystyle\overset{V}{Y}
satisfy the exchange relation

R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{X}_{1}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}\,\overset{W}{Y}_{2}=\overset{W}{Y}_{2}R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{X}_{1}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}.

By the remarks made at the begining of the proof it is equivalent to

R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\overset{V}{A}{}^{-1}_{1}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}\,\overset{W}{B}{}^{-1}_{2}\overset{W}{A}_{2}=\overset{W}{B}{}^{-1}_{2}\overset{W}{A}_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\overset{V}{A}{}^{-1}_{1}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}.

Now, by using again (
32
) and the exchange relation in Proposition
3.4
we get

\displaystyle\displaystyle R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\overset{V}{A}{}^{-1}_{1}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}\,\overset{W}{B}{}^{-1}_{2}\overset{W}{A}_{2}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,R^{\prime}_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{B}{}^{-1}_{2}\,(R^{\prime}_{V,W})^{-1}\,\overset{V}{A}{}^{-1}_{1}\,R^{\prime}_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{A}_{2}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\overset{W}{B}{}^{-1}_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,R^{\prime}_{V,W}\,(R^{\prime}_{V,W})^{-1}\,\overset{V}{A}{}^{-1}_{1}\,R^{\prime}_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{A}_{2}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\overset{W}{B}{}^{-1}_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,R^{\prime}_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{A}_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{A}{}^{-1}_{1}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\overset{W}{B}{}^{-1}_{2}\,\overset{W}{A}_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{A}{}^{-1}_{1}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\overset{W}{B}{}^{-1}_{2}\overset{W}{A}_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{B}_{1}\overset{V}{A}{}^{-1}_{1}\,R_{V,W}^{-1}.

We can now deduce that
\displaystyle\overset{V}{X}\overset{V}{Y}
satisfies the fusion relation:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\overset{V\otimes W}{X}\ \overset{V\otimes W}{Y}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\overset{V}{X}_{1}\,R^{\prime}_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{X}_{2}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}^{-1}\,\overset{V}{Y}_{1}\,R^{\prime}_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{Y}_{2}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}^{-1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\overset{V}{X}_{1}\,R^{\prime}_{V,W}\,(R^{\prime})^{-1}_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{Y}_{1}\,R^{\prime}_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{X}_{2}\,(R^{\prime})^{-1}_{V,W}\,R^{\prime}_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{Y}_{2}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}^{-1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\overset{V}{X}_{1}\,\overset{V}{Y}_{1}\,R^{\prime}_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{X}_{2}\,\overset{W}{Y}_{2}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}^{-1}.\qed

3.2.
Morphism
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\to\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})

Since the left coregular action (
19
) endows
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
with a structure of
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra, we can consider the smash product
\displaystyle H^{\circ}\#H
, which is denoted by
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
and is called the Heisenberg double of
\displaystyle H^{\circ}

[
Mon93
, §4.1.10]
. Explicitly, the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
is the vector space
\displaystyle H^{\circ}\otimes H
with the product

(35)

(\varphi\,\#\,x)(\psi\,\#\,y)=\sum_{(x)}\varphi\star(x_{(1)}\rhd\psi)\,\#\,x_{(2)}y

where we write
\displaystyle\varphi\,\#\,x
for the element
\displaystyle\varphi\otimes x\in H^{\circ}\otimes H
and
\displaystyle\star
is the usual product in
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
.

Consider the following
right
action of
\displaystyle H
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
, which will be used in Proposition
3.7
below:

(36)

(\varphi\,\#\,x)\cdot h=\sum_{(h)}S(h_{(2)})\rhd\varphi\lhd h_{(3)}\,\#\,S(h_{(1)})xh_{(4)}.

Lemma 3.6
.

The action (
36
) endows
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
with a structure of right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra.

Proof.

We compute:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(h)}\bigl((\varphi\,\#\,x)\cdot h_{(1)}\bigr)\bigl((\psi\,\#\,y)\cdot h_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(h)}\bigl(S(h_{(2)})\rhd\varphi\lhd h_{(3)}\,\#\,S(h_{(1)})xh_{(4)}\bigr)\bigl(S(h_{(6)})\rhd\psi\lhd h_{(7)}\,\#\,S(h_{(5)})yh_{(8)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x),(h)\\
(S(h_{(1)})),(h_{(4)})\end{subarray}}\bigl(S(h_{(2)})\rhd\varphi\lhd h_{(3)}\bigr)\star\bigl(S(h_{(1)})_{(1)}x_{(1)}h_{(4)(1)}S(h_{(6)})\rhd\psi\lhd h_{(7)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\#\,S(h_{(1)})_{(2)}x_{(2)}h_{(4)(2)}S(h_{(5)})yh_{(8)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(x),(h)}\bigl(S(h_{(3)})\rhd\varphi\lhd h_{(4)}\bigr)\star\bigl(S(h_{(2)})x_{(1)}h_{(5)}S(h_{(8)})\rhd\psi\lhd h_{(9)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\#\,S(h_{(1)})x_{(2)}h_{(6)}S(h_{(7)})yh_{(10)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(x),(h)}\bigl(S(h_{(3)})\rhd\varphi\lhd h_{(4)}\bigr)\star\bigl(S(h_{(2)})x_{(1)}\rhd\psi\lhd h_{(5)}\bigr)\,\#\,S(h_{(1)})x_{(2)}yh_{(6)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(x),(h)}S(h_{(2)})\rhd\bigl(\varphi\star(x_{(1)}\rhd\psi)\bigr)\lhd h_{(3)}\,\#\,S(h_{(1)})x_{(2)}yh_{(4)}=\bigl((\varphi\,\#\,x)(\psi\,\#\,y)\bigr)\cdot h

We used the properties of the antipode
\displaystyle S
and for the last step we used that both
\displaystyle\rhd
and
\displaystyle\lhd
are
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra structures on
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
.
∎

Recall the map (see
[
BR22
, Th. 4.3]
and the references therein)

(37)

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\Phi_{0,1}:&\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)&\longrightarrow&H\\
&\varphi&\longmapsto&(\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id})(RR^{\prime})=\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2})}\bigl\langle\varphi,R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(2)}\bigr\rangle R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}\end{array}

where
\displaystyle R^{1}
,
\displaystyle R^{2}
denote two copies of
\displaystyle R\in H^{\otimes 2}
. The map
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
is a morphism of
\displaystyle H
-module-algebras when
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
is endowed with the action
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
in (
23
) and
\displaystyle H
is endowed with the right adjoint action
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{ad}^{r}(h)(x)=\sum_{(h)}S(h_{(1)})xh_{(2)}
. Moreover it take values in
\displaystyle H^{\mathrm{lf}}
, the subspace of locally finite elements for
\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}^{r}
.

The morphism (
37
) relates
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
and
\displaystyle H
. We now discuss a morphism which relates
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
. Let

(38)

\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})^{\mathrm{lf}}=\bigl\{v\in\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\,\bigl|\,\dim(v\cdot H)<\infty\bigr.\bigr\}

be the subspace of locally finite elements for the action (
36
). If
\displaystyle H
is finite-dimensional, we obviously have
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})^{\mathrm{lf}}=\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
; but we will see later in the case of
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
that
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})^{\mathrm{lf}}
is in general a strict subspace. The map
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
in the next proposition has been introduced (in a different form) in
[
Ale94
, §5]
.

Proposition 3.7
.

We have a morphism of right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebras

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\Phi_{1,0}:&\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\\
&\beta\otimes\alpha&\longmapsto&\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2}),(R^{3})}\left(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(2)}\rhd\beta\lhd R^{3}_{(1)}R^{1}_{(1)}\right)\,\#\,\left(R^{3}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha)\right)\end{array}

where
\displaystyle R^{1},R^{2},R^{3}
are three copies of
\displaystyle R\in H^{\otimes 2}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
is endowed with the action (
36
). The morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
actually takes values in
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})^{\mathrm{lf}}
.

Proof.

Let us first show that
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
is a morphism of right
\displaystyle H
-modules. Due to (
26
) it is enough to show that
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{A}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{B}
are
\displaystyle H
-linear morphisms
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
. Since
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathfrak{i}_{A}(\alpha)\bigr)=1_{H^{\circ}}\,\#\,\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha)
, we use that

\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
intertwines
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}^{r}
to get:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(\mathfrak{i}_{A}(\alpha))\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\Phi_{1,0}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{A}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(\alpha)\bigr)=1_{H^{\circ}}\,\#\,\Phi_{0,1}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(\alpha)\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{(h)}1_{H^{\circ}}\,\#\,S(h_{(1)})\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha)h_{(2)}=\bigl(1_{H^{\circ}}\,\#\,\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha)\bigr)\cdot h.

For
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\beta)
we compute:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\beta))\bigr)=\Phi_{1,0}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{B}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(\beta)\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2}),(R^{3}),(h)}\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(2)}S(h_{(2)})\rhd\beta\lhd h_{(1)}R^{3}_{(1)}R^{1}_{(1)}\bigr)\,\#\,R^{3}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2}),(R^{3}),(h)}\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(2)}S(h_{(4)})\rhd\beta\lhd h_{(3)}R^{3}_{(1)}R^{1}_{(1)}\bigr)\,\#\,S(h_{(1)})h_{(2)}R^{3}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}S(h_{(5)})h_{(6)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2}),(R^{3}),(h)}\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}S(h_{(5)})R^{2}_{(2)}\rhd\beta\lhd R^{3}_{(1)}h_{(2)}R^{1}_{(1)}\bigr)\,\#\,S(h_{(1)})R^{3}_{(2)}h_{(3)}S(h_{(4)})R^{2}_{(1)}h_{(6)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2}),(R^{3}),(h)}\bigl(S\bigl(h_{(3)}S^{-1}(R^{1}_{(2)})\bigr)R^{2}_{(2)}\rhd\beta\lhd R^{3}_{(1)}h_{(2)}R^{1}_{(1)}\bigr)\,\#\,S(h_{(1)})R^{3}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}h_{(4)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2}),(R^{3}),(h)}\bigl(S(h_{(2)})R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(2)}\rhd\beta\lhd R^{3}_{(1)}R^{1}_{(1)}h_{(3)}\bigr)\,\#\,S(h_{(1)})R^{3}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}h_{(4)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\beta)\bigr)\cdot h.

We simply used that
\displaystyle R\Delta_{H}=\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{H}R
and that
\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}\otimes S^{-1})(R)=R^{-1}
.

The simplest way to show that
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
is a morphism of algebras uses the description of the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
based on the matrices
\displaystyle\overset{V}{A}
,
\displaystyle\overset{V}{B}
(Proposition
3.4
); see
[
Ale94
]
(with different conventions), and
[
Fai19a
, Prop 4.6]
where this is done in detail in the present setup.

The last claim is due to the fact that all the elements of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
are locally finite. To see this, recall first that all the elements in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
are locally finite. Indeed, for each finite dimensional
\displaystyle H
-module
\displaystyle V
, the (finite dimensional) subspace
\displaystyle C(V)\subset H^{\circ}
spanned by the matrix coefficients of
\displaystyle V
is stable by
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
. Any element
\displaystyle v\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
being a finite sum of matrix coefficients, it is contained in some
\displaystyle C(V_{1})+\ldots+C(V_{n})
and we have the claim. Now by (
26
), any element in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
can be written as
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i}\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\beta_{i})\,\mathfrak{i}_{A}(\alpha_{i})
, with
\displaystyle\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
. Since
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{A},\mathfrak{i}_{B}
are
\displaystyle H
-linear,
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\beta_{i})
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{A}(\alpha_{i})
are locally finite for each
\displaystyle i
, and since
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
is a
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra the product
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\beta_{i})\,\mathfrak{i}_{A}(\alpha_{i})
is locally finite as well.
∎

3.3.
The case
\displaystyle H=U^{\mathrm{ad}}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})

Recall from §
2.4
that
\displaystyle U_{q}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\displaystyle U^{\mathrm{ad}}_{q}=U^{\mathrm{ad}}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
denote respectively the simply connected and adjoint quantum groups, which are Hopf algebra over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
and where
\displaystyle q
is an indeterminate. The previous definitions of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
must be adapted when
\displaystyle H=U^{\mathrm{ad}}_{q}
because this Hopf algebra is not quasitriangular in the usual sense. Indeed, as explained in §
2.5
, the
\displaystyle R
-matrix lives in the categorical completion
\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\otimes 2}
. We can overcome this issue thanks to the pairing (
16
) and the fact that for any
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{O}_{q}

(39)

\sum_{(R)}\langle\varphi,R_{(1)}\rangle R_{(2)}\in U_{q}\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{(R)}\langle\varphi,R_{(2)}\rangle R_{(1)}\in U_{q}

(note that
a priori
these elements are in
\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{q}
).

As in §
2.6
, we denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})
the extension of scalars to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}=\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)
. Using (
16
) we extend the left and right coregular actions (
19
) of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
to left and right coregular actions of
\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{q}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})
. Hence
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})
is a right
\displaystyle(\mathbb{U}_{q}\otimes\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{cop}}_{q})
-module-algebra for the action (
21
). Recall from §
2.5
that
\displaystyle R\in\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\otimes 2}
has coefficients in
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
, thus the same is true for the twist
\displaystyle F
defined in (
20
). We have the twisted Hopf algebra
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{0,1}=(\mathbb{U}_{q}\otimes\mathbb{U}^{\mathrm{cop}}_{q})_{F}
and the twisted module-algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})_{F}
. The latter is the
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
-vector space
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})
with the product (
22
), which makes sense due to (
39
). In
[
BR22
, Prop. 4.1]
it is shown that restricting the product of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})_{F}
on the
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
-subspace
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
gives a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
-subalgebra. So we can define

Definition 3.8
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(\mathfrak{g})
is the
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
-vector space
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
endowed with the product (
22
).

We simply write
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
when
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
has been fixed.

Let
\displaystyle\Gamma\in\mathbb{A}_{0,1}^{\otimes 2}
be the twist which has been introduced in Lemma
3.1
. We have the twisted Hopf algebra
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{1,0}=(\mathbb{A}_{0,1}^{\otimes 2})_{\Gamma}
and since
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})_{F}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})_{F}
is a right
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{0,1}^{\otimes 2}
-module-algebra, we can define

Definition 3.9
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(\mathfrak{g})
is the twist
\displaystyle\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})_{F}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})_{F}\bigr)_{\Gamma}
.

We simply write
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
when
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
has been fixed. Explicitly,
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(\mathfrak{g})
is the
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
-vector space
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})
endowed with the product (
24
). Note that contrarily to the case of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{q}
is not a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
-subalgebra so we are forced to work over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
. This is because the terms coming from the
\displaystyle\Theta
factors of the
\displaystyle R
-matrices (see §
2.5
), which have coefficients in
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
, do not compensate each other in the exchange relation of Proposition
3.4
. Actually
\displaystyle\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{2/D})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{2/D})\bigr)_{\Gamma}
is a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{2/D})
-subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
; for
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
we have
\displaystyle D=2
, so in this special case we can work over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
.

By construction
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
is a right
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{0,1}
-module-algebra and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
is a right
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{1,0}
-module-algebra. Specializing the results of §
3.1
, we have morphisms of Hopf algebras

\mathbb{U}_{q}\xrightarrow{\Delta_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}}\mathbb{A}_{0,1}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{U}_{q}\xrightarrow{\Delta^{(3)}_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}}\mathbb{A}_{1,0}

where
\displaystyle\Delta^{(3)}_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}
is the iterated coproduct (see Lemma
3.3
). Combining this with the morphism of Hopf algebras
\displaystyle\iota:U_{q}\to\mathbb{U}_{q}
introduced below (
9
), we obtain that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
are right
\displaystyle U_{q}
-module-algebras for the actions
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
in (
23
) and (
27
) respectively.

Using the pairing from (
16
) and the embedding
\displaystyle\iota:U_{q}\to\mathbb{U}_{q}
, we get a pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})\otimes U_{q}(q^{1/D})\to\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})

where
\displaystyle U_{q}(q^{1/D})=U_{q}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
is the extension of scalars to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
.
It follows that the left (and right) coregular action of
\displaystyle U_{q}(q^{1/D})
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})
is well-defined and allows us to define the Heisenberg double
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}=\mathcal{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
as the smash product

\mathcal{H}_{q}=\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})\,\#\,U_{q}(q^{1/D}).

Explicitly, it is the
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
-vector space
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})\otimes U_{q}(q^{1/D})
endowed with the product (
35
). For simplicity of notation we often do not write the extension of scalars to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
.

Proposition 3.10
.

The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}
does not have non-trivial zero divisors.

Proof.

Recall from §
2
the filtration
\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{\mathbf{m}})_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{2N+1}}
of
\displaystyle U_{q}
which is based on the PBW basis. By Corollary
2.6
the family of subspaces
\displaystyle\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{q}\#\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{\mathbf{m}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{2N+1}}
is a filtration of the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}
. Indeed, if
\displaystyle\varphi\,\#\,x\in\mathcal{O}_{q}\,\#\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{\mathbf{m}}
and
\displaystyle\psi\,\#\,y\in\mathcal{O}_{q}\,\#\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{\mathbf{n}}
we have

(\varphi\,\#\,x)(\psi\,\#\,y)=\sum_{(x)}\varphi\star(x_{(1)}\rhd\psi)\,\#\,x_{(2)}y\in\mathcal{O}_{q}\,\#\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{n}}.

The associated graded algebra is

\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{O}_{q}\#\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}}(\mathcal{H}_{q})=\mathcal{O}_{q}\,\#\,\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}}(U_{q}).

Let us explain this equality more precisely. Recall from §
2.1
that
{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}
denote the matrix coefficients of finite-dimensional
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
-modules, and assume that for each
\displaystyle V
we use a basis of weight vectors
\displaystyle(v_{j})
with weights
\displaystyle(\epsilon_{j})
.
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
is generated by such matrix coefficients. For simplicity, write
{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}
,
\displaystyle\overline{E_{\beta_{i}}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{F_{\beta_{i}}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{K_{\mu}}
instead of the cosets
\displaystyle(_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}\,\#\,1)+\mathcal{O}_{q}\,\#\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{<\mathbf{0}}
,
\displaystyle(\varepsilon\,\#\,E_{\beta_{i}})+\mathcal{O}_{q}\,\#\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{<d(E_{\beta_{i}})}
,
\displaystyle(\varepsilon\,\#\,F_{\beta_{i}})+\mathcal{O}_{q}\,\#\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{<d(F_{\beta_{i}})}
,
\displaystyle(\varepsilon\,\#\,K_{\mu})+\mathcal{O}_{q}\,\#\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{<d(K_{\mu})}
in
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{O}_{q}\#\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}}(\mathcal{H}_{q})
. Thanks to Proposition
2.5
we get

\overline{E_{\beta_{k}}}\,{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}={{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}\,\overline{E_{\beta_{k}}},\quad\overline{F_{\beta_{k}}}\,{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}=q^{-(\beta_{k},\epsilon_{j})}{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}\,\overline{F_{\beta_{k}}},\quad\overline{K_{\nu}}\,{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}=q^{(\nu,\epsilon_{j})}{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}\,\overline{K_{\nu}}.

It follows from these relations and (
8
) that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{O}_{q}\#\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}}(\mathcal{H}_{q})
is a quasi-polynomial ring over
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
, generated over
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
by
\displaystyle\overline{E_{\beta_{i}}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{F_{\beta_{i}}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{K_{\nu}}
(with
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq N
,
\displaystyle\mu\in P
). Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
does not have non-trivial zero divisors
[
BG02
, Th. I.8.9]
, it follows from a general result (see e.g.
[
MCR01
, §1.2.9]
) that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{O}_{q}\#\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}}(\mathcal{H}_{q})
does not have non-trivial zero divisors. By Lemma
2.4
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}
does not have non-trivial zero divisors as well.
∎

It follows from (
39
) that the formulas of the maps
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
in §
3.2
are well-defined and give morphisms of right
\displaystyle U_{q}
-module-algebras

\Phi_{0,1}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}\to U_{q},\qquad\Phi_{1,0}:\mathcal{L}_{1,0}\to\mathcal{H}_{q}

where the action on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
is
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
in (
23
) and (
27
), the action on
\displaystyle U_{q}
is the right adjoint action
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{ad}^{r}(h)(x)=\sum_{(h)}S(h_{(1)})xh_{(2)}
and the action on
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}
is (
36
). It is known that
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
affords an isomorphism of
\displaystyle U_{q}
-module-algebras
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}U_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}}
, where
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}}
is the subspace of locally finite elements for
\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}^{r}

[
Bau00
, Th. 3]
(also see
[
BR22
, Th 4.3]
for this statement in the present framework). We now prove an analogous statement for
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
. Denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}}
the subspace of locally finite elements (see (
38
)).

Theorem 3.11
.

1. The morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}:\mathcal{L}_{1,0}\to\mathcal{H}_{q}
is injective.

2. The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
does not have non-trivial zero divisors.

3. The image of
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}}
.

Proof.

For simplicity we write
\displaystyle U_{q}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
instead of
\displaystyle U_{q}(q^{1/D})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})
.

1. Recall first that

\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\beta\otimes\alpha\bigr)=\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\beta)\,\mathfrak{i}_{A}(\alpha)\bigr)=\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\beta)\bigr)\,\bigl(1_{\mathcal{O}_{q}}\,\#\,\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha)\bigr).

For
\displaystyle\lambda,\sigma\in P
, consider

{}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}_{q})_{\sigma}=\bigl\{\varphi\in\mathcal{O}_{q}\,|\,\forall\,\nu\in P,\>K_{\nu}\rhd\varphi=q^{(\nu,\lambda)}\varphi\text{ and }\varphi\lhd K_{\nu}=q^{(\sigma,\nu)}\varphi\bigr\}

and note that
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
is the direct sum of all these subspaces. It follows that

\mathcal{L}_{1,0}=\bigoplus_{\lambda,\sigma\in P}\mathfrak{i}_{B}\bigl({{}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}_{q})_{\sigma}}\bigr)\,\mathfrak{i}_{A}\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}\bigr),\quad\mathcal{H}_{q}=\bigoplus_{\lambda,\sigma\in P}{{}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}_{q})_{\sigma}}\,\#\,U_{q}.

By (
15
), if
\displaystyle\beta\in{{}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}_{q})_{\sigma}}
we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle K_{\nu}\rhd\!\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(2)}\rhd\beta\lhd R^{3}_{(1)}R^{1}_{(1)}\bigr)\,\#\,R^{3}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}=q^{(\nu,\lambda-\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2})}\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(2)}\rhd\beta\lhd R^{3}_{(1)}R^{1}_{(1)}\bigr)\,\#\,R^{3}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(2)}\rhd\beta\lhd R^{3}_{(1)}R^{1}_{(1)}\bigr)\!\lhd K_{(\nu)}\,\#\,R^{3}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}=q^{(\nu,\sigma-\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{3})}\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(2)}\rhd\beta\lhd R^{3}_{(1)}R^{1}_{(1)}\bigr)\,\#\,R^{3}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}

for some
\displaystyle\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\in Q_{+}
. As a result
\displaystyle\textstyle\Phi_{1,0}(\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\beta))\in\bigoplus_{(\lambda^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\leq(\lambda,\sigma)}{{}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{q})_{\sigma^{\prime}}}\,\#\,U_{q}
, where
\displaystyle\leq
is the partial order on
\displaystyle P^{2}
defined by
\displaystyle(\lambda^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\leq(\lambda,\sigma)
if and only if
\displaystyle\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}\in Q_{+}
and
\displaystyle\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}\in Q_{+}
. Hence thanks to the expression of
\displaystyle R
in (
14
) we see that

(40)

\begin{array}[]{rl}\textstyle\Phi_{1,0}(\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\beta))&\!\!\in\bigl(\Theta^{1}_{(2)}\Theta^{2}_{(2)}\rhd\beta\lhd\Theta^{3}_{(1)}\Theta^{1}_{(1)}\bigr)\,\#\,\Theta^{3}_{(2)}\Theta^{2}_{(1)}+\bigoplus_{(\lambda^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})<(\lambda,\sigma)}{{}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{q})_{\sigma^{\prime}}}\,\#\,U_{q}\\[15.00002pt]
&=q^{(\lambda,\sigma)}\beta\,\#\,K_{\lambda+\sigma}+\bigoplus_{(\lambda^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})<(\lambda,\sigma)}{{}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{q})_{\sigma^{\prime}}}\,\#\,U_{q}\end{array}

where the second equality is due to (
12
) (and
\displaystyle\Theta_{1}
,
\displaystyle\Theta_{2}
,
\displaystyle\Theta_{3}
denote three copies of
\displaystyle\Theta
). We are now in position to show our result. Let
\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
be a non-zero element and write it as
\displaystyle\textstyle x=\sum_{\lambda,\sigma\in P}\sum_{i\in I_{\lambda,\sigma}}\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\beta_{\lambda,\sigma,i})\,\mathfrak{i}_{A}(\alpha_{\lambda,\sigma,i})
with
\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda,\sigma,i}\in{{}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{O}_{q})_{\sigma}}
for all
\displaystyle i\in I_{\lambda,\sigma}
and such that for each
\displaystyle\lambda,\sigma
the elements
\displaystyle\bigl(\beta_{\lambda,\sigma,i}\bigr)_{i\in I_{\lambda,\sigma}}
are linearly independent. Take a
\displaystyle(\lambda,\sigma)
maximal for the order
\displaystyle\leq
such that there exists at least one
\displaystyle i\in I_{\lambda,\sigma}
with
\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda,\sigma,i}\neq 0
and
\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda,\sigma,i}\neq 0
. Then using (
40
) one obtains

\Phi_{1,0}(x)\in q^{(\lambda,\sigma)}\sum_{i\in I_{\lambda,\sigma}}\beta_{\lambda,\sigma,i}\,\#\,K_{\lambda+\sigma}\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha_{\lambda,\sigma,i})+\bigoplus_{(\lambda^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\neq(\lambda,\sigma)}{{}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{q})_{\sigma^{\prime}}}\,\#\,U_{q}.

The morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
is injective (see the comments before the theorem); moreover
\displaystyle K_{\lambda+\sigma}
is invertible, so there exists at least one
\displaystyle i\in I_{\lambda,\sigma}
with
\displaystyle K_{\lambda+\sigma}\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha_{\lambda,\sigma,i})\neq 0
and since the elements
\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda,\sigma,i}
are linearly independent we conclude that
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}(x)\neq 0
.

2. Follows from Proposition
3.10
and item 1.

3. We already know from Proposition
3.7
that
\displaystyle\mathrm{im}(\Phi_{1,0})\subset\mathcal{H}_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}}
. Let us prove the converse inclusion. Let
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes U_{q}
be the tensor product of the right modules
\displaystyle\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{q},\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigr)
and
\displaystyle\bigl(U_{q},\mathrm{ad}^{r}\bigr)
,
i.e.
the right action is

(41)

(\varphi\otimes x)\cdot h=\sum_{(h)}S(h_{(2)})\rhd\varphi\lhd h_{(1)}\otimes S(h_{(3)})xh_{(4)}.

As recalled above the theorem,
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
provides an isomorphism of
\displaystyle U_{q}
-modules
\displaystyle\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{q},\mathrm{coad}^{r})\overset{\sim}{\to}\bigl(U_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}},\mathrm{ad}^{r}\bigr)
. Hence we have an isomorphism of
\displaystyle U_{q}
-modules

\Phi_{0,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}:\>\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes U_{q}\,\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\,U_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}}\otimes U_{q}

and we see that
\displaystyle\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes U_{q}\bigr)^{\mathrm{lf}}\cong\bigl(U_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}}\otimes U_{q}\bigr)^{\mathrm{lf}}\subset(U_{q}\otimes U_{q})^{\mathrm{lf}}=U_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}}\otimes U_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}}
where the last equality is due to
[
KLNY20
, Th. 2]
. The converse inclusion being obvious, we conclude that
\displaystyle\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes U_{q}\bigr)^{\mathrm{lf}}=\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes U_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}}
.
Now consider the map

\begin{array}[]{rcll}I:&\mathcal{H}_{q}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes U_{q}\\
&\varphi\,\#\,x&\longmapsto&\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2}),(R^{3})}\end{array}S\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}\bigr)\rhd\varphi\lhd R^{1}_{(1)}R^{3}_{(1)}\otimes S\bigl(R^{3}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(2)}\bigr)x.

It is an isomorphism of
\displaystyle U_{q}
-modules, thanks to a straightforward computation which uses
\displaystyle R\Delta=\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}R
to pass from the action (
36
) to the action (
41
). Another computation which uses
\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}\otimes S^{-1})(R)=R^{-1}
reveals that

(42)

I\circ\Phi_{1,0}(\beta\otimes\alpha)=\sum_{(\beta)}\beta_{(1)}\otimes\Phi_{0,1}\bigl(\beta_{(2)}\alpha\bigr)

where
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{(\beta)}\beta_{(1)}\otimes\beta_{(2)}
is the coproduct of
\displaystyle\beta
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
and
\displaystyle\beta_{(2)}\alpha
is the product of
\displaystyle\beta_{(2)}
and
\displaystyle\alpha
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
. We need one more fact: there is a map
\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{L}_{0,1}}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}\to\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
such that
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{(\varphi)}\varphi_{(1)}S_{\mathcal{L}_{0,1}}(\varphi_{(2)})=\varepsilon(\varphi)1_{\mathcal{L}_{0,1}}
for all
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
;
it is given by

S_{\mathcal{L}_{0,1}}(\varphi)=\sum_{(R)}S_{\mathcal{O}_{q}}\bigl(S(R_{(1)})\rhd\varphi\lhd R_{(2)}u^{-1}\bigr)

where
\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{O}_{q}}
is the antipode of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
and
\displaystyle\textstyle u=\sum_{(R)}S(R_{(2)})R_{(1)}
is the Drinfeld element, which satisfies
\displaystyle S^{2}(x)=uxu^{-1}
for all
\displaystyle x\in U_{q}
. In fact,
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
is isomorphic to a coend
[
Fai20a
, Prop. 6.3]
which has a natural structure of a Hopf algebra, and
\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{L}_{0,1}}
is the antipode for this structure. We are finally ready to conclude. Take
\displaystyle\psi\otimes y\in(\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes U_{q})^{\mathrm{lf}}
. By the above discussion we know that
\displaystyle y\in U_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}}
, so there exists
\displaystyle\gamma\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
such that
\displaystyle y=\Phi_{0,1}(\gamma)
. By (
42
) and the property of
\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{L}_{0,1}}
we find

I\circ\Phi_{1,0}\biggl(\sum_{(\psi)}\psi_{(1)}\otimes S_{\mathcal{L}_{0,1}}(\psi_{(2)})\gamma\biggr)=\sum_{(\psi)}\psi_{(1)}\otimes\Phi_{0,1}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}S_{\mathcal{L}_{0,1}}(\psi_{(3)})\gamma\bigr)=\psi\otimes y

which proves that
\displaystyle\mathrm{im}(I\circ\Phi_{1,0})=(\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes U_{q})^{\mathrm{lf}}
. Since
\displaystyle I
is an isomorphism of
\displaystyle U_{q}
-modules the result follows.
∎

Note that
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}}
is a strict subspace of
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}
. Observe for instance that the action (
36
) is such that
\displaystyle(1_{\mathcal{O}_{q}}\,\#\,x)\cdot h=1_{\mathcal{O}_{q}}\,\#\,\mathrm{ad}^{r}(h)(x)
. Thus if
\displaystyle x\in U_{q}
is not locally finite then
\displaystyle 1_{\mathcal{O}_{q}}\,\#\,x\not\in\mathcal{H}_{q}^{\mathrm{lf}}
. For instance one can take
\displaystyle x=K_{i}
.

3.4.
Noetherianity of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}

The strategy is to construct a filtration on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
and to show that the associated graded algebra satisfies the relations in Lemma
2.2
. First we need to recall and adapt a filtration of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
taken from
[
VY20
]
.

For
\displaystyle\mu\in P_{+}
and
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
we define

\displaystyle\displaystyle{{}_{\lambda}C(\mu)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\bigl\{\varphi\in C(\mu)\,\big|\,\forall\,\nu\in P,\>K_{\nu}\rhd\varphi=q^{(\lambda,\nu)}\varphi\bigr\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle C(\mu)_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\bigl\{\varphi\in C(\mu)\,\big|\,\forall\,\nu\in P,\>\varphi\lhd K_{\nu}=q^{(\lambda,\nu)}\varphi\bigr\}

where
\displaystyle C(\mu)
is the subspace of matrix coefficients of
\displaystyle V_{\mu}
, the irreducible
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
-module with highest weight
\displaystyle\mu
. Consider the ordered abelian monoid

(43)

\Lambda=\bigl\{(\mu,\lambda)\in P_{+}\times P\,\big|\,\lambda\text{ is a weight of }V_{\mu}\bigr\}

for the order
\displaystyle\preceq
defined by
\displaystyle(\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\preceq(\mu,\lambda)
if and only if
\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}\preceq\mu
and
\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}\preceq\lambda
, where the order
\displaystyle\preceq
on
\displaystyle P
is defined in §
2.3
. Note that
\displaystyle\preceq
is well-founded on
\displaystyle\Lambda
(although it is not on
\displaystyle P_{+}\times P
) and satisfies condition (
4
).

It is not difficult to show that

{}_{\lambda}C(\mu)\,{{}_{\lambda^{\prime}}C(\mu^{\prime})}=\bigoplus_{(\nu,\kappa)\preceq(\mu+\mu^{\prime},\lambda+\lambda^{\prime})}{{}_{\kappa}C(\nu)}

in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
(see
[
VY20
, §3.14.4]
, but with a slightly different product), and similarly for the subspaces
\displaystyle C(\mu)_{\lambda}
. By the general discussion of §
2.2
it follows that we have filtrations
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r}
of the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
given by

\mathcal{F}_{\ell}^{\mu,\lambda}=\bigoplus_{(\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\preceq(\mu,\lambda)}{{}_{\lambda^{\prime}}C(\mu^{\prime})}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\mu,\lambda}=\bigoplus_{(\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\preceq(\mu,\lambda)}C(\mu^{\prime})_{\lambda^{\prime}}

for all
\displaystyle(\mu,\lambda)\in\Lambda
. The filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
has been used in
[
BR21
, §3]
where it was denoted by
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2}
.

For
\displaystyle x\in\{\ell,r\}
, let
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})
be the graded algebra associated to
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{x}
(see §
2.2
). The vector space
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})
can be identified with
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
: we identify
\displaystyle\varphi\in{{}_{\lambda}C(\mu)}
(resp.
\displaystyle\varphi\in C(\mu)_{\lambda}
) with
\displaystyle\varphi+\mathcal{F}_{\ell}^{\prec\mu,\lambda}
(resp.
\displaystyle\varphi+\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\prec\mu,\lambda}
). We denote by
\displaystyle\circ_{x}
the product on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
obtained through this identification.

We introduce some notations in order to describe the product
\displaystyle\circ_{x}
. For
\displaystyle\mu\in P_{+}
, denote by
\displaystyle e_{1}^{\mu},\ldots,e_{m}^{\mu}
a basis of weight vectors of
\displaystyle V_{\mu}
. Let
\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mu}_{i}\in P
be the weight of
\displaystyle e^{\mu}_{i}
and assume that the vectors are numbered in such a way that
\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mu}_{i}\succ\epsilon^{\mu}_{j}
implies
\displaystyle i<j
. Let
\displaystyle e^{1}_{\mu},\ldots,e^{m}_{\mu}
be the dual basis and let
\displaystyle{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}:x\mapsto e^{i}_{\mu}(x\cdot e_{j}^{\mu})
be the associated matrix coefficients. Note that

(44)

K_{\nu}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}=q^{(\nu,\epsilon^{\mu}_{j})}{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}},\quad\text{and}\quad{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\lhd K_{\nu}=q^{(\nu,\epsilon_{i}^{\mu})}{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}

or in other words
\displaystyle{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\in{{}_{\epsilon^{\mu}_{j}}C(\mu)}
and
\displaystyle{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\in C(\mu)_{\epsilon^{\mu}_{i}}
. We use similar notations for a basis
\displaystyle e^{\eta}_{1},\ldots,e^{\eta}_{n}
of
\displaystyle V_{\eta}
. Finally for
\displaystyle\varphi\in C(\mu)
and
\displaystyle\psi\in C(\nu)
, we put

\varphi\,\overline{\star}\,\psi=\pi_{\mu+\nu}(\varphi\star\psi)

where
\displaystyle\star
is the usual product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
and
\displaystyle\pi_{\mu+\nu}:C(\mu)\star C(\nu)\to C(\mu+\nu)
is the projection defined by (
17
).

The following facts were proved in
[
VY20
, pp. 177-178]
for the filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
(but note that in
[
VY20
]
the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
is slightly different from ours). We give a proof of these facts for the filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r}
.

Lemma 3.12
.

For
\displaystyle x\in\{\ell,r\}
and with the notations just introduced, we have

1.
\displaystyle{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\circ_{x}{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}=q^{(\epsilon^{\mu}_{j},\epsilon^{\eta}_{l}-\epsilon^{\eta}_{k})}{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\>\overline{\star}\>{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}+\sum_{s=j+1}^{m}\sum_{t=1}^{l-1}\alpha^{ijkl}_{st}{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{s}}\>\overline{\star}\>{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{t}}
, with
\displaystyle\alpha^{ijkl}_{st}\in\mathbb{C}(q)
.

Moreover
\displaystyle\alpha^{ijkl}_{st}=0
unless
\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}\prec\epsilon^{\mu}_{j}
and
\displaystyle\epsilon^{\eta}_{t}\succ\epsilon^{\eta}_{l}
. It follows in particular that
\displaystyle{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\circ_{\ell}{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}={{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\circ_{r}{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}
.

2.
\displaystyle{{}_{\mu}\phi^{k}_{l}}\circ_{x}{{}_{\eta}\phi^{i}_{j}}=q^{(\epsilon^{\mu}_{i},\epsilon^{\eta}_{k}-\epsilon^{\eta}_{l})}{{}_{\eta}\phi^{i}_{j}}\>\overline{\star}\>{{}_{\mu}\phi^{k}_{l}}+\sum_{s=i+1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{k-1}\beta^{ijkl}_{st}{{}_{\eta}\phi^{s}_{j}}\>\overline{\star}\>{{}_{\mu}\phi^{t}_{l}}
, with
\displaystyle\beta^{ijkl}_{st}\in\mathbb{C}(q)
.

Moreover
\displaystyle\beta^{ijkl}_{st}=0
unless
\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}\prec\epsilon^{\mu}_{i}
and
\displaystyle\epsilon^{\eta}_{t}\succ\epsilon^{\eta}_{k}
.

3. It follows that

(45)

\begin{array}[]{rl}{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\circ_{x}{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\>=\>q^{(\epsilon^{\mu}_{i}+\epsilon^{\mu}_{j},\epsilon^{\eta}_{k}-\epsilon^{\eta}_{l})}{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\circ_{x}{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}&\!\!\!+\>\sum_{s=j}^{m}\sum_{t=1}^{l}\sum_{u=i+1}^{m}\sum_{v=1}^{k-1}\delta^{ijkl}_{stuv}\,{{}_{\eta}\phi^{u}_{s}}\circ_{x}{{}_{\mu}\phi^{v}_{t}}\\
&+\>\sum_{s=j+1}^{m}\sum_{t=1}^{l-1}\gamma^{ijkl}_{st}{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{s}}\circ_{x}{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{t}}\end{array}

with
\displaystyle\delta^{ijkl}_{stuv},\gamma^{ijkl}_{st}\in\mathbb{C}(q)
. Moreover
\displaystyle\delta^{ijkl}_{stuv}=0
unless
\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}\preceq\epsilon^{\mu}_{j},\epsilon^{\eta}_{t}\succeq\epsilon^{\eta}_{l},\epsilon^{\mu}_{u}\prec\epsilon^{\mu}_{i},\epsilon^{\eta}_{v}\succ\epsilon^{\eta}_{k}
and
\displaystyle\gamma^{ijkl}_{st}=0
unless
\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}\prec\epsilon^{\mu}_{j},\epsilon^{\eta}_{t}\succ\epsilon^{\eta}_{l}
.

Proof.

1. We show the equality for
\displaystyle\circ_{r}
. We use the formula (
22
) giving the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
. If we take two summands
\displaystyle R^{1}_{(1)}\otimes R^{1}_{(2)}
and
\displaystyle R^{2}_{(1)}\otimes R^{2}_{(2)}
in the expression (
14
) for
\displaystyle R
, we see by (
15
) and (
44
) that

\biggl(\bigl(R_{(2)}^{2}S(R_{(2)}^{1})\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\bigr)\star\bigl(R_{(1)}^{2}\rhd{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\lhd R_{(1)}^{1}\bigr)\biggr)\lhd K_{\nu}\\
=q^{(\nu,\epsilon^{\mu}_{i}+\epsilon^{\eta}_{k}-\gamma_{1})}\bigl(R_{(2)}^{2}S(R_{(2)}^{1})\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\bigr)\star\bigl(R_{(1)}^{2}\rhd{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\lhd R_{(1)}^{1}\bigr)

for some
\displaystyle\gamma_{1}\in Q_{+}
. Hence, by definition of
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{r}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})
and by (
15
),

\displaystyle\displaystyle{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\circ_{r}{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{(R)}\bigl(R_{(2)}S(\Theta_{(2)})\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\bigr)\>\overline{\star}\>\bigl(R_{(1)}\rhd{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\lhd\Theta_{(1)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=q^{-(\epsilon^{\mu}_{j},\epsilon^{\eta}_{k})}\sum_{(R)}\bigl(R_{(2)}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\bigr)\>\overline{\star}\>\bigl(R_{(1)}\rhd{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\bigr)

where the second equality uses (
12
). Again by (
15
), we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl(K_{\nu}\rhd\bigl(R_{(2)}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\bigr)\bigr)\>\overline{\star}\>\bigl(R_{(1)}\rhd{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\bigr)=q^{(\nu,\epsilon^{\mu}_{j}-\gamma_{2})}\bigl(R_{(2)}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\bigr)\>\overline{\star}\>\bigl(R_{(1)}\rhd{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\bigr),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl(R_{(2)}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\bigr)\>\overline{\star}\>\bigl(K_{\nu}\rhd\bigl(R_{(1)}\rhd{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\bigr)\bigr)=q^{(\nu,\epsilon^{\eta}_{l}+\gamma_{2})}\bigl(R_{(2)}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\bigr)\>\overline{\star}\>\bigl(R_{(1)}\rhd{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\bigr)

for some
\displaystyle\gamma_{2}\in Q_{+}
. Hence

\bigl(R_{(2)}^{2}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\bigr)\>\overline{\star}\>\bigl(R_{(1)}^{2}\rhd{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\bigr)\in\mathrm{span}\bigl({{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{s}}\>\overline{\star}\>{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{t}}\bigr)_{\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}\preceq\epsilon^{\mu}_{j},\>\epsilon^{\eta}_{t}\succeq\epsilon^{\eta}_{l}}\subset\mathrm{span}\bigl({{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{s}}\>\overline{\star}\>{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{t}}\bigr)_{s\geq j,\>t\leq l}

where the inclusion is due to the particular numbering of the basis vectors.
Thanks to the expression (
14
) for
\displaystyle R
, we obtain more precisely

\displaystyle\displaystyle{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\circ_{r}{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=q^{-(\epsilon^{\mu}_{j},\epsilon^{\eta}_{k})}\bigl(\Theta_{(2)}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\bigr)\>\overline{\star}\>\bigl(\Theta_{(1)}\rhd{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\bigr)+\sum_{s=j+1}^{m}\sum_{t=1}^{l-1}\alpha^{ijkl}_{st}{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{s}}\>\overline{\star}\>{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{t}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=q^{(\epsilon^{\mu}_{j},\epsilon^{\eta}_{l}-\epsilon^{\eta}_{k})}{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\>\overline{\star}\>{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}+\sum_{s=j+1}^{m}\sum_{t=1}^{l-1}\alpha^{ijkl}_{st}{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{s}}\>\overline{\star}\>{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{t}}.

2. Using the relation
\displaystyle R\Delta=\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}R
, we see that the formula (
22
) for the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
can be rewritten as follows:

\alpha\beta=\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2})}\bigl(\beta\lhd R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(1)}\bigr)\star\bigl(S(R^{1}_{(2)})\rhd\alpha\lhd R^{2}_{(2)}\bigr).

To obtain the desired equalities in the claim, it suffices to repeat a proof like in item 1 but with this alternative formula for the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
.

3. Each of the formulas in items 1 and 2 allows one to express the product
\displaystyle\overline{\star}
in terms of the product
\displaystyle\circ_{x}
(using induction). We get the result by comparing the two resulting expressions for
\displaystyle\overline{\star}
; for more details see
[
VY20
, pp. 177-178]
.
∎

Recall that
\displaystyle\varpi_{1},\ldots,\varpi_{m}
denote the fundamental weights of the semisimple Lie algebra
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
. Any irreducible
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
-module
\displaystyle V_{\mu}
is a direct summand of some tensor product of the modules
\displaystyle V_{\varpi_{1}},\ldots,V_{\varpi_{m}}
(see e.g.
[
BG02
, §I.6.12]
). Hence the matrix coefficients
\displaystyle{{}_{\varpi_{r}}\phi^{i}_{j}}
generate
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
as an algebra, and there is of course a finite number of such elements. From item 1 of Lemma
3.12
, one can deduce that these matrix coefficients generate
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})
as well (see
[
VY20
, pp. 178]
). We form an ordered list
\displaystyle(u_{1},\ldots,u_{p})
containing all the elements
\displaystyle{{}_{\varpi_{r}}\phi^{i}_{j}}
such that for any
\displaystyle u_{b}={{}_{\varpi_{r}}\phi^{i}_{j}}
and
\displaystyle u_{a}={{}_{\varpi_{s}}\phi^{k}_{l}}
the following condition is satisfied:

(46)

\biggl[\epsilon^{\varpi_{r}}_{i}\prec\epsilon^{\varpi_{s}}_{k}\>\text{ or }\>\bigl(\epsilon^{\varpi_{r}}_{i}=\epsilon^{\varpi_{s}}_{k}\>\text{ and }\>\epsilon^{\varpi_{r}}_{j}\prec\epsilon^{\varpi_{s}}_{l}\bigr)\biggr]\implies b<a.

With this choice of numbering, the formula (
45
) can be rewritten as

(47)

\forall\,1\leq b<a\leq p,\quad u_{a}\circ_{x}u_{b}=q_{ab}u_{b}\circ_{x}u_{a}+\sum_{s=1}^{b-1}\sum_{t=1}^{p}\alpha^{ab}_{st}u_{s}\circ_{x}u_{t}

for certain scalars
\displaystyle q_{ab}\in\mathbb{C}(q)^{\times}
and
\displaystyle\alpha^{ab}_{st}\in\mathbb{C}(q)
. It follows that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})
is Noetherian by Lemma
2.2
and hence
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
is Noetherian by Lemma
2.1
.

We are now ready to study the Noetherianity for
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
. Consider

(48)

\mathcal{F}^{\mu,\lambda,\eta,\kappa}=\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\mu,\lambda}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\ell}^{\eta,\kappa}\subset\mathcal{L}_{1,0}\quad\text{for }(\mu,\lambda),(\eta,\kappa)\in\Lambda.

Here we use that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})^{\otimes 2}
as a vector space and we implicitly extend the scalars of
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{x}^{\mu,\lambda}
to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
. On the indexing set
\displaystyle\Lambda\times\Lambda
of the family
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
of spaces
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mu,\lambda,\eta,\kappa}
, we put the product order, again denoted by
\displaystyle\preceq
, namely
\displaystyle(\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})\preceq(\mu,\lambda,\eta,\kappa)
if and only if
\displaystyle(\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\preceq(\mu,\lambda)
and
\displaystyle(\eta^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})\preceq(\eta,\kappa)
.

Lemma 3.13
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
is a filtration of the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
.

Proof.

Take
\displaystyle\beta\otimes\alpha\in C(\mu)_{\lambda}\otimes{{}_{\kappa}C(\eta)}
and
\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}\otimes\alpha^{\prime}\in C(\mu^{\prime})_{\lambda^{\prime}}\otimes{{}_{\kappa^{\prime}}C(\eta^{\prime})}
and recall that the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
is described in (
24
). By (
15
), we have

(49)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\bigl(R_{(2)}^{4}R_{(1)}^{3}\rhd\beta^{\prime}\lhd R_{(1)}^{1}R_{(1)}^{2}\bigr)\lhd K_{\nu}&=&\!\!\!q^{(\nu,\lambda^{\prime}-\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2})}\bigl(R_{(2)}^{4}R_{(1)}^{3}\rhd\beta^{\prime}\lhd R_{(1)}^{1}R_{(1)}^{2}\bigr)\\[5.0pt]
K_{\nu}\rhd\bigl(R_{(2)}^{3}S(R_{(2)}^{1})\rhd\alpha\lhd R_{(2)}^{2}R_{(1)}^{4}\bigr)&=&\!\!\!q^{(\nu,\kappa-\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{3})}\bigl(R_{(2)}^{3}S(R_{(2)}^{1})\rhd\alpha\lhd R_{(2)}^{2}R_{(1)}^{4}\bigr)\end{array}

for some
\displaystyle\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\in Q_{+}
. In other words:

R_{(2)}^{4}R_{(1)}^{3}\rhd\beta^{\prime}\lhd R_{(1)}^{1}R_{(1)}^{2}\in\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}},\quad R_{(2)}^{3}S(R_{(2)}^{1})\rhd\alpha\lhd R_{(2)}^{2}R_{(1)}^{4}\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}^{\eta,\kappa}

Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{l}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r}
are filtrations of the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
we thus have

\beta\bigl(R_{(2)}^{4}R_{(1)}^{3}\rhd\beta^{\prime}\lhd R_{(1)}^{1}R_{(1)}^{2}\bigr)\in\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\mu+\mu^{\prime},\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}},\quad\bigl(R_{(2)}^{3}S(R_{(2)}^{1})\rhd\alpha\lhd R_{(2)}^{2}R_{(1)}^{4}\bigr)\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}^{\eta+\eta^{\prime},\kappa+\kappa^{\prime}}

which gives the claim by the definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
.
∎

We identify
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{L}_{1,0})
with
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})^{\otimes 2}
as a vector space:
\displaystyle\beta\otimes\alpha\in C(\mu)_{\lambda}\otimes{{}_{\kappa}C(\eta)}
is identified with
\displaystyle\beta\otimes\alpha+\mathcal{F}^{\prec(\mu,\lambda,\eta,\kappa)}
. We denote by
\displaystyle\diamond
the resulting product on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})^{\otimes 2}
. Let
\displaystyle\beta\in C(\mu)_{\lambda}
and
\displaystyle\alpha\in{{}_{\kappa}C(\eta)}
; then (
14
) and (
49
) yield

(50)

\mathfrak{i}_{A}(\alpha)\diamond\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\beta)=\sum_{(R)}\mathfrak{i}_{B}\bigl(R_{(2)}\Theta^{3}_{(1)}\rhd\beta\lhd\Theta^{1}_{(1)}\Theta^{2}_{(1)}\bigr)\diamond\mathfrak{i}_{A}\bigl(\Theta^{3}_{(2)}S(\Theta^{1}_{(2)})\rhd\alpha\lhd\Theta^{2}_{(2)}R_{(1)}\bigr)

where
\displaystyle\Theta^{1}
,
\displaystyle\Theta^{2}
,
\displaystyle\Theta^{3}
are three copies of
\displaystyle\Theta
.

Theorem 3.14
.

The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
is Noetherian.

Proof.

We use the notations and assumptions introduced just before Lemma
3.12
for basis elements and matrix coefficients. We are going to show that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{L}_{1,0})
is Noetherian. The first task is to simplify the formula (
50
) when
\displaystyle\alpha={{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}
and
\displaystyle\beta={{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}
are matrix coefficients of some irreducible modules
\displaystyle V_{\eta}
and
\displaystyle V_{\mu}
. By (
15
) and (
44
), we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl(K_{\nu}\rhd(R_{(2)}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}})\bigr)\otimes({{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\lhd R_{(1)})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=q^{(\nu,\epsilon^{\mu}_{j}-\gamma)}(R_{(2)}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}})\otimes({{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\lhd R_{(1)})

\displaystyle\displaystyle(R_{(2)}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}})\otimes\bigl(({{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\lhd R_{(1)})\lhd K_{\nu})\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=q^{(\nu,\epsilon^{\eta}_{k}-\gamma)}(R_{(2)}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}})\otimes({{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\lhd R_{(1)})

for some
\displaystyle\gamma\in Q_{+}
. Hence

(R_{(2)}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}})\otimes({{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\lhd R_{(1)})\in\mathrm{span}\bigl\{{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{s}}\otimes{{}_{\eta}\phi^{t}_{l}}\bigr\}_{\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}\preceq\epsilon^{\mu}_{j},\>\epsilon^{\eta}_{t}\preceq\epsilon^{\eta}_{k}}\subset\mathrm{span}\bigl\{{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{s}}\otimes{{}_{\eta}\phi^{t}_{l}}\bigr\}_{s\geq j,t\geq k}

where the inclusion is due to the assumption on the numbering of the basis vectors. Using the expression (
14
) we obtain more precisely

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R)}(R_{(2)}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}})\otimes({{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\lhd R_{(1)})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\in(\Theta_{(2)}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}})\otimes({{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\lhd\Theta_{(1)})+\mathrm{span}\bigl\{{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{s}}\otimes{{}_{\eta}\phi^{t}_{l}}\bigr\}_{\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}\prec\epsilon^{\mu}_{j},\>\epsilon^{\eta}_{t}\prec\epsilon^{\eta}_{k}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=q^{(\epsilon^{\mu}_{j},\epsilon^{\eta}_{k})}{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\otimes{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}+\mathrm{span}\bigl\{{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{s}}\otimes{{}_{\eta}\phi^{t}_{l}}\bigr\}_{\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}\prec\epsilon^{\mu}_{j},\>\epsilon^{\eta}_{t}\prec\epsilon^{\eta}_{k}}.

We can now simplify (
50
):

(51)

\begin{array}[]{rl}&\mathfrak{i}_{A}({{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}})\diamond\mathfrak{i}_{B}({{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}})\\[6.99997pt]
&=\sum_{(R)}\mathfrak{i}_{B}\bigl(R_{(2)}\Theta^{3}_{(1)}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\lhd\Theta^{1}_{(1)}\Theta^{2}_{(1)}\bigr)\diamond\mathfrak{i}_{A}\bigl(\Theta^{3}_{(2)}S(\Theta^{1}_{(2)})\rhd{{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\lhd\Theta^{2}_{(2)}R_{(1)}\bigr)\\[25.00003pt]
&=q^{(\epsilon^{\mu}_{i},\epsilon^{\eta}_{k}-\epsilon^{\eta}_{l})+(\epsilon^{\mu}_{j},\epsilon^{\eta}_{l})}\sum_{(R)}\mathfrak{i}_{B}\bigl(R_{(2)}\rhd{{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}}\bigr)\diamond\mathfrak{i}_{A}\bigl({{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}}\lhd R_{(1)}\bigr)\\
&=q^{(\epsilon^{\mu}_{i},\epsilon^{\eta}_{k}-\epsilon^{\eta}_{l})+(\epsilon^{\mu}_{j},\epsilon^{\eta}_{l}+\epsilon^{\eta}_{k})}\,\mathfrak{i}_{B}({{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{j}})\diamond\mathfrak{i}_{A}({{}_{\eta}\phi^{k}_{l}})+\sum_{s=j+1}^{m}\sum_{t=k+1}^{n}c_{st}^{ijkl}\,\mathfrak{i}_{B}({{}_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{s}})\diamond\mathfrak{i}_{A}({{}_{\eta}\phi^{t}_{l}})\end{array}

for some
\displaystyle c_{s}^{ijkl}\in\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
such that
\displaystyle c_{s}^{ijkl}=0
unless
\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}\prec\epsilon^{\mu}_{j}
and
\displaystyle\epsilon^{\eta}_{t}\prec\epsilon^{\eta}_{k}
.

Recall from above (
46
) the finite sequence of elements
\displaystyle(u_{k})
which generate
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})
. Since
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{B}\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\mu,\lambda}\bigr)=\mathcal{F}^{\mu,\lambda,0,0}
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{A}\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{l}^{\eta,\kappa}\bigr)=\mathcal{F}^{0,0,\eta,\kappa}
, the linear maps
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{B}
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{A}
are morphisms of algebras
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{r}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})\to\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{L}_{1,0})
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{l}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})\to\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{L}_{1,0})
. Moreover, we have
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\beta)\diamond\mathfrak{i}_{A}(\alpha)=\beta\otimes\alpha
. It follows that the algebra
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{L}_{1,0})
is generated by the elements
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{B}(u_{k})
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{A}(u_{k})
. We organize them in a sequence
\displaystyle(x_{1},\ldots,x_{2p})
by

x_{1}=\mathfrak{i}_{B}(u_{1}),\ldots,x_{p}=\mathfrak{i}_{B}(u_{p}),\>x_{p+1}=\mathfrak{i}_{A}(u_{1}),\ldots,x_{2p}=\mathfrak{i}_{A}(u_{p}).

Thanks to relations (
47
) and (
51
) we have:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\forall\,1\leq b<a\leq p,\quad x_{a}\diamond x_{b}=q_{ab}x_{b}\diamond x_{a}+\sum_{s=1}^{b-1}\sum_{t=1}^{p}\alpha^{ab}_{st}x_{s}\diamond x_{t}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\forall\,p+1\leq b<a\leq 2p,\quad x_{a}\diamond x_{b}=q_{ab}x_{b}\diamond x_{a}+\sum_{s=1}^{b-1}\sum_{t=1}^{p}\alpha^{ab}_{st}x_{s}\diamond x_{t}

(52)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\forall\,1\leq b\leq p<a\leq 2p,\quad x_{a}\diamond x_{b}=q^{\prime}_{ab}x_{b}\diamond x_{a}+\sum_{s=1}^{b-1}\sum_{t=1}^{p}\lambda^{ab}_{st}x_{s}\diamond x_{t}

for certain scalars
\displaystyle q_{ab},q^{\prime}_{ab}\in\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})^{\times}
and
\displaystyle\alpha^{ab}_{st},\lambda^{ab}_{st}\in\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
. These three relations cover all the possible cases for the indices
\displaystyle a
and
\displaystyle b
, and they all have the form required by Lemma
2.2
. It follows that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{L}_{1,0})
is Noetherian, and that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
is Noetherian as well thanks to Lemma
2.1
.
∎

4.
The graph algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}

Following the definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,n}(H)
in
[
BR22
]
, we will define
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
as a twist of the tensor product of
\displaystyle g
copies of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
and
\displaystyle n
copies of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
and then explain the equivalence with the other constructions based on matrix relations
[
Ale94
,
AGS95
,
BR95
]
or braided tensor product
[
AS96b
]
. See also
[
MW21
]
for a construction based on twisting; in their work
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
corresponds to
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{*}_{\Gamma_{g,n}}
, where
\displaystyle\Gamma_{g,n}
is the “daisy” graph.

The definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
in §
4.1
works for any quasitriangular Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H
. The general definition is adapted to
\displaystyle H=U^{\mathrm{ad}}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
in §
4.2
. Our main results are in §
4.3
and §
4.4
.

4.1.
Definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)

Let
\displaystyle H
be a quasitriangular Hopf algebra with an invertible antipode. As in
[
BR22
, §6.1]
we will use twisted tensor products, so let us recall a few facts about this operation. For two Hopf algebras
\displaystyle A
and
\displaystyle B
, a
bicharacter
is an invertible element
\displaystyle\chi\in B\otimes A
such that

(\Delta_{B}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A})(\chi)=\chi_{23}\,\chi_{13},\qquad(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes\Delta_{A})(\chi)=\chi_{12}\,\chi_{13},

where the subscripts describe embeddings in
\displaystyle B\otimes B\otimes A
and
\displaystyle B\otimes A\otimes A
respectively; for instance
\displaystyle\textstyle\chi_{23}=\sum_{(\chi)}1_{B}\otimes\chi_{(1)}\otimes\chi_{(2)}
. For such a
\displaystyle\chi
, the element
\displaystyle X=1_{A}\otimes\chi\otimes 1_{B}
is a twist for
\displaystyle A\otimes B
(recall the definition of a twist in §
3.1
). The twisted Hopf algebra
\displaystyle(A\otimes B)_{X}
is denoted by
\displaystyle A\otimes^{\chi}B
and is called the
twisted tensor product of
\displaystyle A
and
\displaystyle B
by
\displaystyle\chi
. If
\displaystyle M
is a
\displaystyle A
-module-algebra and
\displaystyle N
is a
\displaystyle B
-module-algebra, then
\displaystyle M\otimes N
is a
\displaystyle(A\otimes B)
-module-algebra; the twist
\displaystyle(M\otimes N)_{X}
is denoted by
\displaystyle M\otimes^{\chi}N
and is called the
twisted tensor product of
\displaystyle M
and
\displaystyle N
by
\displaystyle\chi
.

Example 4.1
.

1. The element
\displaystyle R^{\prime}=\textstyle\sum_{(R)}R_{(2)}\otimes R_{(1)}
is a bicharacter (with
\displaystyle A=B=H
).

2. An easy computation reveals that the Hopf algebra
\displaystyle A_{0,1}(H)
, which was defined as a twist in section
3.1
, can actually be written as
\displaystyle H\otimes^{R^{\prime}}H
.

3. The Hopf algebra
\displaystyle A_{1,0}(H)
from section
3.1
has been defined as
\displaystyle A_{0,1}(H)\otimes^{\gamma}A_{0,1}(H)
(see Lemma
3.1
) and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\otimes^{\gamma}\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
.

Let
\displaystyle f_{A}:H\to A
and
\displaystyle f_{B}:H\to B
be morphisms of Hopf algebras and denote
\displaystyle f_{A}\odot f_{B}=(f_{A}\otimes f_{B})\Delta
. Then

(1)

\displaystyle(f_{B}\otimes f_{A})(R^{\prime})
is a bicharacter,

(2)

\displaystyle f_{A}\odot f_{B}
is a morphism of Hopf algebras
\displaystyle H\to A\otimes^{(f_{B}\otimes f_{A})(R^{\prime})}B
.

The twisted tensor product allows us to iterate the twist operation, as follows. Recall from §
3.1
that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
are module-algebras over the Hopf algebras
\displaystyle A_{0,1}(H)
and
\displaystyle A_{1,0}(H)
respectively. Let us put by convention
\displaystyle A_{0,0}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{0,0}(H)=k
(the base field of
\displaystyle H
). For
\displaystyle n\geq 0
we define

\displaystyle\displaystyle A_{0,n+1}(H)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=A_{0,n}(H)\otimes^{J_{n}}A_{0,1}(H)\quad\text{with }J_{n}=\bigl(\Delta\otimes\Delta^{\odot n}\bigr)(R^{\prime})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,n+1}(H)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{0,n}(H)\otimes^{J_{n}}\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)

where
\displaystyle\Delta^{\odot 0}=\varepsilon
and
\displaystyle\Delta^{\odot n}=\Delta^{\odot(n-1)}\odot\Delta
. For
\displaystyle g\geq 0
we define

\displaystyle\displaystyle A_{g+1,0}(H)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=A_{g,0}(H)\otimes^{K_{g}}A_{1,0}(H)\quad\text{with }K_{g}=\bigl(\Delta^{(3)}\otimes(\Delta^{(3)})^{\odot g}\bigr)(R^{\prime})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g+1,0}(H)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{g,0}(H)\otimes^{K_{g}}\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)

where
\displaystyle\Delta^{(3)}=(\Delta\otimes\Delta)\Delta
is the third iterated coproduct,
\displaystyle(\Delta^{(3)})^{\odot 0}=\varepsilon
and
\displaystyle(\Delta^{(3)})^{\odot g}=(\Delta^{(3)})^{\odot(g-1)}\odot\Delta^{(3)}
. To see that
\displaystyle J_{n}
(resp.
\displaystyle K_{g}
) is a bicharacter, recall that
\displaystyle\Delta:H\to A_{0,1}(H)
(resp.
\displaystyle\Delta^{(3)}:H\to A_{1,0}(H)
) is a morphism of Hopf algebras and make an induction from items 1 and 2 above.

In general we put

A_{g,n}(H)=A_{g,0}(H)\otimes^{F_{g,n}}A_{0,n}(H)\quad\text{with }F_{g,n}=\bigl(\Delta^{\odot n}\otimes(\Delta^{(3)})^{\odot g}\bigr)(R^{\prime})

and

Definition 4.2
.

The graph algebra is
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{g,0}(H)\otimes^{F_{g,n}}\mathcal{L}_{0,n}(H)
.

By construction
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is a (right)
\displaystyle A_{g,n}(H)
-module-algebra. As vectors spaces

A_{g,n}(H)=H^{\otimes(4g+2n)}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)=(H^{\circ})^{\otimes(2g+n)}

and the right action is given by

(\varphi_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\varphi_{2g+n})\cdot(x_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{4g+2n})=\bigl(S(x_{2})\rhd\varphi_{1}\lhd x_{1}\bigr)\otimes\ldots\otimes\bigl(S(x_{4g+2n})\rhd\varphi_{2g+n}\lhd x_{4g+2n-1}\bigr).

Thanks to the morphism of Hopf algebras
\displaystyle(\Delta^{(3)})^{\odot g}\odot\Delta^{\odot n}=\Delta^{(4g+2n-1)}:H\to A_{g,n}(H)
, we obtain a right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra structure on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
, denoted by
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
:

(53)

\begin{array}[]{rl}\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(\varphi_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\varphi_{2g+n})&=(\varphi_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\varphi_{2g+n})\cdot\Delta^{(4g+2n-1)}(h)\\[5.0pt]
&=\sum_{(h)}\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(1)})(\varphi_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h_{(2g+n)})(\varphi_{2g+n})\end{array}

where the right action
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
in the second line is defined in (
23
). In other words,
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)^{\otimes(2g+n)}
as a
\displaystyle H
-module.

We now reformulate the definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
as a braided tensor product, which will be more convenient in order to describe the product. Let
\displaystyle\mathrm{Mod}\text{-}H
be the category of right
\displaystyle H
-modules; it is braided, with braiding:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}c_{U,V}:&U\otimes V&\longrightarrow&V\otimes U\\
&u\otimes v&\longmapsto&\sum_{(R)}\,(v\cdot R_{(1)})\otimes(u\cdot R_{(2)})\end{array}.

Let
\displaystyle(M,m_{M},1_{M})
and
\displaystyle(N,m_{N},1_{N})
be
\displaystyle H
-module-algebras (
i.e.
algebras in
\displaystyle\mathrm{Mod}\text{-}H
) and define

\displaystyle\displaystyle m_{M\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,N}:(M\otimes N)\otimes(M\otimes N)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{M}\,\otimes\,c_{N,M}\,\otimes\,\mathrm{id}_{N}}M\otimes M\otimes N\otimes N\xrightarrow{m_{M}\otimes m_{N}}M\otimes N,

\displaystyle\displaystyle 1_{M\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,N}=1_{M}\otimes 1_{N}.

This endows
\displaystyle M\otimes N
with a structure of (right)
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra, denoted by
\displaystyle M\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,N
and called the braided tensor product of
\displaystyle M
and
\displaystyle N

[
Maj92
, Lem. 9.2.12]
. It is easy to see that
\displaystyle\widetilde{\otimes}
is associative. We often write a pure tensor
\displaystyle m\otimes n\in M\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,N
as
\displaystyle m\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,n
to stress that we use the braided product in
\displaystyle M\otimes N
. Explicitly:

(54)

(m\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,n)(m^{\prime}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,n^{\prime})=\sum_{(R)}m(m^{\prime}\cdot R_{(1)})\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,(n\cdot R_{(2)})n^{\prime}.

The embeddings of
\displaystyle k
-vector spaces

(55)

\begin{array}[]{rcll}j_{M}:&M&\longrightarrow&M\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,N\\
&m&\longmapsto&m\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,1\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{rcll}j_{N}:&N&\longrightarrow&M\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,N\\
&n&\longmapsto&1\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,n\end{array}

are morphisms of
\displaystyle H
-module-algebras and we have

(56)

\displaystyle\displaystyle j_{M}(m)j_{N}(n)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=m\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,n,

(57)

\displaystyle\displaystyle j_{N}(n)j_{M}(m)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{(R)}j_{M}(m\cdot R_{(1)})j_{N}(n\cdot R_{(2)}).

Proposition 4.3
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)^{\widetilde{\otimes}g}\>\widetilde{\otimes}\>\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)^{\widetilde{\otimes}n}
as right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebras.

Proof.

Note that
\displaystyle K_{g}=\bigl(\Delta^{(3)}\otimes\Delta^{(4g-1)}\bigr)(R^{\prime})
; then by definition of the multiplication
\displaystyle m_{\mathcal{L}_{g+1,0}(H)}
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g+1,0}(H)
(recall (
18
) and the definition of the twisted tensor product) we have for
\displaystyle u,v\in\mathcal{L}_{g,0}(H)
and
\displaystyle x,y\in\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle m_{\mathcal{L}_{g+1,0}(H)}\bigl((u\otimes x)\otimes(v\otimes y)\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(K_{g})}m_{\mathcal{L}_{g,0}(H)}\bigl(u\otimes\bigl(v\cdot(K_{g})_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr)\otimes m_{\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)}\bigl(\bigl(x\cdot(K_{g})_{(1)}\bigr)\otimes y\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R)}m_{\mathcal{L}_{g,0}(H)}\bigl(u\otimes\bigl(v\cdot\Delta^{(4g-1)}(R_{(1)})\bigr)\bigr)\otimes m_{\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)}\bigl(\bigl(x\cdot\Delta^{(3)}(R_{(2)})\bigr)\otimes y\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R)}m_{\mathcal{L}_{g,0}(H)}\bigl(u\otimes\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R_{(1)})(v)\bigr)\otimes m_{\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R_{(2)})(x)\otimes y\bigr)=(u\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,x)(v\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,y).

For the third equality we used (
53
). Hence
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g+1,0}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{g,0}(H)\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
and it follows that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,0}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)^{\widetilde{\otimes}g}
. One shows similarly that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,n}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)^{\widetilde{\otimes}n}
and that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,0}(H)\otimes^{F_{g,n}}\mathcal{L}_{0,n}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{g,0}(H)\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{L}_{0,n}(H)
.
∎

Thanks to Proposition
4.3
and (
55
) we see that there are embeddings of
\displaystyle H
-module-algebras
\displaystyle j_{i}:\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
for
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq g
and
\displaystyle j_{i}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
for
\displaystyle g+1\leq i\leq g+n
, given by

(58)

j_{i}(x)=1^{\widetilde{\otimes}(i-1)}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,x\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,1^{\widetilde{\otimes}(g+n-i)}

where
\displaystyle 1
is the unit of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
(
i.e.

\displaystyle\varepsilon\otimes\varepsilon
) or of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
(
i.e.

\displaystyle\varepsilon
) depending on the position.
Recall the embeddings
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{A},\mathfrak{i}_{B}
from (
25
); we denote

(59)

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathfrak{i}_{A(i)}=j_{i}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{A},\>\>\mathfrak{i}_{B(i)}=j_{i}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{B}\quad\text{ for }1\leq i\leq g,\\[1.99997pt]
\mathfrak{i}_{M(i)}=j_{i}\quad\text{ for }g+1\leq i\leq g+n\end{array}

which are all embeddings
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
. Then from (
56
) and (
26
) we find

(60)

\begin{array}[]{rl}&\mathfrak{i}_{B(1)}(\varphi_{1})\,\mathfrak{i}_{A(1)}(\varphi_{2})\ldots\mathfrak{i}_{B(g)}(\varphi_{2g-1})\,\mathfrak{i}_{A(g)}(\varphi_{2g})\,\mathfrak{i}_{M(g+1)}(\varphi_{2g+1})\ldots\mathfrak{i}_{M(g+n)}(\varphi_{2g+n})\\[5.0pt]
=&j_{1}(\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2})\ldots j_{g}(\varphi_{2g-1}\otimes\varphi_{2g})\,j_{g+1}(\varphi_{2g+1})\ldots j_{g+n}(\varphi_{2g+n})\\[5.0pt]
=&(\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2})\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\ldots\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,(\varphi_{2g-1}\otimes\varphi_{2g})\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\varphi_{2g+1}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\ldots\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\varphi_{2g+n}\end{array}

and any element in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is a linear combination of such elements.

Proposition 4.4
.

The product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is entirely determined by the following equalities:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{X(i)}(\varphi)\,\mathfrak{i}_{X(i)}(\psi)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathfrak{i}_{X(i)}(\varphi\psi)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{A(i)}(\varphi)\,\mathfrak{i}_{B(i)}(\psi)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{(R^{1}),\ldots,(R^{4})}\!\!\!\mathfrak{i}_{B(i)}\bigl(R_{(2)}^{4}R_{(1)}^{3}\rhd\psi\lhd R_{(1)}^{1}R_{(1)}^{2}\bigr)\,\mathfrak{i}_{A(i)}\bigl(R_{(2)}^{3}S(R_{(2)}^{1})\rhd\varphi\lhd R_{(2)}^{2}R_{(1)}^{4}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{Y(j)}(\varphi)\,\mathfrak{i}_{X(i)}(\psi)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{(R^{1}),\ldots,(R^{4})}\!\!\!\mathfrak{i}_{X(i)}\bigl(S(R^{3}_{(1)}R^{4}_{(1)})\rhd\psi\lhd R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(1)}\bigr)\,\mathfrak{i}_{Y(j)}\bigl(S(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{3}_{(2)})\rhd\varphi\lhd R^{2}_{(2)}R^{4}_{(2)}\bigr)

for all
\displaystyle\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
, where
\displaystyle 1\leq i<j\leq g+n
and
\displaystyle X(i)
(resp.
\displaystyle Y(j)
) is indifferently
\displaystyle A(i)
,
\displaystyle B(i)
or
\displaystyle M(i)
(resp.
\displaystyle A(j)
,
\displaystyle B(j)
or
\displaystyle M(j)
) and
\displaystyle R^{1},\ldots,R^{4}
are four copies of
\displaystyle R\in H^{\otimes 2}
.

Proof.

It is clear that these formulas determine the product since they allow us to compute the product of any two elements from (
60
). The first formula simply expresses the fact that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{X(i)}
is a morphism of algebras. The second formula is obtained from (
24
) and (
26
) thanks to the algebra embedding
\displaystyle j_{i}:\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
. Let us prove the third formula. Assume for instance that
\displaystyle i<j\leq g
. Using Proposition
4.3
and the associativity of
\displaystyle\widetilde{\otimes}
we can write
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{i,0}(H)\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{L}_{g-i,n}(H)
; let
\displaystyle j_{\mathcal{L}_{i,0}(H)}
and
\displaystyle j_{\mathcal{L}_{g-i,n}(H)}
be the corresponding
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra embeddings (see (
55
)). It is easy to see that

\mathfrak{i}^{(g,n)}_{X(i)}=j_{\mathcal{L}_{i,0}(H)}\circ\mathfrak{i}^{(i,0)}_{X(i)},\qquad\mathfrak{i}^{(g,n)}_{Y(j)}=j_{\mathcal{L}_{g-i,n}(H)}\circ\mathfrak{i}^{(g-i,n)}_{Y(j-i)}

where
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}^{(g,n)}_{X(l)}
,
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}^{(i,0)}_{X(l)}
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}^{(g-i,n)}_{X(l)}
are the
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra embeddings from (
59
) for
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{i,0}(H)
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g-i,n}(H)
respectively. Hence we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}^{(g,n)}_{Y(j)}(\varphi)\,\mathfrak{i}^{(g,n)}_{X(i)}(\psi)=j_{\mathcal{L}_{g-i,n}(H)}\bigl(\mathfrak{i}^{(g-i,n)}_{Y(j-i)}(\varphi)\bigr)j_{\mathcal{L}_{i,0}(H)}\bigl(\mathfrak{i}^{(i,0)}_{X(i)}(\psi)\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R)}j_{\mathcal{L}_{i,0}(H)}\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R_{(1)})\bigl(\mathfrak{i}^{(i,0)}_{X(i)}(\psi)\bigr)\right)j_{\mathcal{L}_{g-i,n}(H)}\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R_{(2)})\bigr(\mathfrak{i}^{(g-i,n)}_{Y(j-i)}(\varphi)\bigl)\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R),(R_{(1)}),(R_{(2)})}j_{\mathcal{L}_{i,0}(H)}\!\left(\mathfrak{i}^{(i,0)}_{X(i)}\!\left(S((R_{(1)})_{(2)})\rhd\psi\lhd(R_{(1)})_{(1)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 130.0002pt\times j_{\mathcal{L}_{g-i,n}(H)}\!\left(\mathfrak{i}^{(g-i,n)}_{Y(j-i)}\!\left(S((R_{(2)})_{(2)})\rhd\psi\lhd(R_{(2)})_{(1)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),\ldots,(R^{4})}\!\!\!\mathfrak{i}_{X(i)}^{(g,n)}\bigl(S(R^{3}_{(1)}R^{4}_{(1)})\rhd\psi\lhd R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(1)}\bigr)\,\mathfrak{i}^{(g,n)}_{Y(j)}\bigl(S(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{3}_{(2)})\rhd\varphi\lhd R^{2}_{(2)}R^{4}_{(2)}\bigr).

For the second equality we used (
57
), for the third equality we used the
\displaystyle H
-linearity of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}^{(i,0)}_{X(i)}
,
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}^{(g-i,n)}_{Y(j-i)}
and the definition (
23
) of the
\displaystyle H
-action on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
, and for the last equality we used that
\displaystyle(\Delta\otimes\Delta)(R)=R_{14}R_{13}R_{24}R_{23}
, which follows from the axioms of a
\displaystyle R
-matrix. The other cases for
\displaystyle i
and
\displaystyle j
are treated similarly.
∎

Recall the notations introduced before Proposition
3.4
, in particular the matrix
\displaystyle\overset{V}{M}\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\otimes\mathrm{End}(V)
for any finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-module
\displaystyle V
. Let

(61)

\begin{array}[]{l}\overset{V}{A}(i)=(\mathfrak{i}_{A(i)}\otimes\mathrm{id})\bigl(\overset{V}{M}\bigr),\>\>\overset{V}{B}(i)=(\mathfrak{i}_{B(i)}\otimes\mathrm{id})\bigl(\overset{V}{M}\bigr)\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq g\\[3.99994pt]
\overset{V}{M}(i)=(\mathfrak{i}_{M(i)}\otimes\mathrm{id})\bigl(\overset{V}{M}\bigr)\quad\text{for }g+1\leq i\leq g+n\end{array}

which are elements in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\otimes\mathrm{End}(V)
, or in other words matrices of size
\displaystyle\dim(V)
with coefficients in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
. In mathematical physics
\displaystyle\overset{V}{A}(i),\overset{V}{B}(i),\overset{V}{M}(i)
are seen as quantum holonomy matrices associated to the usual generators
\displaystyle a_{i},b_{i},m_{i}
of the fundamental group of the surface
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}
with an open disk removed.

Proposition 4.5
.

The following relations hold true for any finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-modules
\displaystyle V,W
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\overset{V\otimes W}{X}\!\!\!\!(i)=\overset{V}{X}\hskip-1.0pt(i)_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{VW}\,\overset{W}{X}\hskip-1.0pt(i)_{2}\,(R^{\prime})_{VW}^{-1}\quad\text{ for }1\leq i\leq g+n,

\displaystyle\displaystyle R_{VW}\,\overset{V}{B}(i)_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{VW}\,\overset{W}{A}(i)_{2}=\overset{W}{A}(i)_{2}\,R_{VW}\,\overset{V}{B}(i)_{1}\,R_{VW}^{-1}\quad\text{ for }1\leq i\leq g,

\displaystyle\displaystyle R_{VW}\,\overset{V}{X}\hskip-1.0pt(i)_{1}\,R_{VW}^{-1}\,\overset{W}{Y}\hskip-1.0pt(j)_{2}=\overset{W}{Y}\hskip-1.0pt(j)_{2}\,R_{VW}\,\overset{V}{X}\hskip-1.0pt(i)_{1}\,R_{VW}^{-1}\quad\text{ for }1\leq i<j\leq g+n,

where
\displaystyle X(i)
(resp.
\displaystyle Y(j)
) is indifferently
\displaystyle A(i)
,
\displaystyle B(i)
or
\displaystyle M(i)
(resp.
\displaystyle A(j)
,
\displaystyle B(j)
or
\displaystyle M(j)
).

These matrix equalities entirely describe the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
.

Proof.

This is completely parallel to the proof of Proposition
3.4
. By the same arguments we already get that the first and second matrix relations are equivalent to the first and second formulas in Proposition
4.4
. It remains to show that the third matrix relation is equivalent to the third formula in Proposition
4.4
; this is similar to the computation in the proof of Proposition
3.4
and is left as an exercise for the reader.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

In many papers the matrix relations in Proposition
4.5
are taken as a definition for the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
.

4.2.
The case
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})

Since
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
