is not quasitriangular in the usual sense (§
2.5
), the above definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
must be slightly adapted. We use the notations from sections
3.3
and
4.1
. Let us put by convention
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{0,0}=\mathcal{L}_{0,0}(q^{1/D})=\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
. Recall from §
3.3
that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
are module-algebras over the Hopf algebras
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{1,0}
respectively. For
\displaystyle n\geq 0
we define

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{0,n+1}=\mathbb{A}_{0,n}\otimes^{J_{n}}\mathbb{A}_{0,1}\quad\text{with }J_{n}=\bigl(\Delta_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}\otimes\Delta_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}^{\odot n}\bigr)(R^{\prime})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,n+1}(q^{1/D})=\mathcal{L}_{0,n}(q^{1/D})\otimes^{J_{n}}\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})_{F}

where
\displaystyle\textstyle R^{\prime}=\sum_{(R)}R_{(2)}\otimes R_{(1)}
is the flip of the
\displaystyle R
-matrix
\displaystyle R\in\mathbb{U}_{q}^{\otimes 2}
. For
\displaystyle g\geq 0
let

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{g+1,0}=\mathbb{A}_{g,0}\otimes^{K_{g}}\mathbb{A}_{1,0}\quad\text{with }K_{g}=\bigl(\Delta_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}^{(3)}\otimes(\Delta_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}^{(3)})^{\odot g}\bigr)(R^{\prime})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g+1,0}=\mathcal{L}_{g,0}\otimes^{K_{g}}\mathcal{L}_{1,0}.

In
[
BR22
, Prop. 6.2]
it is shown that restricting the product of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,n}(q^{1/D})
on the
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
-subspace
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\otimes n}
gives a
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
-subalgebra, which is denoted by
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,n}
. However, as we have seen in section
3.3
, the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
(and hence
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,0}
) can only be defined over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
. This forces us to define the general case over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
, as follows:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{g,n}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathbb{A}_{g,0}\otimes^{F_{g,n}}\mathbb{A}_{0,n}\quad\text{with }F_{g,n}=\bigl(\Delta_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}^{\odot n}\otimes(\Delta_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}^{(3)})^{\odot g}\bigr)(R^{\prime})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{g,0}\otimes^{F_{g,n}}\mathcal{L}_{0,n}(q^{1/D}).

Explicitly,
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is the
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
-vector space
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})^{\otimes(2g+n)}
endowed with the product described in Proposition
4.4
.

By construction
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is a right
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{g,n}
-module-algebra. As in §
4.1
, we have a morphism of Hopf algebras
\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbb{U}_{q}}^{(4g+2n-1)}:\mathbb{U}_{q}\to\mathbb{A}_{g,n}
. Combining this with the morphism of Hopf algebras
\displaystyle\iota:U_{q}\to\mathbb{U}_{q}
introduced below (
9
), we obtain that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is a right
\displaystyle U_{q}
-module-algebra for the action
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
in (
53
).

Note that the factorization of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
as the braided tensor product
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}^{\widetilde{\otimes}g}\>\widetilde{\otimes}\>\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\widetilde{\otimes}n}
(Prop.
4.3
) holds true for
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
as well, though the whole category of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
-modules (not necessarily finite-dimensional) is not braided. This is because all the elements of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
are locally-finite, and so the pairing (
16
) and (
39
) ensure that the formula (
54
) for the product in
\displaystyle M\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,N
is well-defined when
\displaystyle M=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
,
\displaystyle N=\mathcal{L}_{g^{\prime},n^{\prime}}
and the right action
\displaystyle\cdot
is
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
.

Proposition 4.7
.

The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is finitely generated.

Proof.

Recall the algebra
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{l}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})
from §
3.4
. It is proved in
[
VY20
, pp. 178]
that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{l}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})
is finitely generated, thanks to the first item in Lemma
3.12
. As a result
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
is finitely generated, see Lemma
2.3
. Let
\displaystyle x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
be the generators of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
. We deduce from (
60
) that the elements
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{B(k)}(x_{s})
,
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{A(k)}(x_{s})
,
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{M(l)}(x_{s})
with
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq g
,
\displaystyle g+1\leq l\leq g+n
,
\displaystyle 1\leq s\leq m
generate
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
.
∎

4.3.
Noetherianity of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}

Here are the steps of the proof:

(1)

we introduce a filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{1}
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
whose graded algebra
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
simplifies the third relation in Proposition
4.4
,

(2)

we construct a filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{2}
of
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
thanks to the filtrations on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
and on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
from §
3.4
,

(3)

using Lemma
2.2
, we show that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\bigl(\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})\bigr)
is Noetherian,

(4)

it follows from Lemma
2.1
that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
is Noetherian and that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is Noetherian.

We write
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
instead of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(q^{1/D})
for simplicity of notation. For
\displaystyle\mu,\omega\in P_{+}
and
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
consider the subspaces

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})_{\mu,\lambda}=\bigl\{\varphi\in C(\mu)\,\big|\,\forall\,\nu\in P,\>\mathrm{coad}^{r}(K_{\nu}^{-1})(\varphi)=q^{(\lambda,\nu)}\varphi\bigr\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{1,0})_{\mu,\omega,\lambda}=\bigl\{x\in C(\mu)\otimes C(\omega)\,\big|\,\forall\,\nu\in P,\>\mathrm{coad}^{r}(K_{\nu}^{-1})(x)=q^{(\lambda,\nu)}x\bigr\}

where
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
is defined in (
23
) for
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
and in (
27
) for
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
, and
\displaystyle C(\mu)
is the subspace of matrix coefficients of the irreducible
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
-module with highest weight
\displaystyle\mu
. For a finite sequence
\displaystyle\bigl([\mu],[\lambda]\bigr)
, with
\displaystyle[\mu]=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{2g+n})\in(P_{+})^{2g+n}
and
\displaystyle[\lambda]=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{g+n})\in P^{g+n}
, we put

(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})_{[\mu],[\lambda]}=(\mathcal{L}_{1,0})_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\lambda_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes(\mathcal{L}_{1,0})_{\mu_{2g-1},\mu_{2g},\lambda_{g}}\otimes(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})_{\mu_{2g+1},\lambda_{g+1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})_{\mu_{2g+n},\lambda_{g+n}}.

Recall the partial order
\displaystyle\preceq
on
\displaystyle P
defined in §
2.3
. For
\displaystyle\mu,\mu^{\prime},\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in P_{+}
and
\displaystyle\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in P
we write
\displaystyle(\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\preceq(\mu,\lambda)
(resp.
\displaystyle(\mu^{\prime},\omega^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\preceq(\mu,\omega,\lambda)
) to mean that
\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}\preceq\mu
and
\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}\preceq\lambda
(resp.
\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}\preceq\mu
and
\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}\preceq\omega
and
\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}\preceq\lambda
). We say that
\displaystyle\bigl([\mu^{\prime}],[\lambda^{\prime}]\bigr)\prec_{1}\bigl([\mu],[\lambda]\bigr)
if and only if

\displaystyle\displaystyle\>(\mu^{\prime}_{2g+n},\lambda^{\prime}_{g+n})\prec(\mu_{2g+n},\lambda_{g+n})

or

\displaystyle\displaystyle\>\Big((\mu^{\prime}_{2g+n},\lambda^{\prime}_{g+n})=(\mu_{2g+n},\lambda_{g+n})\text{ and }(\mu^{\prime}_{2g+n-1},\lambda^{\prime}_{g+n-1})\prec(\mu_{2g+n-1},\lambda_{g+n-1})\Big)\text{ or }\ldots

This defines a partial order
\displaystyle\preceq_{1}
on
\displaystyle(P_{+})^{2g+n}\times P^{g+n}
, which is a block lexicographic order starting from the right. For instance if
\displaystyle g=1
and
\displaystyle n=1
we have
\displaystyle\bigl([\mu^{\prime}_{1},\mu^{\prime}_{2},\mu^{\prime}_{3}],[\lambda^{\prime}_{1},\lambda^{\prime}_{2}]\bigr)\prec_{1}\bigl([\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3}],[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]\bigr)
if and only if

(\mu^{\prime}_{3},\lambda^{\prime}_{2})\prec(\mu_{3},\lambda_{2})\text{ or }\Big((\mu^{\prime}_{3},\lambda^{\prime}_{2})=(\mu_{3},\lambda_{2})\text{ and }(\mu^{\prime}_{1},\mu^{\prime}_{2},\lambda^{\prime}_{1})\prec(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\lambda_{1})\Big).

Now let

\Xi=\bigl\{\bigl([\mu],[\lambda]\bigr)\in(P_{+})^{2g+n}\times P^{g+n}\,\big|\,(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})_{[\mu],[\lambda]}\neq 0\bigr\}.

Then
\displaystyle(\Xi,\preceq_{1})
is an ordered abelian monoid and the order
\displaystyle\preceq_{1}
is well-founded on
\displaystyle\Xi
. Moreover
\displaystyle\preceq_{1}
satisfies the condition (
4
) and it follows that

\mathcal{G}_{1}^{[\mu],[\lambda]}=\bigoplus_{([\mu^{\prime}],[\lambda^{\prime}])\preceq_{1}([\mu],[\lambda])}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})_{[\mu^{\prime}],[\lambda^{\prime}]}\qquad(\text{with }\bigl([\mu^{\prime}],[\lambda^{\prime}]\bigr)\in\Xi)

defines a filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{1}
of the vector space
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
indexed by
\displaystyle\Xi
. For any
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq g
and
\displaystyle g+1\leq l\leq g+n
it is convenient to use the obvious embeddings

(62)

S_{k}:P_{+}^{2}\times P\to P_{+}^{2g+n}\times P^{g+n},\qquad S_{l}:P_{+}\times P\to P_{+}^{2g+n}\times P^{g+n}

which are such that

(63)

(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})_{S_{k}(\mu,\omega,\lambda)}=j_{k}\bigl((\mathcal{L}_{1,0})_{\mu,\omega,\lambda}\bigr),\qquad(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})_{S_{l}(\mu,\lambda)}=j_{l}\bigl((\mathcal{L}_{0,1})_{\mu,\lambda}\bigr)

for
\displaystyle\mu,\omega\in P_{+}
,
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
, and where
\displaystyle j_{k}
,
\displaystyle j_{l}
are the embeddings from (
58
).

Lemma 4.8
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{1}
is a filtration of the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
.

Proof.

Let

y_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes y_{g+n}=j_{1}(y_{1})\ldots j_{g+n}(y_{g+n})\in(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})_{[\mu^{\prime}],[\lambda^{\prime}]}

where by definition we have
\displaystyle y_{k}\in(\mathcal{L}_{1,0})_{\mu^{\prime}_{2k-1},\mu^{\prime}_{2k},\lambda^{\prime}_{k}}
for
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq g
and
\displaystyle y_{l}\in(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})_{\mu^{\prime}_{g+l},\lambda^{\prime}_{l}}
for
\displaystyle g+1\leq l\leq g+n
. Take an element
\displaystyle x\in(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})_{\mu,\lambda}
; so
\displaystyle j_{m}(x)\in(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})_{S_{m}(\mu,\lambda)}
for any
\displaystyle m\in\{g+1,\ldots,g+n\}
(see (
62
) and (
63
)). By Proposition
4.3
and (
57
) we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle j_{m}(x)\,j_{1}(y_{1})\ldots j_{g+n}(y_{g+n})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>\,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),\ldots,(R^{m-1})}j_{1}\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R^{1}_{(1)})(y_{1})\right)\ldots j_{m-1}\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R^{m-1}_{(1)})(y_{m-1})\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hphantom{\sum_{(R^1), \ldots, (R^{m-1})}}\>\>\times j_{m}\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}\ldots R^{m-1}_{(2)}\bigr)(x)y_{m}\right)j_{m+1}(y_{m+1})\ldots j_{g+n}(y_{g+n}).

For any term in this sum and any
\displaystyle\nu\in P
, the fact that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
is a
\displaystyle U_{q}
-module-algebras for
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
together with (
15
) gives

\mathrm{coad}^{r}(K_{\nu}^{-1})\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}\ldots R^{m-1}_{(2)}\bigr)(x)\,y_{m}\right)\\
=q^{(\nu,\,\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}_{m}-\gamma_{1}-\ldots-\gamma_{m-1})}\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}\ldots R^{m-1}_{(2)}\bigr)(x)\,y_{m}

for some
\displaystyle\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m-1}\in Q_{+}
. Moreover due to (
17
) and (
22
) we have

\textstyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}\ldots R^{m-1}_{(2)}\bigr)(x)\,y_{m}\in\bigoplus_{\kappa\preceq\mu+\mu_{g+m}^{\prime}}C(\kappa).

It follows from these two facts that
\displaystyle j_{m}(x)\,j_{1}(y_{1})\ldots j_{g+n}(y_{g+n})\in\mathcal{G}_{1}^{S_{m}(\mu,\lambda)+([\mu^{\prime}],[\lambda^{\prime}])}
by definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{1}
. If now
\displaystyle x
is an element in
\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{1,0})_{\mu,\omega,\lambda}
,
\displaystyle j_{m}(x)\in(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})_{S_{m}(\mu,\omega,\lambda)}
for any
\displaystyle m\in\{1,\ldots,g\}
, and we have an analogous proof. These two particular cases imply the result.
∎

Let us identify
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
as vector spaces; namely
\displaystyle x\in(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})_{[\mu],[\lambda]}
is identified with the coset
\displaystyle x+\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\prec_{1}([\mu],[\lambda])}\in\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
. Denote by
\displaystyle\bullet
the resulting product on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
. Consider the canonical projections

\pi_{\eta}:\bigoplus_{\kappa\preceq\eta}C(\kappa)\to C(\eta),\quad\pi_{(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}:\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa\preceq\eta\\
\kappa^{\prime}\preceq\eta^{\prime}\end{subarray}}C(\kappa)\otimes C(\kappa^{\prime})\to C(\eta)\otimes C(\eta^{\prime})

which allow us to define the truncated product
\displaystyle\overline{\,\cdot\,}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
:

x\,\overline{\,\cdot\,}\,y=\pi_{\mu+\mu^{\prime}}(xy)\qquad(\text{resp. }x\,\overline{\,\cdot\,}\,y=\pi_{(\mu+\mu^{\prime},\omega+\omega^{\prime})}(xy))

for
\displaystyle x\in C(\mu)
and
\displaystyle y\in C(\mu^{\prime})
(resp.
\displaystyle x\in C(\mu)\otimes C(\omega)
and
\displaystyle y\in C(\mu^{\prime})\otimes C(\omega^{\prime})
). We denote by
\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{0,1},\overline{\,\cdot\,})
and
\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{1,0},\overline{\,\cdot\,})
the vector spaces
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
endowed with the products
\displaystyle\overline{\,\cdot\,}
above. The next lemma describes
\displaystyle\bullet
thanks to the embeddings of vector spaces
\displaystyle j_{k}
from (
58
).

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
\displaystyle k,l\in\{1,\ldots,g+n\}
,
\displaystyle\mu,\omega,\mu^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}\in P_{+}
and
\displaystyle\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in P
. Take
\displaystyle x
in
\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{1,0})_{\mu,\omega,\lambda}
or
\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})_{\mu,\lambda}
, depending if
\displaystyle k
is
\displaystyle\leq g
or
\displaystyle>g
. Similarly, take
\displaystyle y
in
\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{1,0})_{\mu^{\prime},\omega^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}
or
\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})_{\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}
, depending if
\displaystyle l
is
\displaystyle\leq g
or
\displaystyle>g
. Then

\displaystyle\displaystyle j_{k}(x)\bullet j_{k}(y)=j_{k}(x\,\overline{\,\cdot\,}\,y)\quad\text{ for all }k,

\displaystyle\displaystyle j_{k}(x)\bullet j_{l}(y)=j_{k}(x)j_{l}(y)\quad\text{if }k<l,

\displaystyle\displaystyle j_{k}(x)\bullet j_{l}(y)=q^{(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})}j_{l}(y)j_{k}(x)\quad\text{if }k>l.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle\simeq
be the identification
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})\simeq\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
. Let us prove the first formula. Take for instance
\displaystyle k>g
. By assumption
\displaystyle j_{k}(x)\in(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})_{S_{k}(\mu,\lambda)}
and
\displaystyle j_{k}(y)\in(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})_{S_{k}(\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})}
. Then
\displaystyle\textstyle xy\in\bigoplus_{\kappa\preceq\mu+\mu^{\prime}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})_{\kappa,\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}}
and we have

j_{k}(x)\bullet j_{k}(y)\simeq\left(j_{k}(x)+\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\prec_{1}S_{k}(\mu,\lambda)}\right)\left(j_{k}(y)+\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\prec_{1}S_{k}(\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})}\right)=j_{k}(xy)+\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\prec_{1}S_{k}(\mu+\mu^{\prime},\lambda+\lambda^{\prime})}\simeq j_{k}(x\,\overline{\,\cdot\,}\,y).

The second formula is obtained similarly. For the third formula, recall the expression of
\displaystyle R
in (
14
) and its commutations rules with
\displaystyle K_{\nu}
in (
15
). Then from (
57
) and the definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{1}
we get

\displaystyle\displaystyle j_{k}(x)\bullet j_{l}(y)\simeq\left(j_{k}(x)+\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\prec_{1}S_{k}(\mu,\lambda)}\right)\left(j_{l}(y)+\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\prec_{1}S_{l}(\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})}\right)=j_{k}(x)j_{l}(y)+\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\prec_{1}S_{k}(\mu,\lambda)+S_{l}(\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>\,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R)}j_{l}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R_{(1)})(y)\bigr)j_{k}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R_{(2)})(x)\bigr)+\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\prec_{1}S_{k}(\mu,\lambda)+S_{l}(\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>\,

\displaystyle\displaystyle j_{l}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(\Theta_{(1)})(y)\bigr)j_{k}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(\Theta_{(2)})(x)\bigr)+\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\prec_{1}S_{k}(\mu,\lambda)+S_{l}(\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>\,

\displaystyle\displaystyle q^{(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})}j_{l}(y)j_{k}(x)+\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\prec_{1}S_{k}(\mu,\lambda)+S_{l}(\mu^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})}\simeq q^{(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})}j_{l}(y)j_{k}(x).\qed

Recall from §
3.4
the filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
indexed by
\displaystyle\Lambda
(where
\displaystyle\Lambda
is defined in (
43
)) and the filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
indexed by
\displaystyle\Lambda\times\Lambda
. Since we identified
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
with
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}^{\otimes g}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\otimes n}
as a vector space, we can consider the family of subspaces
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{2}
indexed by
\displaystyle\Lambda^{2g+n}
and defined by

\mathcal{G}_{2}^{\mu_{1},\lambda_{1},\eta_{1},\kappa_{1},\ldots,\mu_{g},\lambda_{g},\eta_{g},\kappa_{g},\mu_{g+1},\lambda_{g+1},\ldots,\mu_{g+n},\lambda_{g+n}}\\
=\mathcal{F}^{\mu_{1},\lambda_{1},\eta_{1},\kappa_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\mu_{g},\lambda_{g},\eta_{g},\kappa_{g}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\ell}^{\mu_{g+1},\lambda_{g+1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\ell}^{\mu_{g+n},\lambda_{g+n}}.

We endow
\displaystyle P^{4g+2n}
with the partial order
\displaystyle\preceq_{2}
given by

(p_{1},\ldots,p_{4g+2n})\preceq_{2}(p^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,p^{\prime}_{4g+2n})\quad\iff\quad\forall\,i,\>p_{i}\preceq p^{\prime}_{i}

which gives by restriction a partial order on
\displaystyle\Lambda^{2g+n}
which satisfies (
4
) and is well-founded. Then
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{2}
becomes a filtration of the vector space
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
.

Lemma 4.10
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{2}
is a filtration of the algebra
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
.

Proof.

Take
\displaystyle j_{1}(x_{1})\ldots j_{g+n}(x_{g+n})\in\mathcal{G}_{2}^{S}
and
\displaystyle j_{1}(y_{1})\ldots j_{g+n}(y_{g+n})\in\mathcal{G}_{2}^{S^{\prime}}
for some
\displaystyle S,S^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{2g+n}
, where
\displaystyle j_{k}
is the embedding of vector spaces (
58
). We can assume that these elements are homogeneous in
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
. Then according to Lemma
4.9
:

\bigl(j_{1}(x_{1})\ldots j_{g+n}(x_{g+n})\bigr)\bullet\bigl(j_{1}(y_{1})\ldots j_{g+n}(y_{g+n})\bigr)=q^{N}j_{1}(x_{1}\,\overline{\,\cdot\,}\,y_{1})\ldots j_{g+n}(x_{g+n}\,\overline{\,\cdot\,}\,y_{g+n})

for some
\displaystyle N\in(1/D)\mathbb{Z}
. It is easy to see that
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
are also filtrations of
\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{1,0},\overline{\,\cdot\,})
and
\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{0,1},\overline{\,\cdot\,})
respectively, which gives the result.
∎

For
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq g
and
\displaystyle g+1\leq l\leq g+n
we will use the obvious embeddings

\mathbf{S}_{k}:\Lambda^{2}\to(\Lambda^{2})^{g}\times\Lambda^{n},\qquad\mathbf{S}_{l}:\Lambda\to(\Lambda^{2})^{g}\times\Lambda^{n}.

Theorem 4.11
.

The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is Noetherian.

Proof.

We are going to show that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\bigl(\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})\bigr)
is Noetherian. Recall that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
is identified with
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
endowed with the product
\displaystyle\bullet
. To avoid confusion we do not identify again
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\bigl(\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})\bigr)
with
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
as a vector space and instead we use cosets
\displaystyle x+\mathcal{G}_{2}^{\prec_{2}S}
.

Note first that thanks to the first formula in Lemma
4.9
the linear maps
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{B(k)},\mathfrak{i}_{A(k)},\mathfrak{i}_{M(s)}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
from (
59
) are morphisms of algebras

\overline{\mathfrak{i}}_{B(k)},\overline{\mathfrak{i}}_{A(k)},\overline{\mathfrak{i}}_{M(s)}:\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{0,1},\overline{\,\cdot\,}\,\bigr)\to\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}).

It is easy to see that the filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{x}
(
\displaystyle x\in\{\ell,r\}
) of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
is also a filtration of
\displaystyle\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{0,1},\overline{\,\cdot\,}\,\bigr)
and that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{0,1},\overline{\,\cdot\,}\,\bigr)=\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})
. If
\displaystyle\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
are such that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{B}(\varphi)\in\mathcal{F}^{\mu,\lambda,0,0}
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{A}(\psi)\in\mathcal{F}^{0,0,\eta,\kappa}
we let

\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{B(k)}(\varphi)=\overline{\mathfrak{i}}_{B(k)}(\varphi)+\mathcal{G}_{2}^{\prec_{2}\mathbf{S}_{k}(\mu,\lambda,0,0)},\qquad\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{A(k)}(\psi)=\overline{\mathfrak{i}}_{A(k)}(\psi)+\mathcal{G}_{2}^{\prec_{2}\mathbf{S}_{k}(0,0,\eta,\kappa)}

for
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq g
. Similarly, for
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{F}^{\mu,\lambda}_{\ell}\subset\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
and
\displaystyle g+1\leq s\leq g+n
we let

\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{M(s)}(\varphi)=\overline{\mathfrak{i}}_{M(s)}(\varphi)+\mathcal{G}_{2}^{\prec_{2}\mathbf{S}_{s}(\mu,\lambda)}.

Since
\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{i}}_{B(k)},\overline{\mathfrak{i}}_{A(k)},\overline{\mathfrak{i}}_{M(s)}
are morphisms of algebras and

\overline{\mathfrak{i}}_{B(k)}\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{r}^{\prec\mu,\lambda}\bigr)=\mathcal{G}_{2}^{\prec_{2}\mathbf{S}_{k}(\mu,\lambda,0,0)},\quad\overline{\mathfrak{i}}_{A(k)}\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{\ell}^{\prec\eta,\kappa}\bigr)=\mathcal{G}_{2}^{\prec_{2}\mathbf{S}_{k}(0,0,\eta,\kappa)},\quad\overline{\mathfrak{i}}_{M(s)}\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{\ell}^{\prec\mu,\lambda}\bigr)=\mathcal{G}_{2}^{\prec_{2}\mathbf{S}_{s}(\mu,\lambda)}

we obtain morphisms of algebras

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{B(k)}:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\>\,\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{r}}\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{0,1},\overline{\,\cdot\,}\,\bigr)=\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{r}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})\to\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\bigl(\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})\bigr),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{A(k)},\>\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{M(s)}:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\>\,\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{\ell}}\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{0,1},\overline{\,\cdot\,}\,\bigr)=\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{\ell}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})\to\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\bigl(\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})\bigr).

Recall from §
3.4
that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{x}}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})
with
\displaystyle x\in\{\ell,r\}
is generated by a finite number of matrix coefficients
\displaystyle u_{1},\ldots,u_{p}
. Thanks to the second formula in Lemma
4.9
, we deduce that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\bigl(\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})\bigr)
is generated by the finite set of elements
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{B(k)}(u_{r})
,
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{A(k)}(u_{r})
,
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{M(s)}(u_{r})
for
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq g
,
\displaystyle g+1\leq s\leq g+n
and
\displaystyle 1\leq r\leq p
. By applying the morphism
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{X(r)}
where
\displaystyle X(r)
is indifferently
\displaystyle A(r)
,
\displaystyle B(r)
or
\displaystyle M(r)
to (
47
), we obtain

\forall\,1\leq b<a\leq p,\quad\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{X(r)}(u_{a})\,\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{X(r)}(u_{b})=q_{ab}\,\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{X(r)}(u_{b})\,\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{X(r)}(u_{a})+\sum_{s=1}^{b-1}\sum_{t=1}^{p}\alpha^{ab}_{st}\,\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{X(r)}(u_{s})\,\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{X(r)}(u_{t})

with certain scalars
\displaystyle q_{ab}\in\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})^{\times}
and
\displaystyle\alpha^{ab}_{st}\in\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
. Similarly, it follows from (
52
) that

\forall\,1\leq b<a\leq p,\quad\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{A(r)}(u_{a})\,\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{B(r)}(u_{b})=q^{\prime}_{ab}\,\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{B(r)}(u_{b})\,\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{A(r)}(u_{a})+\sum_{s=1}^{b-1}\sum_{t=1}^{p}\lambda^{ab}_{st}\,\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{B(r)}(u_{s})\,\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{A(r)}(u_{t})

for any
\displaystyle 1\leq r\leq g
and with certain scalars
\displaystyle q^{\prime}_{ab}\in\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})^{\times}
and
\displaystyle\lambda^{ab}_{st}\in\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
.
Finally, we see from the third formula in Lemma
4.9
that

\forall\,1\leq a,b\leq p,\quad\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{Y(s)}(u_{a})\,\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{X(r)}(u_{b})=q^{\prime\prime}_{ab}\,\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{X(r)}(u_{b})\,\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{Y(s)}(u_{a})

where
\displaystyle r<s
and
\displaystyle X(r)
(resp.
\displaystyle Y(s)
) is indifferently
\displaystyle A(r)
,
\displaystyle B(r)
or
\displaystyle M(r)
(resp.
\displaystyle A(s)
,
\displaystyle B(s)
or
\displaystyle M(s)
) and
\displaystyle q^{\prime\prime}_{ab}\in\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})^{\times}
.

We define a sequence
\displaystyle(\widehat{x}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{x}_{(2g+n)p})
by

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widehat{x}_{1}=\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{B(1)}(u_{1}),\ldots,\widehat{x}_{p}=\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{B(1)}(u_{p}),\>\widehat{x}_{p+1}=\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{A(1)}(u_{1}),\ldots,\widehat{x}_{2p}=\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{A(1)}(u_{p}),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 15.00002pt\vdots\hskip 115.00017pt\vdots\hskip 115.00017pt\vdots

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widehat{x}_{2(g-1)p+1}=\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{B(g)}(u_{1}),\ldots,\widehat{x}_{2(g-1)p+p}=\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{B(g)}(u_{p}),\>\widehat{x}_{2(g-1)p+p+1}=\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{A(g)}(u_{1}),\ldots,\widehat{x}_{2gp}=\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{A(g)}(u_{p}),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widehat{x}_{2gp+1}=\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{M(g+1)}(u_{1}),\ldots,\widehat{x}_{(2g+1)p}=\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{M(g+1)}(u_{p}),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 15.00002pt\vdots\hskip 135.0002pt\vdots

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widehat{x}_{(2g+n-1)p+1}=\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{M(g+n)}(u_{1}),\ldots,\widehat{x}_{(2g+n)p}=\widehat{\mathfrak{i}}_{M(g+n)}(u_{p}).

The relations above show that the generators
\displaystyle\widehat{x}_{s}
satisfy the relations required by Lemma
2.2
. It follows that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\bigl(\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})\bigr)
is Noetherian. Using Lemma
2.1
two times, we obtain that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
is Noetherian and that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is Noetherian.
∎

Remark 4.12
.

One can wonder why we did not use the simpler filtration by the subspaces
\displaystyle\textstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{[\lambda]}=\cup_{[\mu]\in P^{2g+n}}\mathcal{G}_{1}^{[\mu],[\lambda]}
indexed by
\displaystyle P^{g+n}
with lexicographic order from the right, for which all the above proofs seem simpler since the product
\displaystyle\overline{\,\cdot\,}
does not appear in
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
. The problem is that the lexicographic order on
\displaystyle P^{g+n}
is not well-founded, hence Lemma
2.1
does not apply to
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
and the last argument in the proof of Theorem
4.11
becomes wrong.

4.4.
Noetherianity and finiteness of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}

The subalgebra of
\displaystyle U_{q}
-invariant elements is

\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}=\bigl\{x\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}\,\big|\,\forall\,h\in U_{q},\>\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)=\varepsilon(h)x\bigr\}

where the right action
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
was defined in (
53
). More generally if
\displaystyle V
is a module over a Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H
, the subspace of invariant elements is

V^{H}=\bigl\{v\in V\>|\>\forall\,h\in H,\>h\cdot v=\varepsilon(h)v\bigr\}

where
\displaystyle\varepsilon
is the counit of
\displaystyle H
.

We start with a general result about the structure of invariant elements in module-algebras; it is just an abstract formulation of the discussion below Theorem 3.2 in
[
BR21
]
, which is itself inspired by
[
DC70
, Chap. 3, §1]
. Denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{I}
an additive abelian monoid endowed with a partial order
\displaystyle\leq
such that

(64)

\begin{array}[]{l}\forall\,i\in\mathcal{I},\>\>0\leq i\\
\forall\,i,j,k\in\mathcal{I},\>\>i\leq j\implies i+k\leq j+k.\end{array}

We assume moreover that
\displaystyle\leq
is well-founded. For instance,
\displaystyle\mathcal{I}=P_{+}
endowed with the order
\displaystyle\preceq
(see §
2.3
) satisfies these assumptions.

Theorem 4.13

(Hilbert–Nagata theorem for module-algebras)
.

Let
\displaystyle H
be a Hopf algebra and let
\displaystyle\textstyle A=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathcal{I}}A_{i}
be a graded
\displaystyle k
-algebra which is a
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra. Assume that

•

\displaystyle A_{0}=k\,1
,

•

\displaystyle A
is Noetherian and finitely generated,

•

for each
\displaystyle i\in\mathcal{I}
,
\displaystyle A_{i}
is stable under the action of
\displaystyle H
and is a semisimple
\displaystyle H
-module.

Then the subalgebra
\displaystyle A^{H}
is Noetherian and finitely generated.

The proof resorts on three basic lemmas. Note that since
\displaystyle A_{i}
is semisimple we can write

\textstyle A_{i}=(A_{i})^{H}\oplus\bigoplus_{n}V_{i,n}

where the
\displaystyle V_{i,n}
are some simple
\displaystyle H
-modules not isomorphic to the trivial module
\displaystyle k
. Projecting along this decomposition gives a
\displaystyle H
-morphism
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{i}:A_{i}\to(A_{i})^{H}
. Since each
\displaystyle A_{i}
is
\displaystyle H
-stable we have
\displaystyle\textstyle A^{H}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(A_{i})^{H}
and we can define

\mathfrak{R}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathfrak{R}_{i}:A\to A^{H}.

The morphism
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}
is called the
Reynolds operator
.

Lemma 4.14
.

For
\displaystyle x\in A^{H}
and
\displaystyle y\in A
, it holds
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(xy)=x\mathfrak{R}(y)
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(yx)=\mathfrak{R}(y)x
.

Proof.

We can assume without loss of generality that
\displaystyle x\in(A_{i})^{H}
and
\displaystyle y\in A_{j}
. Consider the map

\begin{array}[]{rcll}m_{x}:&A_{j}&\longrightarrow&A_{i+j}\\
&y&\longmapsto&xy\end{array}

It is
\displaystyle H
-linear:

h\cdot m_{x}(y)=h\cdot(xy)=\sum_{(h)}(h_{(1)}\cdot x)(h_{(2)}\cdot y)=\sum_{(h)}(\varepsilon(h_{(1)})x)(h_{(2)}\cdot y)=x(h\cdot y)=m_{x}(h\cdot y).

Let
\displaystyle A_{j}=(A_{j})^{H}\oplus V_{j}
and
\displaystyle A_{i+j}=(A_{i+j})^{H}\oplus V_{i+j}
, where the semisimple modules
\displaystyle V_{j}
and
\displaystyle V_{i+j}
do not have any direct summand isomorphic to the trivial module
\displaystyle k
. Then by Schur’s lemma

\mathrm{Hom}_{H}(A_{j},A_{i+j})=\mathrm{Hom}_{k}\bigl((A_{j})^{H},(A_{i+j})^{H}\bigr)\oplus\mathrm{Hom}_{H}(V_{j},V_{i+j}).

It follows that if we write
\displaystyle y=\mathfrak{R}(y)\oplus v\in(A_{j})^{H}\oplus V_{j}
, we have

xy=m_{x}(y)=m_{x}(\mathfrak{R}(y)\oplus v)=m_{x}\bigl(\mathfrak{R}(y)\bigr)\oplus m_{x}(v)=x\mathfrak{R}(y)\oplus xv\in(A_{i+j})^{H}\oplus V_{i+j}.

But by definition of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}
we also have
\displaystyle xy=\mathfrak{R}(xy)\oplus w
, which gives the desired equality. The second one is shown similarly.
∎

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
\displaystyle A
be a Noetherian algebra and
\displaystyle X\subset A
. There exists a finite subset
\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{fin}}\subset X
such that
\displaystyle AX=AX_{\mathrm{fin}}
, where
\displaystyle AY
denotes the left ideal generated by
\displaystyle Y\subset A
. The same is true for right ideals.

Proof.

Since
\displaystyle A
is Noetherian,
\displaystyle AX
is finitely generated. So there exists elements

g_{1}=\sum_{i\in I_{1}}a_{i}^{(1)}x_{i}^{(1)},\>\ldots,\>g_{n}=\sum_{i\in I_{n}}a_{i}^{(n)}x_{i}^{(n)}

(where
\displaystyle a_{i}^{(j)}\in A
,
\displaystyle x_{i}^{(j)}\in X
and
\displaystyle I_{j}
is finite for each
\displaystyle j
) such that
\displaystyle AX=Ag_{1}+\ldots+Ag_{n}
. Take
\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{fin}}=\{x_{i}^{(1)}\}_{i\in I_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup\{x_{i}^{(n)}\}_{i\in I_{n}}
. Since
\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{fin}}
is a (finite) subset of
\displaystyle X
, we have
\displaystyle AX_{\mathrm{fin}}\subset AX
. On the other hand, since
\displaystyle g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\in AX_{\mathrm{fin}}
, we have
\displaystyle AX\subset A(AX_{\mathrm{fin}})=AX_{\mathrm{fin}}
.
∎

The last lemma is a well-known fact:

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
\displaystyle\textstyle A=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathcal{I}}A_{i}
be a graded
\displaystyle k
-algebra such that
\displaystyle A_{0}=k\,1
. If
\displaystyle A
is Noetherian, then it is finitely generated.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle\textstyle A_{+}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathcal{I}\setminus\{0\}}A_{i}
. Since
\displaystyle A_{+}
is an ideal, it is finitely generated: there exists
\displaystyle g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}
such that
\displaystyle A_{+}=Ag_{1}+\ldots+Ag_{n}
. We can assume that the elements
\displaystyle g_{s}
are homogeneous (
i.e.

\displaystyle g_{s}\in A_{j_{s}}
with
\displaystyle j_{s}>0
). We will show that
\displaystyle A_{i}\subset k\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\rangle
by well-founded induction on
\displaystyle i
. Firstly,
\displaystyle A_{0}=k\,1\subset k\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\rangle
. Now assume that
\displaystyle\textstyle\bigoplus_{j<i}A_{j}\subset k\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\rangle
for some
\displaystyle i\in\mathcal{I}\setminus\{0\}
and take
\displaystyle x\in A_{i}
. Since
\displaystyle A_{i}\subset A_{+}
we have
\displaystyle\textstyle x=\sum_{s=1}^{n}a_{s}g_{s}
. The elements
\displaystyle x
and
\displaystyle g_{s}
being homogeneous, the
\displaystyle a_{s}
can be assumed to be homogeneous as well:
\displaystyle a_{s}\in A_{l_{s}}
. Then
\displaystyle j_{s}+l_{s}=i
for all
\displaystyle s
, which implies
\displaystyle l_{s}<i
due to the assumptions (
64
) on
\displaystyle\leq
. Hence
\displaystyle a_{s}\in\textstyle\bigoplus_{j<i}A_{j}
so that
\displaystyle a_{s}\in k\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\rangle
by the induction hypothesis. It follows that
\displaystyle x\in k\langle g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\rangle
, as desired.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.13
.

Let
\displaystyle I
be a left ideal in
\displaystyle A^{H}
; we want to show that it is finitely generated. Lemma
4.15
gives us elements
\displaystyle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in I
such that
\displaystyle AI=Ax_{1}+\ldots+Ax_{n}
. We have
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(AI)=I
. Indeed, the inclusion
\displaystyle I\subset\mathfrak{R}(AI)
is obvious since
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(I)=I
. Conversely, take
\displaystyle\textstyle x=\sum_{s=1}^{n}a_{s}x_{s}\in AI
. Applying Lemma
4.14
, we get
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathfrak{R}(x)=\sum_{s=1}^{n}\mathfrak{R}(a_{s})x_{s}\in I
, so that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(AI)\subset I
. Now, using Lemma
4.14
again, we find

I=\mathfrak{R}(AI)=\mathfrak{R}(Ax_{1}+\ldots+Ax_{n})=\mathfrak{R}(A)x_{1}+\ldots+\mathfrak{R}(A)x_{n}=A^{H}x_{1}+\ldots+A^{H}x_{n}

which means that
\displaystyle I
is generated by
\displaystyle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
. The same arguments show that right ideals of
\displaystyle A^{H}
are finitely generated as well. Thus
\displaystyle A^{H}
is Noetherian. To show that the algebra
\displaystyle A^{H}
is finitely generated, note that it is graded by
\displaystyle\mathcal{I}
if we define
\displaystyle(A^{H})_{i}=(A_{i})^{H}
; hence Lemma
4.16
applies.
∎

We will now use Theorem
4.13
to prove that the subalgebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{U_{q}}_{g,n}
is Noetherian and finitely generated, which is our main result in this section. Recall that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})^{\otimes(2g+n)}
as a vector space. For
\displaystyle[\mu]=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{2g+n})\in P_{+}^{2g+n}
let

C([\mu])=C(\mu_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes C(\mu_{2g+n})\subset\mathcal{L}_{g,n}

where
\displaystyle C(\mu_{i})
is the subspace of matrix coefficients of the irreducible
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
-module with highest weight
\displaystyle\mu_{i}
. We say that
\displaystyle[\mu^{\prime}]\preceq[\mu]
if and only if
\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}_{i}\preceq\mu_{i}
for all
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq 2g+n
, where we recall that
\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}_{i}\preceq\mu_{i}
means that
\displaystyle\mu_{i}-\mu^{\prime}_{i}\in D^{-1}Q_{+}
. This gives an order on
\displaystyle P_{+}^{2g+n}
which satisfies the conditions (
4
) and (
64
) and is well-founded. We see from the formulas in Proposition
4.4
and (
22
) that the subspaces

\mathcal{W}^{[\mu]}=\bigoplus_{[\mu^{\prime}]\preceq[\mu]}C([\mu^{\prime}])

form a filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{W}
of the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
indexed by
\displaystyle P_{+}^{2g+n}
. Let

(65)

\textstyle\pi_{[\mu]}:\bigoplus_{[\mu^{\prime}]\preceq[\mu]}C([\mu^{\prime}])\to C([\mu])

be the canonical projection and put
\displaystyle x\,\overline{\,\cdot\,}\,y=\pi_{[\mu]+[\eta]}(xy)
for
\displaystyle x\in C([\mu])
,
\displaystyle y\in C([\eta])
. Then
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
is identified with the vector space
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
endowed with the product
\displaystyle\overline{\,\cdot\,}
.

Theorem 4.17
.

The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}
is Noetherian and finitely generated.

Proof.

Recall the filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{1}
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
that we used in §
4.3
. We see that
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{1}
is a filtration of
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
as well and that

\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\bigl(\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})\bigr)=\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}).

In the proof of Theorem
4.11
we showed that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\bigl(\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})\bigr)
is Noetherian and finitely generated. It follows from Lemmas
2.1
and
2.3
that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
also has these properties. Using again these lemmas, we obtain that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
is Noetherian and finitely generated.

Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is a
\displaystyle U_{q}
-module-algebra and the canonical projections
\displaystyle\pi_{[\mu]}
in (
65
) are
\displaystyle U_{q}
-linear for
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
, we see that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})
is a
\displaystyle U_{q}
-module-algebra for
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
. It satisfies all the assumptions of Theorem
4.13
(recall that
\displaystyle U_{q}\text{-}\mathrm{mod}
is semisimple). Hence
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})^{U_{q}}
is Noetherian and finitely generated. To conclude, let

\mathcal{W}^{[\mu]}_{\mathrm{inv}}=\bigl(\mathcal{W}^{[\mu]}\bigr)^{U_{q}}=\mathcal{W}^{[\mu]}\cap\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}\subset\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}.

Then
\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{inv}}
is a filtration of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}
and we have

\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{L}_{g,n})^{U_{q}}=\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{inv}}}\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}\bigr).

The result then follows from Lemma
2.1
for Noetherianity and from Lemma
2.3
for finiteness.
∎

5.
The Alekseev morphism

Let
\displaystyle H
be a quasitriangular Hopf algebra with an invertible antipode
\displaystyle S
. Usually the Alekseev morphism
[
Ale94
]
is expected to be a morphism of algebras

\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\to\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
is the Heisenberg double (§
3.2
). However, without further assumptions on
\displaystyle H
(e.g. finite-dimensionality, as in
[
Fai20a
]
) we have to introduce a bigger algebra than
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
in order to make sense of the formulas given in
[
Ale94
]
; we call this algebra the two-sided Heisenberg double. We construct it in §
5.1
, and define the Alekseev morphism in §
5.2
. The case of finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
is discussed in §
5.3
. Then in §
5.4
we focus on the quantum group
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
and we use the Alekseev morphism to prove that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}\bigl(U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})\bigr)
does not have non-trivial zero divisors.

5.1.
The two-sided Heisenberg double

This section does not use the
\displaystyle R
-matrix of
\displaystyle H
. Recall that
\displaystyle H^{\mathrm{cop}}
is
\displaystyle H
with the opposite coproduct. The restricted dual
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
endowed with its usual product
\displaystyle\star
is a left
\displaystyle(H\otimes H^{\mathrm{cop}})
-module-algebra for the action

(x\otimes y)\to\varphi=x\rhd\varphi\lhd S^{-1}(y).

As a result we can make the following definition:

Definition 5.1
.

The two-sided Heisenberg double is the smash product
\displaystyle H^{\circ}\#(H\otimes H^{\mathrm{cop}})
. It is an associative algebra which we denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
.

By the general definition of a smash product (see e.g.
[
Mon93
, Def. 4.1.3]
),
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
is the vector space
\displaystyle H^{\circ}\otimes(H\otimes H^{\mathrm{cop}})
endowed with the multiplication

(66)

\begin{array}[]{rl}\bigl(\varphi\,\#\,(x\otimes y)\bigr)\bigl(\psi\,\#\,(z\otimes t)\bigr)&=\sum_{(x\,\otimes\,y)}\varphi\star\bigl((x\otimes y)_{(1)}\to\psi\bigr)\,\#\,(x\otimes y)_{(2)}(z\otimes t)\\[15.00002pt]
&=\sum_{(x),(y)}\varphi\star\bigl(x_{(1)}\rhd\psi\lhd S^{-1}(y_{(2)})\bigr)\,\#\,(x_{(2)}z\otimes y_{(1)}t).\end{array}

where we write
\displaystyle\varphi\,\#\,(x\otimes y)
for the element
\displaystyle\varphi\otimes(x\otimes y)\in H^{\circ}\otimes(H\otimes H)
. From this formula we see that
\displaystyle H^{\circ}\#\,(1\otimes 1)
,
\displaystyle\varepsilon\,\#\,(H\otimes 1)
and
\displaystyle\varepsilon\,\#\,(1\otimes H)
are subalgebras of
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
which are canonically isomorphic to
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
,
\displaystyle H
and
\displaystyle H
respectively. Moreover
\displaystyle H^{\circ}\#(H\otimes 1)
is a subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
which is canonically isomorphic to the Heisenberg double
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
from §
3.2
.

We note that
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
has a natural representation:

Proposition 5.2
.

There is a representation
\displaystyle\blacktriangleright
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
on
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
given by

\varphi\,\#\,(x\otimes y)\blacktriangleright\psi=\varphi\star\bigl(x\rhd\psi\lhd S^{-1}(y)\bigr).

Proof.

This is actually a general fact: for any left
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra
\displaystyle A
there is a representation
\displaystyle\blacktriangleright
of
\displaystyle A\,\#\,H
on
\displaystyle A
defined by
\displaystyle(a\,\#\,h)\blacktriangleright x=a(h\cdot x)
. The proof of this claim is straightforward.
∎

The representation of
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
on
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
(obtained by restricting
\displaystyle\blacktriangleright
to this subalgebra) is known to be faithful
[
Mon93
, Lem. 9.4.2]
. In general this is not true for the representation of
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
on
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
. For instance if
\displaystyle H
is finite-dimensional we have
\displaystyle H^{\circ}=H^{*}
and
\displaystyle\dim\bigl(\mathcal{HH}(H^{*})\bigr)=\dim(H)^{3}
while
\displaystyle\dim\bigl(\mathrm{End}_{k}(H^{*})\bigr)=\dim(H)^{2}
, which forces the representation morphism
\displaystyle\rho:\mathcal{HH}(H^{*})\to\mathrm{End}_{k}(H^{*})
to have a non-zero kernel.

Finally, let us mention another construction of
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
. We know from §
3.2
that
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
is a right
\displaystyle H
-module-algebra; it follows that
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
is a left
\displaystyle H^{\mathrm{cop}}
-module-algebra for the action

h\cdot(\varphi\,\#\,x)=(\varphi\,\#\,x)\cdot S^{-1}(h)

where the
\displaystyle\cdot
on the right-hand side is defined in (
36
). As a result we can consider the smash product
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\#H^{\mathrm{cop}}
and this algebra is isomorphic to
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
:

\begin{array}[]{ccll}\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\#H^{\mathrm{cop}}&\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}&\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})\\
(\varphi\,\#\,x)\,\#\,y&\longmapsto&\sum_{(y)}\varphi\,\#\,\bigl(xy_{(2)}\otimes S(y_{(1)})\bigr)\end{array}

5.2.
Definition of the Alekseev morphism

Recall that
\displaystyle H
is a quasitriangular Hopf algebra with an invertible antipode
\displaystyle S
. We use the following notations in
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
:

•

we write
\displaystyle\varphi
instead of
\displaystyle\varphi\,\#\,(1\otimes 1)
,

•

we write
\displaystyle h
instead of
\displaystyle\varepsilon\,\#\,(h\otimes 1)
,

•

we write
\displaystyle\widetilde{h}
instead of
\displaystyle\varepsilon\,\#\,(1\otimes h)
,

where
\displaystyle\varphi\in H^{\circ}
and
\displaystyle h\in H
. The multiplication in
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
is then described by

(67)

h\varphi=\sum_{(h)}(h_{(1)}\rhd\varphi)\,h_{(2)},\qquad\widetilde{h}\varphi=\sum_{(h)}\bigl(\varphi\lhd S^{-1}(h_{(2)})\bigr)\,\widetilde{h_{(1)}},\qquad\widetilde{h}g=g\widetilde{h}

for all
\displaystyle\varphi\in H^{\circ}
and
\displaystyle h,g\in H
. In particular the Heisenberg double
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
is identified with the subalgebra of elements of the form
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i}\varphi_{i}\,h_{i}
. We note also that by the very definition
\displaystyle\widetilde{gh}=\widetilde{g}\,\widetilde{h}
. Finally, we endow
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}
with the usual multiplication on a tensor product of algebras.

We will use the embeddings
\displaystyle j_{k}:\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
for
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq g
and
\displaystyle j_{g+l}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
for
\displaystyle 1\leq l\leq n
from (
58
) and the morphisms
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to H
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}:\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\to\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\subset\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
from (
37
) and Proposition
3.7
. Let

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathsf{D}_{g,n}:&H&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}\\
&h&\longmapsto&\sum_{(h)}\widetilde{h_{(1)}}\,h_{(2)}\otimes\ldots\otimes\widetilde{h_{(2g-1)}}\,h_{(2g)}\otimes h_{(2g+1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes h_{(2g+n)}\end{array}

which is a morphism of algebras. We use the convention
\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{0,0}(h)=\varepsilon(h)
. Note for further use that

(68)

\mathsf{D}_{0,n+1}(h)=\sum_{(h)}h_{(1)}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n}(h_{(2)}),\qquad\mathsf{D}_{g+1,n}(h)=\sum_{(h)}\widetilde{h_{(1)}}\,h_{(2)}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{g,n}(h_{(3)}).

Theorem 5.3
.

There is a morphism of algebras

\Phi_{g,n}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\to\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}

defined by

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(j_{g+l}(\varphi)\bigr)=\sum_{(R)}1^{\otimes g}\,\otimes 1^{\otimes(l-1)}\otimes\Phi_{0,1}\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(R_{(1)}\bigr)(\varphi)\right)\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n-l}\bigl(R_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(j_{k}(x)\bigr)=\sum_{(R)}1^{\otimes(k-1)}\otimes\Phi_{1,0}\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(R_{(1)}\bigr)(x)\right)\otimes\mathsf{D}_{g-k,n}\bigl(R_{(2)}\bigr)

for any
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
,
\displaystyle 1\leq l\leq n
,
\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
,
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq g
, and where
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
is defined in (
23
) and (
27
).

By (
60
) the value of
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
on general elements is

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(x_{1}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\ldots\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,x_{g}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\varphi_{1}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\ldots\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\varphi_{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(j_{1}(x_{1})\bigr)\ldots\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(j_{g}(x_{g})\bigr)\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(j_{g+1}(\varphi_{1})\bigr)\ldots\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(j_{g+n}(\varphi_{n})\bigr).

Note that for
\displaystyle g>0
the morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
actually takes values in
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})\otimes\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes(g-1)}\otimes H^{\otimes n}
where
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
is identified with the subalgebra
\displaystyle H^{\circ}\,\#\,(H\otimes 1)
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
. This remark will be important in the proof of Theorem
5.8
in §
5.4
below.

The following lemma is the key point for the proof of Theorem
5.3
:

Lemma 5.4
.

For all
\displaystyle h\in H
and
\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
we have

\mathsf{D}_{g,n}(h)\,\Phi_{g,n}(x)=\sum_{(h)}\Phi_{g,n}\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(S^{-1}(h_{(2)})\bigr)(x)\right)\mathsf{D}_{g,n}(h_{(1)}).

Proof.

The proof is by induction. First we prove the result for
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n}
by induction on
\displaystyle n
. Then we fix
\displaystyle n
and we prove the result for
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
by induction on
\displaystyle g
. So let us consider the case
\displaystyle g=0
. Thanks to the relation
\displaystyle R\Delta=\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}R
we get for all
\displaystyle h\in H
and
\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)

(69)

\begin{array}[]{rl}h\,\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi)&=\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2})}\varphi\bigl(R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(2)}\bigr)\,hR^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}\\[13.99995pt]
&=\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2}),(h)}\varphi\!\left(S^{-1}(h_{(3)})h_{(2)}R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(2)}\right)\,h_{(1)}R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}\\[13.99995pt]
&=\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2}),(h)}\varphi\!\left(S^{-1}(h_{(3)})R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(2)}h_{(2)}\right)\,R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}h_{(1)}\\[13.99995pt]
&=\sum_{(h)}\Phi_{0,1}\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(S^{-1}(h_{(2)})\bigr)(\varphi)\right)h_{(1)}\end{array}

which is the desired formula for
\displaystyle(g,n)=(0,1)
. Now assume that the formula is true for some
\displaystyle n\geq 1
(still with
\displaystyle g=0
) and let
\displaystyle\varphi\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,x\in\mathcal{L}_{0,n+1}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{L}_{0,n}(H)
. We have in
\displaystyle H^{\otimes(n+1)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{0,n+1}(h)\Phi_{0,n+1}(\varphi\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,x)=\sum_{(R),(h)}h_{(1)}\Phi_{0,1}\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(R_{(1)}\bigr)(\varphi)\right)\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n}\bigl(h_{(2)}R_{(2)}\bigr)\Phi_{0,n}(x)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R),(h)}\Phi_{0,1}\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(R_{(1)}S^{-1}(h_{(2)})\bigr)(\varphi)\right)h_{(1)}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n}\bigl(h_{(3)}R_{(2)}\bigr)\Phi_{0,n}(x)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R),(h)}\Phi_{0,1}\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(S^{-1}(h_{(3)})R_{(1)}\bigr)(\varphi)\right)h_{(1)}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n}\bigl(R_{(2)}h_{(2)}\bigr)\Phi_{0,n}(x)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R),(h)}\Phi_{0,1}\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(S^{-1}(h_{(4)})R_{(1)}\bigr)(\varphi)\right)h_{(1)}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n}\bigl(R_{(2)}\bigr)\Phi_{0,n}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(S^{-1}(h_{(3)})\bigr)(x)\bigr)\mathsf{D}_{0,n}\bigl(h_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(h)}\Phi_{0,n+1}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(S^{-1}(h_{(2)})\bigr)(\varphi\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,x)\bigr)\mathsf{D}_{0,n+1}(h_{(1)}).

For the first equality we used (
68
), for the second we used (
69
), for the third we used that
\displaystyle(S\otimes\mathrm{id})(R)=R^{-1}
together with
\displaystyle R^{-1}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}=\Delta R^{-1}
, for the fourth we used the induction hypothesis and for the fifth we used (
68
), the definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n+1}
and the definition of
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
in (
53
). Now we treat the case
\displaystyle g>0
. Observe first that for all
\displaystyle h\in H
and
\displaystyle\beta\otimes\alpha\in\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
, we have in
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{1,0}(h)\Phi_{1,0}(\beta\otimes\alpha)=\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2}),(R^{3}),(h)}\widetilde{h_{(1)}}h_{(2)}\left(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(2)}\rhd\beta\lhd R^{3}_{(1)}R^{1}_{(1)}\right)R^{3}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2}),(R^{3}),(h)}\left(h_{(3)}R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(2)}\rhd\beta\lhd R^{3}_{(1)}R^{1}_{(1)}S^{-1}(h_{(2)})\right)h_{(4)}R^{3}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha)\widetilde{h_{(1)}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2}),(R^{3}),(h)}\left(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(2)}h_{(3)}\rhd\beta\lhd S^{-1}(h_{(4)})R^{3}_{(1)}R^{1}_{(1)}\right)R^{3}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}h_{(2)}\Phi_{0,1}(\alpha)\widetilde{h_{(1)}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(h)}\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(S^{-1}(h_{(3)})\bigr)(\beta)\otimes\varepsilon\bigr)\Phi_{0,1}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(S^{-1}(h_{(2)})\bigr)(\alpha)\bigr)\mathsf{D}_{1,0}(h_{(1)})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(h)}\Phi_{1,0}\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(S^{-1}(h_{(2)})\bigr)(\beta\otimes\alpha)\right)\mathsf{D}_{1,0}(h_{(1)})

which is the formula for the case
\displaystyle(g,n)=(1,0)
. The first equality is the definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
(Proposition
3.7
), the second equality uses (
67
), the third equality uses
\displaystyle R\Delta=\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}R
three times, the fourth equality uses (
69
), the definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
and the definition of
\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{1,0}
and the fifth equality uses the definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
and (
27
). For the general case, fix
\displaystyle n
and make an induction on
\displaystyle g
; this is completely similar to the computation for
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n+1}
above, using (
68
) and the result for
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
just proved.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.3
.

We will obtain the result by the same type of induction as in the proof of Lemma
5.4
,
i.e.
first one proves the result for
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n}
by induction on
\displaystyle n
and then one fixes
\displaystyle n
and proves the result for
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
by induction on
\displaystyle g
. Let us do for instance the second part, the first part being completely similar and left to the reader. So assume that
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
is a morphism of algebras and let
\displaystyle x\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,u,y\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,v\in\mathcal{L}_{g+1,n}(H)=\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
. We have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{g+1,n}\bigl((x\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,u)(y\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,v)\bigr)=\sum_{(R)}\Phi_{g+1,n}\bigl(x\,\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R_{(1)})(y)\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R_{(2)})(u)\,v\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2})}\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R^{2}_{(1)})\bigl(x\,\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R^{1}_{(1)})(y)\bigr)\bigr)\otimes\mathsf{D}_{g,n}(R^{2}_{(2)})\,\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R^{1}_{(2)})(u)\,v\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2}),(R^{3})}\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R^{2}_{(1)})(x)\bigr)\,\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R^{1}_{(1)}R^{3}_{(1)})(y)\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 177.00023pt\otimes\mathsf{D}_{g,n}(R^{2}_{(2)}R^{3}_{(2)})\,\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R^{1}_{(2)})(u)\bigr)\,\Phi_{g,n}(v)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(R^{1}),(R^{2}),\\
(R^{3}),(R^{4})\end{subarray}}\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R^{2}_{(1)})(x)\bigr)\,\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R^{1}_{(1)}R^{3}_{(1)}R^{4}_{(1)})(y)\bigr)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip 110.00017pt\otimes\mathsf{D}_{g,n}(R^{2}_{(2)})\,\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}S^{-1}(R^{3}_{(2)})\bigr)(u)\bigr)\,\mathsf{D}_{g,n}(R^{4}_{(2)})\,\Phi_{g,n}(v)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{2}),(R^{4})}\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R^{2}_{(1)})(x)\bigr)\,\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(R^{4}_{(1)})(y)\bigr)\otimes\mathsf{D}_{g,n}(R^{2}_{(2)})\,\Phi_{g,n}(u)\,\mathsf{D}_{g,n}(R^{4}_{(2)})\,\Phi_{g,n}(v)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{g+1,n}(x\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,u)\,\Phi_{g+1,n}(y\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,v).

The first equality uses (
54
), the second uses the definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{g+1,n}
, the third uses that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
is a module-algebra for
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
together with the formula
\displaystyle(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})(R)=R_{13}R_{23}
and then uses that
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
are morphisms of algebras (by Proposition
3.7
and the induction hypothesis), the fourth uses Lemma
5.4
and the formula
\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)(R)=R_{13}R_{12}
, the fifth uses
\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}\otimes S^{-1})(R)=R^{-1}
and the sixth uses the definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{g+1,n}
.
∎

For completeness let us provide an alternative definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
based on the matrices
\displaystyle\overset{V}{B}(i)
,
\displaystyle\overset{V}{A}(i)
,
\displaystyle\overset{V}{M}(j)
from (
61
). Let
\displaystyle R^{(+)}=R
and
\displaystyle\textstyle R^{(-)}=(R^{\prime})^{-1}=\sum_{(R)}R_{(2)}\otimes S(R_{(1)})
and write as usual
\displaystyle\textstyle R^{(\pm)}=\sum_{(R^{(\pm)})}R^{(\pm)}_{(1)}\otimes R^{(\pm)}_{(2)}
. Note that
\displaystyle R^{(-)}
is also a
\displaystyle R
-matrix. For a finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-module
\displaystyle V
we define

\displaystyle\displaystyle\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(\pm)}}=\sum_{(R^{(\pm)})}R^{(\pm)}_{(2)}\otimes\rho_{V}\!\left(R^{(\pm)}_{(1)}\right)\in H\otimes\mathrm{End}_{k}(V)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\overset{V}{\widetilde{L}}{{}^{(\pm)}}=\sum_{(R^{(\pm)})}\widetilde{R^{(\pm)}_{(2)}}\otimes\rho_{V}\!\left(R^{(\pm)}_{(1)}\right)\in\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})\otimes\mathrm{End}_{k}(V)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\overset{V}{T}=\sum_{i,j}{{}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}}\otimes E_{ij}\in H^{\circ}\otimes\mathrm{End}(V)

where
\displaystyle\rho_{V}:H\to\mathrm{End}_{k}(V)
is the representation morphism,
\displaystyle{{}_{V}\phi^{e^{i}}_{e_{j}}}:h\mapsto e^{i}(h\cdot e_{j})
are the matrix coefficients of
\displaystyle V
in some basis
\displaystyle(e_{i})
and
\displaystyle E_{ij}(e_{k})=\delta_{jk}e_{i}
. These elements can be seen as matrices of size
\displaystyle\dim(V)
with coefficients in
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
. They satisfy various exchange relations; for instance it is an exercise to show that (
67
) implies

\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(\pm)}_{1}}\overset{W}{T}_{2}=\overset{W}{T}_{2}\,\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(\pm)}_{1}}\,R^{(\pm)}_{VW},\quad R^{(\pm)}_{VW}\,\overset{V}{\widetilde{L}}{{}^{(\pm)}_{1}}\,\overset{W}{T}_{2}=\overset{W}{T}_{2}\,\overset{V}{\widetilde{L}}{{}^{(\pm)}_{1}},\quad\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(\pm)}_{1}}\,\overset{W}{\widetilde{L}}{{}^{(\pm)}_{2}}=\overset{W}{\widetilde{L}}{{}^{(\pm)}_{2}}\,\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(\pm)}_{1}}

where we use the notations introduced before Proposition
3.4
. Let
\displaystyle\overset{V}{M}
(resp.
\displaystyle\overset{V}{A}
,
\displaystyle\overset{V}{B}
) be the matrix with coefficients in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
(resp. in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
) introduced before Proposition
3.4
. We have

(70)

\Phi_{0,1}\bigl(\overset{V}{M}\bigr)=\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(+)}}\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(-)-1}},\quad\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\overset{V}{A}\bigr)=\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(+)}}\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(-)-1}},\quad\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\overset{V}{B}\bigr)=\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(+)}}\,\overset{V}{T}\,\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(-)-1}}

where
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}\bigl(\overset{V}{M}\bigr)
is viewed as a matrix with coefficients in
\displaystyle H
while
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\overset{V}{A}\bigr)
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}\bigl(\overset{V}{B}\bigr)
are viewed as matrices with coefficients in
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
. Finally for
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq g
let
\displaystyle J_{k}:\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})\to\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}
and for
\displaystyle g+1\leq l\leq g+n
let
\displaystyle J_{g+l}:H\to\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}
be the obvious embeddings. We define

\displaystyle\displaystyle\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(\pm)}}(i)=(J_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id})\bigl(\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(\pm)}}\bigr)\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq g+n,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\overset{V}{\widetilde{L}}{{}^{(\pm)}}(k)=(J_{k}\otimes\mathrm{id})\bigl(\overset{V}{\widetilde{L}}{{}^{(\pm)}}\bigr),\quad\overset{V}{T}(k)=(J_{k}\otimes\mathrm{id})\bigl(\overset{V}{T}\bigr)\quad\text{for }1\leq k\leq g.

A straightforward computation based on the axiom
\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)(R)=R_{13}R_{12}
reveals that

(71)

\sum_{(R)}\left(1^{\otimes g}\,\otimes 1^{\otimes l}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n-l}\bigl(R_{(2)}\bigr)\right)\otimes\rho_{V}\bigl(R_{(1)}\bigr)=\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(+)}}(g+n)\ldots\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(+)}}(g+l+1)

and similarly

(72)

\begin{array}[]{l}\sum_{(R)}\left(1^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathsf{D}_{g-k,n}\bigl(R_{(2)}\bigr)\right)\otimes\rho_{V}\bigl(R_{(1)}\bigr)\\
=\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(+)}}(g+n)\ldots\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(+)}}(g+1)\,\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(+)}}(g)\overset{V}{\widetilde{L}}{{}^{(+)}}(g)\ldots\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(+)}}(k+1)\overset{V}{\widetilde{L}}{{}^{(+)}}(k+1).\end{array}

Let us denote the matrices in (
71
) and (
72
) by
\displaystyle\Lambda_{0,n-l}
and
\displaystyle\Lambda_{g-k,n}
respectively, with the convention
\displaystyle\Lambda_{0,0}=1
. A computation which combines (
70
) with (
71
) and (
72
) yields

(73)

\begin{array}[]{l}\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(\overset{V}{M}(g+l)\bigr)=\Lambda_{0,n-l}\,\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(+)}}(g+l)\,\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(-)}}(g+l)^{-1}\Lambda_{0,n-l}^{-1},\\
\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(\overset{V}{A}(k)\bigr)=\Lambda_{g-k,n}\,\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(+)}}(k)\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(-)}}(k)^{-1}\,\Lambda_{g-k,n}^{-1},\\
\Phi_{g,n}\bigl(\overset{V}{B}(k)\bigr)=\Lambda_{g-k,n}\,\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(+)}}(k)\,\overset{V}{T}(k)\,\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(-)}}(k)^{-1}\,\Lambda_{g-k,n}^{-1}\end{array}

for
\displaystyle 1\leq l\leq n
and
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq g
. This is the matrix definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
.

Remark 5.5
.

Another possible definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
uses
\displaystyle\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(-)}}
,
\displaystyle\overset{V}{\widetilde{L}}{{}^{(-)}}
instead of
\displaystyle\overset{V}{L}{{}^{(+)}}
,
\displaystyle\overset{V}{\widetilde{L}}{{}^{(+)}}
to define different conjugation matrices
\displaystyle\Lambda
, where the conjugation starts from the first tensorand (here it starts from the last tensorand). See e.g.
[
Fai20a
, §3.3]
. For
\displaystyle g=0
our definition here agrees with the one in
[
BR22
, §6.2]
, as can be seen from the first equality in (
73
).

5.3.
Finite-dimensional case

Here we quickly discuss the case where
\displaystyle H
is finite-dimensional, which is treated in detail in
[
Fai20a
]
. In this case one can use
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
instead of
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
.

Recall from §
5.1
the representation of
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{*})
on
\displaystyle H^{*}
, which is faithful. Since
\displaystyle\dim\bigl(\mathcal{H}(H^{*})\bigr)=\dim\bigl(\mathrm{End}_{k}(H^{*})\bigr)
the representation morphism
\displaystyle\rho:\mathcal{H}(H^{*})\to\mathrm{End}_{k}(H^{*})
is an isomorphism. For
\displaystyle h\in H
let
\displaystyle Q_{h}\in\mathcal{H}(H^{*})
be defined by
\displaystyle\rho(Q_{h})(\psi)=\psi\lhd S^{-1}(h)
for all
\displaystyle\psi\in H^{*}
(be aware that
\displaystyle Q_{h}
was denoted by
\displaystyle\widetilde{h}
in
[
Fai20a
]
). We have
\displaystyle Q_{hg}=Q_{h}\,Q_{g}
so that
\displaystyle\{Q_{h}\,|\,h\in H\}
is a subalgebra of
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{*})
isomorphic to
\displaystyle H
. These elements obey the following commutation rules in
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{*})
:

(74)

Q_{h}\,\varphi=\sum_{(h)}\bigl(\varphi\lhd S^{-1}(h_{(2)})\bigr)\,Q_{h_{(1)}},\qquad Q_{h}\,g=g\,Q_{h}

for all
\displaystyle\varphi\in H^{*}
and
\displaystyle h,g\in H
.
Let

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{fin}}_{g,n}:&H&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{H}(H^{*})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}\\
&h&\longmapsto&\sum_{(h)}Q_{h_{(1)}}\,h_{(2)}\otimes\ldots\otimes Q_{h_{(2g-1)}}\,h_{(2g)}\otimes h_{(2g+1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes h_{(2g+n)}\end{array}.

which is a morphism of algebras and define

\Phi_{g,n}^{\mathrm{fin}}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\to\mathcal{H}(H^{*})^{\otimes g}\otimes H^{\otimes n}

by the formulas of Theorem
5.3
but with
\displaystyle\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{fin}}
instead of
\displaystyle\mathsf{D}
. Thanks to (
74
), the proof of Theorem
5.3
remains valid with the elements
\displaystyle Q_{h}
instead of
\displaystyle\widetilde{h}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}^{\mathrm{fin}}
is a morphism of algebras.

Hence we have two possible different morphisms in the finite-dimensional case:
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}^{\mathrm{fin}}
. They are related as follows. Let
\displaystyle r_{V_{i}}:H\to\mathrm{End}_{k}(V_{i})
be any representations of
\displaystyle H
for
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq n
and let
\displaystyle\rho:\mathcal{HH}(H^{*})\to\mathrm{End}_{k}(H^{*})
be the representation from Proposition
5.2
, which by restriction gives the isomorphism
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(H^{*})\cong\mathrm{End}_{k}(H^{*})
. Then the two representations of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl(\rho^{\otimes g}\otimes r_{V_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes r_{V_{n}}\bigr)\circ\Phi_{g,n}^{\mathrm{fin}}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\to\mathrm{End}_{k}\bigl((H^{*})^{\otimes g}\otimes V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n}\bigr),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigl(\rho^{\otimes g}\otimes r_{V_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes r_{V_{n}}\bigr)\circ\Phi_{g,n}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\to\mathrm{End}_{k}\bigl((H^{*})^{\otimes g}\otimes V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n}\bigr)

are
equal
, simply because
\displaystyle\rho(Q_{h})=\rho(\widetilde{h})
. This property is satisfying, as one of the main applications of
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
is to construct representations of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
.

When
\displaystyle H
is infinite dimensional we cannot define the elements
\displaystyle Q_{h}\in\mathcal{H}(H^{\circ})
, because we do not know if the representation morphism
\displaystyle\rho:\mathcal{H}(H^{*})\to\mathrm{End}_{k}(H^{\circ})
is surjective. This is why we introduced the bigger algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}(H^{\circ})
in which the elements
\displaystyle\widetilde{h}
exist by construction for all
\displaystyle h
and are a substitute for
\displaystyle Q_{h}
.

5.4.
The case
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})

The left and right coregular actions of
\displaystyle U_{q}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}
allow us to define the two-sided Heisenberg double
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}=\mathcal{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
as the smash product

\mathcal{H}_{q}=\mathcal{O}_{q}\,\#\,\bigl(U_{q}\otimes U_{q}^{\mathrm{cop}}\bigr).

where the scalars are implicitly extended to
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
. It is the
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
-vector space
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}\otimes\bigl(U_{q}\otimes U_{q}\bigr)
endowed with the product (
66
).

Let
\displaystyle G
be the connected, complex, semisimple algebraic group
\displaystyle G
with Lie algebra
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}
. Recall from §
2
that
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}=\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)
is the subalgebra of
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\circ}
generated over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
by the family of all matrix coefficients of the irreducible
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules of type
\displaystyle 1
.

Lemma 5.6
.

For all
\displaystyle k\geq 1
the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)^{\otimes k}
does not have non-trivial zero divisors.

Proof.

The direct product
\displaystyle G^{\times k}
is a connected, complex, semisimple algebraic group with Lie algebra
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\oplus k}
. By definition,
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(G^{\times k})
is generated over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
by the family of all matrix coefficients of the irreducible
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\oplus k})
-modules of type
\displaystyle 1
. Let us note that
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\oplus k})\cong U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})^{\otimes k}
as Hopf algebras. It is a general fact that given two algebras
\displaystyle A
and
\displaystyle B
the irreducible
\displaystyle(A\otimes B)
-modules are all of the form
\displaystyle V\otimes W
where
\displaystyle V
is an irreducible
\displaystyle A
-module and
\displaystyle W
is an irreducible
\displaystyle B
-module, see e.g.
[
E+11
, Th. 3.10.2]
. Hence we have an isomorphism of algebras

\begin{array}[]{ccll}\mathcal{O}_{q}(G^{\times k})&\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}&\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)^{\otimes k}\\
{}_{V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{k}}\phi^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}&\longmapsto&{}_{V_{1}}\phi^{i_{1}}_{j_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes{{}_{V_{k}}\phi^{i_{k}}_{j_{k}}}\end{array}

where each
\displaystyle V_{l}
is an irreducible
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
-module of type
\displaystyle 1
,
\displaystyle(v_{l,i})
is a basis of
\displaystyle V_{l}
with dual basis
\displaystyle(v^{l,j})
and the
\displaystyle\phi
’s are the matrix coefficients in these bases. By
[
BG02
, Th. I.8.9]
the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(G^{\times k})
has non-trivial zero divisors and we are done.
∎

Proposition 5.7
.

The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes U_{q}^{\otimes n}
does not have non-trivial zero divisors.

Proof.

We first introduce a filtration
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}_{q}
which straightforwardly generalizes the filtration of
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}
used in the proof of Proposition
3.10
. It is defined by

\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}=\mathcal{O}_{q}\,\#\,(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{\mathbf{m}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{\mathbf{n}})

for
\displaystyle(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in(\mathbb{N}^{2N+1})^{2}
. We endow the monoid
\displaystyle(\mathbb{N}^{2N+1})^{2}
with the partial order defined by
\displaystyle(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\leq(\mathbf{m}^{\prime},\mathbf{n}^{\prime})
if and only if
\displaystyle\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
and
\displaystyle\mathbf{n}\leq\mathbf{n}^{\prime}
, where
\displaystyle\leq
on
\displaystyle\mathbb{N}^{2N+1}
is the lexicographic order from the right (
7
). Thanks to formula (
66
) for the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}_{q}
and to Corollary
2.6
we see that
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}
is a filtration of
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}_{q}
. We have

\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{HH}_{q})=\mathcal{O}_{q}\,\#\,\bigl(\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}}(U_{q})\otimes\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}}(U_{q})\bigr).

Let us explain this equality more precisely. Let
{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}:x\mapsto v^{i}(x\cdot v_{j})
be the matrix coefficients (see §
2.1
) of a finite-dimensional
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}
-module
\displaystyle V
in a basis of weight vectors
\displaystyle(v_{i})
with weights
\displaystyle(\epsilon_{i})
. Write

{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j},\quad\overline{E_{\beta_{k}}},\quad\overline{F_{\beta_{k}}},\quad\overline{K_{\mu}},\quad\overline{\widetilde{E_{\beta_{k}}}},\quad\overline{\widetilde{F_{\beta_{k}}}},\quad\overline{\widetilde{K_{\mu}}}

instead of

\displaystyle{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}+\mathcal{G}^{<(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0})},\quad

\displaystyle\displaystyle E_{\beta_{k}}+\mathcal{G}^{<(d(E_{\beta_{k}}),\mathbf{0})},\quad F_{\beta_{k}}+\mathcal{G}^{<(d(F_{\beta_{k}}),\mathbf{0})},\quad K_{\mu}+\mathcal{G}^{<(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0})}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widetilde{E_{\beta_{k}}}+\mathcal{G}^{<(\mathbf{0},d(E_{\beta_{k}}))},\quad\widetilde{F_{\beta_{k}}}+\mathcal{G}^{<(\mathbf{0},d(F_{\beta_{k}}))},\quad\widetilde{K_{\mu}}+\mathcal{G}^{<(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0})}

in
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{HH}_{q})
. Note that
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{<(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0})}=0
. The subalgebra generated by
\displaystyle\overline{E_{\beta_{k}}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{F_{\beta_{k}}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{K_{\mu}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{\widetilde{E_{\beta_{k}}}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{\widetilde{F_{\beta_{k}}}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{\widetilde{K_{\mu}}}
for
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq N
and
\displaystyle\mu\in P
is isomorphic to
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}}(U_{q})^{\otimes 2}
and is thus a quasi-polynomial ring (over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
). Moreover by Proposition
2.5
and (
67
) we get

(75)

\begin{array}[]{lll}\overline{E_{\beta_{k}}}\,{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}={{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}\,\overline{E_{\beta_{k}}},&\overline{F_{\beta_{k}}}\,{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}=q^{-(\beta_{k},\epsilon_{j})}{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}\,\overline{F_{\beta_{k}}},&\overline{K_{\mu}}\,{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}=q^{(\mu,\epsilon_{j})}{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}\,\overline{K_{\nu}},\\[5.0pt]
\overline{\widetilde{E_{\beta_{k}}}}\,{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}=q^{-(\beta_{k},\epsilon_{i})}{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}\,\overline{\widetilde{E_{\beta_{k}}}},&\overline{\widetilde{F_{\beta_{k}}}}\,{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}={{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}\,\overline{\widetilde{F_{\beta_{k}}}},&\overline{K_{\mu}}\,{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}=q^{-(\mu,\epsilon_{i})}{{}_{V}\phi^{i}_{j}}\,\overline{K_{\nu}}\end{array}

for all
\displaystyle\mu\in P
. Hence
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{HH}_{q})
is a quasi-polynomial ring over
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})
, generated over
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})
by
\displaystyle\overline{E_{\beta_{k}}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{F_{\beta_{k}}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{K_{\mu}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{\widetilde{E_{\beta_{k}}}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{\widetilde{F_{\beta_{k}}}}
,
\displaystyle\overline{\widetilde{K_{\mu}}}
for
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq N
and
\displaystyle\mu\in P
.

Now for
\displaystyle(\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{m}_{g},\mathbf{n}_{g},\mathbf{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{n})\in(\mathbb{N}^{2N+1})^{2g+n}
let

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{m}_{g},\mathbf{n}_{g},\mathbf{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{n}}=\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{m}_{g},\mathbf{n}_{g}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{\mathbf{p}_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}^{\mathbf{p}_{n}}\subset\mathcal{HH}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes U_{q}^{\otimes n}.

We endow the monoid
\displaystyle(\mathbb{N}^{2N+1})^{2g+n}
with the direct product order:

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{m}_{g},\mathbf{n}_{g},\mathbf{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{n})\leq(\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{1},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{g},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{g},\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{n})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\iff\forall\,i,j,\quad\mathbf{m}_{i}\leq\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{i},\quad\mathbf{n}_{i}\leq\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{i},\quad\mathbf{p}_{j}\leq\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{j}.

It is clear that
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}
is a filtration of
\displaystyle\mathcal{HH}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes U_{q}^{\otimes n}
and that

\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{T}}\bigl(\mathcal{HH}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes U_{q}^{\otimes n}\bigr)=\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{HH}_{q})^{\otimes g}\otimes\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}}(U_{q})^{\otimes n}.

We have seen that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{HH}_{q})
is a quasipolynomial ring over
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})
and we know that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{DCK}}}(U_{q})
is a quasipolynomial ring over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q)
(§
2.4
). Hence
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{T}}\bigl(\mathcal{HH}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes U_{q}^{\otimes n}\bigr)
is a quasipolynomial ring over
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})^{\otimes g}
. Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}(q^{1/D})^{\otimes g}
does not have non-trivial zero divisors by Lemma
5.6
, it follows from a general result (see e.g.
[
MCR01
, §1.2.9]
) that
\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{HH}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes U_{q}^{\otimes n})
does not have non-trivial zero divisors. The conclusion then follows from Lemma
2.4
.
∎

Theorem 5.8
.

1. The morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}\to\mathcal{HH}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes U_{q}^{\otimes n}
is injective.

2. The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
does not have non-trivial zero divisors.

Proof.

1. The proof is by induction. First we prove the result for
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n}
by induction on
\displaystyle n
. Then we fix
\displaystyle n
and we prove the result for
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
by induction on
\displaystyle g
.

It has been shown in
[
Bau00
, Th. 3]
that the morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
is injective (see
[
BR22
, Th 4.3]
for this statement in the present framework). Now assume that
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n}
is injective for some
\displaystyle n
. Let
\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}^{r}
be the right adjoint action of
\displaystyle U_{q}
on itself:
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{ad}^{r}(h)(x)=\sum_{(h)}S(h_{(1)})xh_{(2)}
. For
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
consider the subspaces

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\bigl\{\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1}\,\big|\,\forall\,\nu\in P,\>\>\mathrm{coad}^{r}(K_{\nu})(\varphi)=q^{(\lambda,\nu)}\varphi\bigr\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle(U_{q})_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\bigl\{x\in U_{q}\,\big|\,\forall\,\nu\in P,\>\>\mathrm{ad}^{r}(K_{\nu})(x)=q^{(\lambda,\nu)}x\bigr\}.

We have
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P}(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})_{\lambda}
and
\displaystyle\textstyle U_{q}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P}(U_{q})_{\lambda}
, thus

\mathcal{L}_{0,n+1}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P}\,(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})_{\lambda}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{L}_{0,n},\qquad U_{q}^{\otimes(n+1)}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P}\,(U_{q})_{\lambda}\otimes(U_{q})^{\otimes n}

where for the first equality we used that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,n+1}=\mathcal{L}_{0,1}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{L}_{0,n}
(Prop.
4.3
). Since

\forall\,h\in U_{q},\>\forall\,\psi\in\mathcal{L}_{0,1},\quad\Phi_{0,1}\bigl(\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(\psi)\bigr)=\mathrm{ad}^{r}(h)\bigl(\Phi_{0,1}(\psi)\bigr)

we have
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}\bigl((\mathcal{L}_{0,1})_{\lambda}\bigr)\subset(U_{q})_{\lambda}
. Take
\displaystyle\varphi\in(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})_{\lambda}
and
\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{L}_{0,n}
. By (
12
) we have

\mathrm{coad}^{r}(\Theta_{(1)})(\varphi)\otimes\Theta_{(2)}=\varphi\otimes K_{\lambda}.

It follows from (
15
) and the expression of
\displaystyle R
in (
14
) that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n+1}(\varphi\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,x)
