\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{(R)}\Phi_{0,1}\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(R_{(1)}\bigr)(\varphi)\right)\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n}\bigl(R_{(2)}\bigr)\Phi_{0,n}(x)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\in\Phi_{0,1}\!\left(\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(\Theta_{(1)}\bigr)(\varphi)\right)\otimes\mathsf{D}_{0,n}\bigl(\Theta_{(2)}\bigr)\Phi_{0,n}(x)+\bigoplus_{\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda}\,(U_{q})_{\lambda^{\prime}}\otimes(U_{q})^{\otimes n}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi)\otimes(K_{\lambda})^{\otimes n}\Phi_{0,n}(x)+\bigoplus_{\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda}\,(U_{q})_{\lambda^{\prime}}\otimes(U_{q})^{\otimes n}

where
\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda
means that
\displaystyle\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}\in Q_{+}\!\setminus\!\{0\}
and we used that
\displaystyle K_{\lambda}
is grouplike for the last equality. We are now in position to show our result. Let
\displaystyle y\in\mathcal{L}_{0,n+1}
be a non-zero element and write it as
\displaystyle\textstyle y=\sum_{\lambda\in P}\sum_{i\in I_{\lambda}}\varphi_{\lambda,i}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,x_{\lambda,i}
with
\displaystyle\varphi_{\lambda,i}\in(\mathcal{L}_{0,1})_{\lambda}
for all
\displaystyle i\in I_{\lambda}
and such that for each
\displaystyle\lambda
the elements
\displaystyle\bigl(\varphi_{\lambda,i}\bigr)_{i\in I_{\lambda}}
are linearly independent over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})
. Take a
\displaystyle\lambda
maximal for the order
\displaystyle\leq
on
\displaystyle P
such that there exists at least one
\displaystyle i\in I_{\lambda}
with
\displaystyle\varphi_{\lambda,i}\neq 0
and
\displaystyle x_{\lambda,i}\neq 0
. Then

\Phi_{0,n+1}(y)\in\sum_{i\in I_{\lambda}}\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{\lambda,i})\otimes(K_{\lambda})^{\otimes n}\Phi_{0,n}(x_{\lambda,i})+\bigoplus_{\lambda^{\prime}\neq\lambda}\,(U_{q})_{\lambda}\otimes(U_{q})^{\otimes n}

By the induction hypothesis the morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n}
is injective so
\displaystyle(K_{\lambda})^{\otimes n}\Phi_{0,n}(x_{\lambda,i})\neq 0
(
\displaystyle K_{\lambda}
being invertible) for at least one
\displaystyle i\in I_{\lambda}
. Moreover the elements
\displaystyle\varphi_{\lambda,i}
are linearly independent so the elements
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{\lambda,i})
are linearly independent as well and it follows that
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n+1}(y)\neq 0
.

Let us now examine the case of
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
. Recall first from Theorem
3.11
that
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}
is injective. We see easily from the formulas in Theorem
5.3
that
\displaystyle\Phi_{g,n}
actually takes values in
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{HH}_{q}^{\otimes(g-1)}\otimes U_{q}^{\otimes n}
. Recall the right action (
36
) of
\displaystyle U_{q}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{q}
and consider the subspaces

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{1,0})_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\bigl\{x\in\mathcal{L}_{1,0}\,\big|\,\forall\,\nu\in P,\>\>\mathrm{coad}^{r}(K_{\nu})(x)=q^{(\lambda,\nu)}x\bigr\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\mathcal{H}_{q})_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\bigl\{y\in\mathcal{H}_{q}\,\big|\,\forall\,\nu\in P,\>y\cdot K_{\nu}=q^{(\lambda,\nu)}y\bigr\}.

One sees easily that
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathcal{H}_{q}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P}(\mathcal{H}_{q})_{\lambda}
and we obtain the decompositions

\mathcal{L}_{g+1,n}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P}\,(\mathcal{L}_{1,0})_{\lambda}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{L}_{g,n},\quad\mathcal{H}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{HH}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes U_{q}^{\otimes n}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P}\,(\mathcal{H}_{q})_{\lambda}\otimes\mathcal{HH}_{q}^{\otimes g}\otimes U_{q}^{\otimes n}.

Moreover by Proposition
3.7
we have
\displaystyle\Phi_{1,0}\bigl((\mathcal{L}_{1,0})_{\lambda}\bigr)\subset(\mathcal{H}_{q})_{\lambda}
. After these preliminary remarks the proof (by induction) of the injectivity of
\displaystyle\Phi_{g+1,n}
is completely similar to the proof for
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,n+1}
above and is thus left to the reader.

2. Follows from Proposition
5.7
and item 1.
∎

6.
Topological interpretation of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)

Let
\displaystyle H
be a ribbon Hopf algebra with an invertible antipode. In this last section we show that the algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is isomorphic to the stated skein algebra of a surface as defined in
[
CKL
]
. If moreover
\displaystyle H\text{-}\mathrm{mod}
is semisimple we show that the subalgebra of invariant elements
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
is isomorphic to the skein algebra of the surface. Finally we explain why this last property is false when
\displaystyle H
-mod is not semisimple.

6.1.
Stated skein algebras of surfaces

In this subsection we recall the definitions of the topological objects that we will use. For details, see
[
Lê18
,
CL22
,
CKL
]
.

Denote by
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g}
the compact closed oriented surface of genus
\displaystyle g\geq 0
, by
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}
the surface obtained from
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g}
by removing
\displaystyle n
points, by
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
the punctured bordered surface obtained from
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}
by removing an open disk
\displaystyle D
, and then by
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
the surface obtained from
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
by removing a point from the boundary component
\displaystyle\partial(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
. Here is a picture of
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
:

The puncture on the boundary is represented by a big black dot (•) while the
\displaystyle n
inner punctures are represented by the cross marks (
\displaystyle\mathsf{x}
). For our purposes it is much more convenient to use a less familiar view of
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
:

(76)

Let
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}\times[0,1]
be the thickening of
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
. A point
\displaystyle P\in\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is a couple
\displaystyle(p,h)
and the number
\displaystyle h
is called the
height
of
\displaystyle P
. Recall from
[
Lê18
, §2.2]
that a
\displaystyle H
-colored
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-tangle is an oriented, framed, compact, properly embedded
1-dimensional submanifold
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}\subset\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
such that

(1)

the framing is vertical at any point of
\displaystyle\partial\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}\cap\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
,

(2)

the points of
\displaystyle\partial\mathbf{T}
have distincts heights,

(3)

each connected component of
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
is colored (i.e. labelled) by a finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-module.

Here we moreover allow the
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-tangles to contain
coupons
, which are squares colored by
\displaystyle H
-morphisms in the usual coherent way. The coupons are not allowed to intersect
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
. This gives
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-ribbon graphs
, which are the generalization to surfaces of the homogeneous colored directed ribbon graphs from
[
RT90
]
.

An isotopy of
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-ribbon graphs is an isotopy which preserves all the properties listed above. We represent a
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-ribbon graph
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
by its diagram, which is the projection of
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
on
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
. We use isotopy to ensure that the projection has at most double points, and at double points we record the over/under-passing information as usual. Moreover, the height information of the boundary points
\displaystyle\partial\mathbf{T}
must be recorded with the diagram; thanks to isotopy it is enough to record the order of the boundary points with respect to the relation
\displaystyle<
defined by
\displaystyle(p,h)<(p^{\prime},h^{\prime})
if and only
\displaystyle h<h^{\prime}
. It is customary to identify a
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-ribbon graph with its isotopy class.

Let
\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}
be an orientation of
\displaystyle\partial\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
. We say that a diagram of the
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-ribbon graph
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
is
\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}
-ordered if the boundary points of
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
have increasing heights when one goes along
\displaystyle\partial\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
according to
\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}
and starting from the point
\displaystyle\bullet
. In a
\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}
-ordered diagram the height relation
\displaystyle<
of the boundary points of
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
is given by
\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}
. Using isotopy it is clear that any
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-ribbon graph can be represented by a
\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}
-ordered diagram. Here is an example of such a diagram for
\displaystyle(g,n)=(1,1)
:

(77)

The colors (i.e. labels)
\displaystyle U,V,W,X,Y
are finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-modules and
\displaystyle f:W\otimes V^{*}\to V
,
\displaystyle g:U\to Y
are
\displaystyle H
-morphisms. With this choice of orientation of the boundary, the diagram implicitly means that the heights of the boundary points labeled by
\displaystyle U
,
\displaystyle W
and
\displaystyle Y
respectively form a strictly increasing sequence. In the sequel we always use ordered diagrams with respect to this orientation.

Let
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
be a
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-ribbon graph, let
\displaystyle p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}
be the boundary points of
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
and let
\displaystyle V_{i}
be the color of the strand to which belongs
\displaystyle p_{i}
for each
\displaystyle i
. A
state
of
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
is a map which to each
\displaystyle p_{i}
associates an element of
\displaystyle V_{i}
or
\displaystyle V_{i}^{*}
, depending if the orientation of the strand is incoming or outcoming at
\displaystyle p_{i}
respectively. In practice we label
\displaystyle p_{i}
by an element of
\displaystyle V_{i}
or
\displaystyle V_{i}^{*}
on the ribbon graph diagram. Here is an example of a state for a ribbon graph like in (
77
):

with
\displaystyle u\in U
,
\displaystyle w\in W
,
\displaystyle\gamma\in Y^{*}
; in this picture we just show
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
in a disk which intersects the boundary. A
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-ribbon graph together with a state is called a
stated ribbon graph
. If
\displaystyle s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{l})
is a state of the ribbon graph
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
(
i.e.
a labelling of the boundary points ordered by increasing height), we denote by
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{s}
the ribbon graph
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
with the state
\displaystyle s
.

Denote by
\displaystyle M^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
the vector space freely generated by the (isotopy classes of) stated ribbon graphs, over the base field
\displaystyle k
of
\displaystyle H
. In
\displaystyle M^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
we “multilinearize” the states,
i.e
we put the relations
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},\ldots,\lambda s_{i}+\mu s^{\prime}_{i},\ldots,s_{n})}=\lambda\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},\ldots,s_{i},\ldots,s_{n})}+\mu\mathbf{T}^{(s_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{i},\ldots,s_{n})}
for all
\displaystyle i
, all
\displaystyle\lambda,\mu\in k
and any
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-ribbon graph
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
which can be labelled with these states.

Let
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{s}
be a stated ribbon graph which looks as follows in a disk
\displaystyle D
which intersects the boundary:

(78)

where
\displaystyle m,l\geq 0
and
\displaystyle T
is any oriented and colored
\displaystyle(m,l)
-ribbon graph in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We emphasize that
\displaystyle m
or
\displaystyle l
may be equal to
\displaystyle 0
. Each strand carries an orientation. For
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq l
let
\displaystyle\epsilon(i)\in\{\pm\}
be
\displaystyle+
(resp.
\displaystyle-
) if the orientation of the strand colored by
\displaystyle V_{i}
points inward (resp. outward) the surface at the boundary. Set
\displaystyle V_{i}^{+}=V_{i}
and
\displaystyle V_{i}^{-}=V_{i}^{*}
so that
\displaystyle s_{i}\in V_{i}^{-\epsilon(i)}
. Similarly for
\displaystyle 1\leq j\leq m
let
\displaystyle\eta(j)\in\{\pm\}
be
\displaystyle+
(resp.
\displaystyle-
) if the strand colored by
\displaystyle W_{j}
is outcoming (resp. incoming) at the bottom of
\displaystyle T
and set
\displaystyle W_{j}^{+}=W_{j}
and
\displaystyle W_{j}^{-}=W_{j}^{*}
. The Reshetikhin–Turaev functor
\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{RT}}

[
RT90
]
applied to
\displaystyle T
gives a linear map

F_{\mathrm{RT}}(T):W_{1}^{\eta(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes W_{m}^{\eta(m)}\to V_{1}^{\epsilon(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{l}^{\epsilon(l)}.

Denote as usual
\displaystyle\langle-,-\rangle
for the evaluation pairing:
\displaystyle\langle\varphi,v\rangle=\langle v,\varphi\rangle=\varphi(v)
for any
\displaystyle v\in V_{i}
,
\displaystyle\varphi\in V_{i}^{*}
. We extend this pairing straightforwardly:

(79)

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}\langle-,-\rangle:&\left(V_{1}^{-\epsilon(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{l}^{-\epsilon(l)}\right)\otimes\left(V_{1}^{\epsilon(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{l}^{\epsilon(l)}\right)&\to&k\\[5.0pt]
&(s_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes s_{l})\otimes(x_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{l})&\mapsto&\langle s_{1},x_{1}\rangle\ldots\langle s_{l},x_{l}\rangle\end{array}

Let
\displaystyle\bigl(w^{j,+}_{r}\bigr)_{r}
be a basis of
\displaystyle W_{j}
and
\displaystyle\bigl(w^{j,-}_{r}\bigr)_{r}
be its dual basis. Then we identify
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{s}
with the following element of
\displaystyle M^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
:

(80)

We stress that the stated ribbon graphs appearing in the linear combination (
80
) are equal to
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
outside the disk
\displaystyle D
represented in (
78
). Such an identification is called a
stated skein relation
.

Definition 6.1

(
[
CKL
]
)
.

The stated skein algebra of
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
associated to
\displaystyle H
, which we denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
, is the quotient of
\displaystyle M^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
by all the stated skein relations (
80
).

Given two stated ribbon graphs
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{1}^{s}
,
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{2}^{t}
we can use isotopy to transform
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{1}^{s}
into
\displaystyle\textstyle(\mathbf{T}_{1}^{s})^{-}\subset\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}\times[0,\frac{1}{2}[
and
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{2}
into
\displaystyle\textstyle(\mathbf{T}_{2}^{t})^{+}\subset\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}\times]\frac{1}{2},1]
. The disjoint union
\displaystyle(\mathbf{T}_{1}^{s})^{-}\cup(\mathbf{T}_{2}^{t})^{+}
is denoted by
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{1}^{s}\ast\mathbf{T}_{2}^{t}
. Each boundary point keeps its state in
\displaystyle(\mathbf{T}_{1}^{s})^{-}\cup(\mathbf{T}_{2}^{t})^{+}
so that the result is naturally a stated ribbon graph. This operation
\displaystyle\ast
defines an associative algebra structure on
\displaystyle M^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
which descends to
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
. The unit is the empty
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-ribbon graph.

Here is an example of stated skein relation which we will use later:

(81)

where
\displaystyle v\in X
,
\displaystyle\varphi\in Y^{*}
,
\displaystyle c
is the braiding in
\displaystyle H\text{-}\mathrm{mod}
,
\displaystyle(x_{i})
is a basis of
\displaystyle X
,
\displaystyle(y_{j})
is a basis of
\displaystyle Y
,
\displaystyle(x^{i})
,
\displaystyle(y^{j})
are their dual bases and
\displaystyle\varphi(R_{(1)}?)
denotes the linear form on
\displaystyle Y
defined by
\displaystyle y\mapsto\varphi(R_{(1)}y)
. The detail of the second equality is

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}\left\langle v\otimes\varphi,c_{Y,X^{*}}(y_{i}\otimes x^{j})\right\rangle y^{i}\otimes x_{j}=\sum_{(R),i,j}\left\langle v\otimes\varphi,R_{(2)}x^{j}\otimes R_{(1)}y_{i}\right\rangle y^{i}\otimes x_{j}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R),i,j}\left\langle x^{j},S(R_{(2)})v\right\rangle\left\langle\varphi,R_{(1)}y_{i}\right\rangle y^{i}\otimes x_{j}=\sum_{(R)}\varphi(R_{(1)}?)\otimes S(R_{(2)})v.

One proves similarly that

(82)

6.2.
Isomorphism
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})\cong\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)

This subsection is a generalization of
[
Fai20b
, §5]
, which dealt with the case
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. The main ingredient in the definition of the isomorphism will be the
holonomy map

\mathrm{hol}:\bigl\{\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\text{-ribbon graphs}\bigr\}\to\bigl\{\text{tensors with coefficients in }\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\bigr\}

defined in
[
Fai20b
, §4.1]
.

Let
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{s}\subset\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}\times[0,1]
be a stated ribbon graph with state
\displaystyle s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{l})
which looks as follows near the boundary:

As before, let
\displaystyle\epsilon(i)\in\{\pm\}
be
\displaystyle+
(resp.
\displaystyle-
) if the orientation of the strand colored by
\displaystyle V_{i}
points inward (resp. outward) the surface at the boundary, and set
\displaystyle V_{i}^{+}=V_{i}
,
\displaystyle V_{i}^{-}=V_{i}^{*}
so that
\displaystyle s_{i}\in V^{-\epsilon(i)}
. By definition
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}
is the
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-ribbon graph obtained from
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{s}
by forgetting the states. Then the holonomy map gives an element

\mathrm{hol}(\mathbf{T})\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\otimes V_{1}^{\epsilon(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{l}^{\epsilon(l)}.

Let
\displaystyle\langle-,-\rangle
be the evaluation pairing as in (
79
). Then
\displaystyle\langle s_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes s_{l},-\rangle
is a linear map
\displaystyle V_{1}^{\epsilon(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{l}^{\epsilon(l)}\to k
and we define

\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}(\mathbf{T}^{s})=\left(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)}\otimes\langle s_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes s_{l},-\rangle\right)\!(\mathrm{hol}(\mathbf{T}))\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H).

Extending linearly, this gives a map
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}:M^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
. It is immediate from the definition of
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}
in
[
Fai20b
]
that
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}
is compatible with the stated skein relations, so it descends to
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
.

Definition 6.2
.

We call
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
the stated holonomy map.

Proposition 6.3
.

The stated holonomy map is a morphism of algebras.

Proof.

This follows from one of the main properties of
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}
proven in
[
Fai20b
]
. Let
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{s}_{1}
,
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{t}_{2}
be stated ribbon graphs and let

be their boundary points and states. By definition of the product of stated ribbon graphs and of an ordered
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-ribbon graph diagram, the boundary points of
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{s}_{1}\ast\mathbf{T}_{2}^{t}
are arranged as follows:

Let
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{1}
,
\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{2}
be the
\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
-ribbon graphs without states and write
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{hol}(\mathbf{T}_{1})=\sum_{i}x_{i}\otimes\mathbf{v}_{i}
,
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{hol}(\mathbf{T}_{2})=\sum_{j}y_{j}\otimes\mathbf{w}_{j}
with
\displaystyle x_{i},y_{j}\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
,
\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{i}\in V_{1}^{\epsilon(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{l}^{\epsilon(l)}
and
\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{j}\in W_{1}^{\eta(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes W_{m}^{\eta(m)}
, where the
\displaystyle\epsilon
’s and
\displaystyle\eta
’s are signs as above.
We know from
[
Fai20b
, Th. 4.4]
that
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{hol}(\mathbf{T}_{1}\ast\mathbf{T}_{2})=\sum_{i,j}x_{i}y_{j}\otimes\mathbf{v}_{i}\otimes\mathbf{w}_{j}
. Hence:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}(\mathbf{T}^{s}_{1}\ast\mathbf{T}^{t}_{2})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\left(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)}\otimes\langle s_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes s_{l}\otimes t_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes t_{m},-\rangle\right)\!(\mathrm{hol}(\mathbf{T}_{1}\ast\mathbf{T}_{2}))

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}x_{i}y_{j}\,\langle s_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes s_{l},\mathbf{v}_{i}\rangle\,\langle t_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes t_{m},\mathbf{w}_{j}\rangle=\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}(\mathbf{T}^{s}_{1})\,\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}(\mathbf{T}^{t}_{2}).\qed

We now define what will be a right inverse to
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}
. Recall that
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
is spanned by the matrix coefficients
{}_{V}\phi^{f}_{v}
of finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-modules and that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is
\displaystyle(H^{\circ})^{\otimes(2g+n)}
as a vector space. We define a linear map
\displaystyle\xi_{g,n}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\to\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
by

(83)

Lemma 6.4
.

The linear map
\displaystyle\xi_{g,n}:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\to\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
is surjective.

Proof.

Due to the stated skein relations (
80
) in
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
we see that every element is equal to a linear combination of “standard” stated tangles of the form

In this picture each strand carries some orientation and color and

is some state. We put the crossings in view of the definition (

∎

Theorem 6.5
.

The stated holonomy map
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is an isomorphism of algebras.

Proof.

We already know from Proposition
6.3
that
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}
is a morphism of algebras. It is immediate from the definition of
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}
in
[
Fai20b
]
that
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}\circ\xi_{g,n}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)}
(the role of the crossings in the definition (
83
) of
\displaystyle\xi_{g,n}
is precisely to obtain this equality). This implies that the linear map
\displaystyle\xi_{g,n}
is injective. It follows from Lemma
6.4
that
\displaystyle\xi_{g,n}
is an isomorphism of vector spaces. Hence
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}=\xi^{-1}_{g,n}
is an isomorphism as well.
∎

A corollary of the previous proof is that
\displaystyle\xi_{g,n}
is a morphism of algebras. It is nevertheless worthwile to mention that the direct proof (
i.e.
without resorting on
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}
) of this fact allows one to recover the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
by means of stated skein relations, thus giving a topological flavour to the formulas in Proposition
4.4
. We explain this for
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{0,1}^{\circ,\bullet})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{1,0}^{\circ,\bullet})
.

So let us first discuss the case of
\displaystyle\xi_{0,1}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{0,1}^{\circ,\bullet})
. It is useful to note that

(84)

h\rhd{{}_{V}\phi^{f}_{v}}={{}_{V}\phi^{f}_{hv}},\qquad{{}_{V}\phi^{f}_{v}}\lhd h={{}_{V}\phi^{f(h?)}_{v}}

where we recall that
\displaystyle\rhd
and
\displaystyle\lhd
are the left and right coregular actions of
\displaystyle H
on
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
, see (
19
). We then have the following stated skein computation, which recovers the formula (
22
) for the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)
:

The first equality is by definition of the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{0,1}^{\circ,\bullet})
, the second equality uses the stated skein relations (
81
) and (
82
), the third equality uses the all-in-one relation (see the proof of Lemma
6.4
), the fourth equality is the definition of
\displaystyle\xi_{0,1}
and the last equality uses (
84
) and (
3
).

Let us now consider
\displaystyle\xi_{1,0}:\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)\to\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{1,0}^{\circ,\bullet})
. Recall that the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(H)
is fully described by the formulas (
33
). Let us write

\xi_{b}=\xi_{1,0}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{B},\qquad\xi_{a}=\xi_{1,0}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{A}

which are morphisms of algebras
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{1,0}^{\circ,\bullet})
, and we recall that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{B}
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{A}
are defined in (
25
). Then the definition of
\displaystyle\xi_{1,0}
in (

) is such that

(85)

\xi_{1,0}(\beta\otimes\alpha)=\xi_{b}(\beta)\,\xi_{a}(\alpha).

We have two natural morphisms of algebras
\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}_{b},\mathfrak{e}_{a}:\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{0,1}^{\circ,\bullet})\to\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{1,0}^{\circ,\bullet})
defined by

(we use that every element of
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{0,1}^{\circ,\bullet})
can be written as a linear combination of such stated tangles, see the proof of Lemma

). They satisfy
\displaystyle\xi_{b}=\mathfrak{e}_{b}\circ\xi_{0,1}
,
\displaystyle\xi_{a}=\mathfrak{e}_{a}\circ\xi_{0,1}
. Hence by the previous computation for
\displaystyle\xi_{0,1}
we find

\xi_{b}(\varphi)\,\xi_{b}(\psi)=\mathfrak{e}_{b}\bigl(\xi_{0,1}(\varphi)\,\xi_{0,1}(\psi)\bigr)=\mathfrak{e}_{b}\bigl(\xi_{0,1}(\varphi\psi)\bigr)=\xi_{b}(\varphi\psi)

and similarly for
\displaystyle\xi_{a}
; these two equalities correspond to the first and second formulas of (
33
). Finally:

The first equality is by definition of the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{1,0}^{\circ,\bullet})
, the second equality is a trick, the third equality uses stated skein relations like (

\displaystyle\xi_{1,0}

). This equality corresponds to the third formula of (

For general
\displaystyle g
and
\displaystyle n
write

\xi_{b_{i}}=\xi_{g,n}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{B(i)},\qquad\xi_{a_{i}}=\xi_{g,n}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{A(i)},\qquad\xi_{m_{j}}=\xi_{g,n}\circ\mathfrak{i}_{M(j)}.

for all
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq g
,
\displaystyle g+1\leq j\leq g+n
and observe that by definition of
\displaystyle\xi_{g,n}
(see (

)) we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\xi_{g,n}\bigl(\varphi_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\varphi_{2g+n}\bigr)=\xi_{b_{1}}(\varphi_{1})\,\xi_{a_{1}}(\varphi_{2})\ldots\xi_{b_{g}}(\varphi_{2g-1})\,\xi_{a_{g}}(\varphi_{g})\,\xi_{m_{g+1}}(\varphi_{2g+1})\ldots\xi_{m_{g+n}}(\varphi_{2g+n}).

As above, one can show using the stated skein relations that the elements
\displaystyle\xi_{b_{i}}(\varphi)
,
\displaystyle\xi_{a_{i}}(\varphi)
,
\displaystyle\xi_{m_{j}}(\varphi)\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
with

obey the same product than the elements

,
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{A(i)}(\varphi)
,

. The reader can treat the case
\displaystyle(g,n)=(0,2)
as an exercise.

The computations above reveal the topological relevance of the elements
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{B(i)}(\varphi)
,
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{A(i)}(\varphi)
,
\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{M(j)}(\varphi)
and of the formulas in Proposition
