for their products.

6.3.
Isomorphism
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\cong\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
for semisimple
\displaystyle H\text{-}\mathrm{mod}

This part is a generalization of
[
BR22
, §8.2]
, which dealt with the case
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
.

Recall that
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
is the compact oriented surface of genus
\displaystyle g
with one boundary component and
\displaystyle n
punctures not belonging to the boundary. Recall that
\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{RT}}
denotes the Reshetikhin–Turaev functor
[
RT90
]
, and
\displaystyle k
is the base field of
\displaystyle H
.

Definition 6.6
.

The skein algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
is the
\displaystyle k
-vector space generated by the isotopy classes of ribbon links with coupons (i.e. ribbon graphs without boundary points) in
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}\times[0,1]
, modulo the skein relations:

(86)

where
\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\in k
and the
\displaystyle T_{i}
’s are any ribbon graphs in
\displaystyle[0,1]^{3}
having common colors on the incoming and outcoming strands. The sum on the left represents a linear combination of ribbon links which are equal outside of some cube in
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}\times[0,1]
which is depicted in grey.

Note that up to introducing identity coupons, we can always assume that the incoming and outcoming strands of
\displaystyle T_{i}
have this orientation. Then
\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{RT}}(T_{i})\in\mathrm{Hom}_{H}\bigl(X_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes X_{k},Y_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes Y_{l}\bigr)
. The product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
is given by stacking (see after Definition
6.1
).

Recall that
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}
is
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
with a puncture on the boundary. This puncture is irrelevant when we restrict to ribbon links, so if
\displaystyle L
is the isotopy class of a ribbon link in
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}\times[0,1]
we have the corresponding isotopy class
\displaystyle I(L)
in
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet}\times[0,1]
. Note that
\displaystyle I(L)
can be seen as a stated ribbon graph without boundary points (and thus without states):
\displaystyle I(L)\in\mathcal{S}_{H}^{\mathrm{st}}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
.

Lemma 6.7
.

The map
\displaystyle L\mapsto I(L)
induces a well-defined morphism of algebras
\displaystyle I:\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\to\mathcal{S}_{H}^{\mathrm{st}}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}
be links in
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}\times[0,1]
and
\displaystyle\textstyle L=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}L_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
. We have to show that if
\displaystyle L=0
is a skein relation (
86
) in
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
then
\displaystyle\textstyle I(L)=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}I(L_{i})=0
follows from the stated skein relations in
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}^{\mathrm{st}}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
. For notational simplicity we take ribbon graphs
\displaystyle T_{i}
which have
\displaystyle 2
incoming strands and
\displaystyle 1
outcoming strand in (
86
). Using isotopy we can assume that the cube in (
86
) is very close to
\displaystyle\partial(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
. Therefore in
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}^{\mathrm{st}}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
we have

where all ribbon graphs are equal outside of the grey cube and
\displaystyle(y_{j})
,
\displaystyle(x^{(1)}_{k})
,
\displaystyle(x^{(2)}_{l})
are bases of
\displaystyle Y
,
\displaystyle X_{1}
,
\displaystyle X_{2}
with dual bases
\displaystyle(y^{j})
,
\displaystyle(x^{(1),k})
,
\displaystyle(x^{(2),l})
. For the second equality we used (
80
) and for the third equality we used that
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\,F_{\mathrm{RT}}(T_{i})=0
by assumption. It is clear that
\displaystyle I
is a morphism of algebras since both products are by stacking.
∎

Note that since
\displaystyle I(L)
is a stated ribbon graph without boundary points (and thus without states),
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}\bigl(I(L)\bigr)
is just an element of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
. Thus we can make the following definition:

Definition 6.8
.

The morphism
\displaystyle W=\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}\circ I:\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is called the Wilson loop map.

Recall that the subalgebra of
\displaystyle H
-invariant elements is

\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)=\bigl\{x\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\,\big|\,\forall\,h\in H,\>\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(x)=\varepsilon(h)x\bigr\}.

Theorem 6.9
.

If the ribbon Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H
has semisimple category
\displaystyle H\text{-}\mathrm{mod}
, the Wilson loop map takes values in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{H}_{g,n}(H)
and provides an isomorphism of algebras
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{L}^{H}_{g,n}(H)
.

It is well-known that the ribbon category
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
of finite dimensional
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules of type
\displaystyle 1
is semisimple. Therefore we can apply this theorem to
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
, meaning that the ribbon links with coupons defining
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
are colored by objects and morphisms in
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
. Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
is equivalent to the category of finite dimensional modules of type
\displaystyle 1
over the simply connected quantum group
\displaystyle U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, we can indifferently take
\displaystyle H=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
.

It is true that
\displaystyle W
takes values in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{H}_{g,n}(H)
even if
\displaystyle H
-mod is not semisimple, but in general it is not an isomorphism. See §
6.4
for more details on the non-semisimple case.

In all the remaining of this section we assume that
\displaystyle H
-mod is semisimple.
In order to prove the theorem we first define two auxiliary maps
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
.

Let
\displaystyle\mathrm{Irr}(H)
be the set of irreducible finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-modules up to isomorphisms. Since
\displaystyle H\text{-}\mathrm{mod}
is semisimple the matrix coefficients of the modules in
\displaystyle\mathrm{Irr}(H)
form a basis of
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
,
i.e.

\displaystyle\textstyle H^{\circ}=\bigoplus_{X\in\mathrm{Irr}(H)}C(X)
. Hence

(87)

\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathrm{Irr}(H)^{2g+n}}C(X_{1})\otimes C(X_{2})\otimes\ldots\otimes C(X_{2g+n})

where
\displaystyle\mathbf{X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{2g+n})
. For any such
\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\in\mathrm{Irr}(H)^{2g+n}
, let

\textstyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{X}}:\mathrm{Hom}_{k}\!\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2g+n}X_{i}\otimes X_{i}^{*},k\right)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}C(X_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes C(X_{2g+n})

(
\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{k}
denotes the space of all linear maps) be the isomorphism of vector spaces defined by

\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{X}}(f)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\ldots,i_{N}\\
j_{1},\ldots,j_{N}\end{subarray}}f\!\left(x_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\otimes x^{(1),j_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{i_{N}}^{(N)}\otimes x^{(N),j_{N}}\right)\,{{}_{X_{1}}\phi^{i_{1}}_{j_{1}}}\otimes\ldots\otimes{{}_{X_{N}}\phi^{i_{N}}_{j_{N}}}

where
\displaystyle N=2g+n
,
\displaystyle\bigl(x_{i_{l}}^{(l)}\bigr)
is a basis of
\displaystyle X_{l}
with dual basis
\displaystyle\bigl(x^{(l),j_{l}}\bigr)
and
{}_{X_{l}}\phi^{i_{l}}_{j_{l}}
denotes the matrix coefficients of
\displaystyle X_{l}
in this basis. When
\displaystyle f
is
\displaystyle H
-linear, it is useful to represent the element
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{X}}(f)
by using the diagrammatic calculus of
[
Fai20b
, §3]
as follows:

(88)

Lemma 6.10
.

By restriction to
\displaystyle H
-linear maps, the
\displaystyle k
-linear isomorphism
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{X}}
gives an isomorphism of vector spaces
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{Hom}_{H}\!\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2g+n}X_{i}\otimes X_{i}^{*},k\right)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\bigl(C(X_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes C(X_{2g+n})\bigr)^{H}
. It follows that we have an isomorphism of vector spaces

\mathcal{E}:\bigoplus_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathrm{Irr}(H)^{2g+n}}\mathrm{Hom}_{H}\!\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2g+n}X_{i}\otimes X_{i}^{*},k\right)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H).

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle\mathrm{act}^{r}
be the right action of
\displaystyle H
on
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{Hom}_{k}\!\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2g+n}X_{i}\otimes X_{i}^{*},k\right)
defined by

\mathrm{act}^{r}(h)(f)\bigl(x_{1}\otimes\varphi_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{N}\otimes\varphi_{N}\bigr)=\sum_{(h)}f\bigl(h_{(1)}\cdot x_{1}\otimes h_{(2)}\cdot\varphi_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes h_{(2N-1)}\cdot x_{N}\otimes h_{(2N)}\cdot\varphi_{N}\bigr)

for all
\displaystyle x_{l}\in X_{l}
,
\displaystyle\varphi_{l}\in X_{l}^{*}
, where
\displaystyle N=2g+n
and
\displaystyle h\in H
. Then
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{X}}
intertwines the right actions
\displaystyle\mathrm{act}^{r}
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
. This is due to the definition of
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
(see (
53
) and (
23
)) and to the following formulas, which come from (
84
):

(89)

\begin{array}[]{l}\sum_{i_{l},j_{l}}\bigl(h\rhd{{}_{X_{l}}\phi^{i_{l}}_{j_{l}}}\bigr)\otimes x_{i_{l}}\otimes x^{j_{l}}=\sum_{i_{l},j_{l}}{{}_{X_{l}}\phi^{i_{l}}_{j_{l}}}\otimes x_{i_{l}}\otimes S^{-1}(h)\cdot x^{j_{l}},\\[15.00002pt]
\sum_{i_{l},j_{l}}\bigl({{}_{X_{l}}\phi^{i_{l}}_{j_{l}}}\lhd h\bigr)\otimes x_{i_{l}}\otimes x^{j_{l}}=\sum_{i_{l},j_{l}}{{}_{X_{l}}\phi^{i_{l}}_{j_{l}}}\otimes h\cdot x_{i_{l}}\otimes x^{j_{l}}.\end{array}

For the first formula we used that if
\displaystyle V
is a
\displaystyle H
-module, then the action of
\displaystyle h\in H
on
\displaystyle\psi\in V^{*}
is defined by
\displaystyle\langle h\cdot\psi,v\rangle=\langle\psi,S(h)\cdot v\rangle
for all
\displaystyle v\in V
. The lemma follows from this intertwining property because

\textstyle f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{H}\!\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2g+n}X_{i}\otimes X_{i}^{*},k\right)\quad\iff\quad\forall\,h\in H,\>\>\mathrm{act}^{r}(h)(f)=\varepsilon(h)f.\qed

Now, for
\displaystyle\mathbf{X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{2g+n})\in\mathrm{Irr}(H)^{2g+n}
, let

\textstyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{X}}:\mathrm{Hom}_{H}\!\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2g+n}X_{i}\otimes X_{i}^{*},k\right)\to\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})

defined by

The map
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{X}}
is
\displaystyle k
-linear thanks to the skein relations (
86
). Let

\mathcal{F}:\bigoplus_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathrm{Irr}(H)^{2g+n}}\mathrm{Hom}_{H}\!\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2g+n}X_{i}\otimes X_{i}^{*},k\right)\to\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})

be the sum of the linear maps
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{X}}
.

Lemma 6.11
.

The linear map
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
is surjective.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
6.4
we observe that every element of
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
is a linear combination of links of the form

where
\displaystyle T_{\mathbf{X}}
is some oriented and colored
\displaystyle(4g+2n,0)
-ribbon graph in
\displaystyle[0,1]^{3}
. We can assume that the colors
\displaystyle\mathbf{X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{2g+n})
are irreducible. Indeed, if a strand is colored by a non-irreducible module
\displaystyle V
, then write
\displaystyle\textstyle V=\bigoplus_{l}S_{l}
where the
\displaystyle S_{l}
are irreducible. We can introduce coupons containing the injections
\displaystyle I_{l}:S_{l}\to V
and projections
\displaystyle\pi_{l}:V\to S_{l}
thanks to the relation
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{id}_{V}=\sum_{l}I_{l}\circ\pi_{l}
. By sliding these coupons along the strand we get a sum of links where the strands under consideration are colored by the modules
\displaystyle S_{l}
.

Thanks to the skein relations (
86
) we can replace the ribbon graph
\displaystyle T_{\mathbf{X}}
by a coupon colored by
\displaystyle\textstyle F_{\mathrm{RT}}(T_{\mathbf{X}})\in\mathrm{Hom}_{H}\!\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2g+n}X_{i}\otimes X_{i}^{*},k\right)
and it follows that
\displaystyle L_{\mathbf{X}}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{X}}\bigl(F_{\mathrm{RT}}(T_{\mathbf{X}})\bigr)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.9
.

By definition of
\displaystyle\mathrm{hol}

[
Fai20b
, Def. 4.2]
and (
88
), we have a commutative diagram

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bigoplus_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathrm{Irr}(H)^{2g+n}}\mathrm{Hom}_{H}\!\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2g+n}X_{i}\otimes X_{i}^{*},k\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{W}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)}

The surjectivity of
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
(Lemma
6.11
) implies that
\displaystyle W
takes values in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
. Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
is an isomorphism (Lemma
6.10
),
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
is necessarily injective, so it is an isomorphism of vector spaces. It follows that
\displaystyle W
is an isomorphism between
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{H}_{g,n}(H)
.
∎

Recall the map
\displaystyle I
introduced before Lemma
6.7
. Our results imply the following important fact, which is not obvious despite seeming completely natural:

Corollary 6.12
.

Assume that
\displaystyle H\text{-}\mathrm{mod}
is semisimple. Then the morphism of algebras
\displaystyle I:\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\to\mathcal{S}_{H}^{\mathrm{st}}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
is injective.

Proof.

By the definition of
\displaystyle W
(Definition
6.8
), Theorem
6.5
and Theorem
6.9
we have a commutative diagram where the rows are isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{H}^{\mathrm{st}}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathrm{hol}^{\mathrm{st}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\sim}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\circlearrowleft}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{W}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\sim}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{I}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
(subalgebra)

It immediately follows that
\displaystyle I
is injective.
∎

This corollary means that the skein algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
is isomorphic to the subalgebra of ribbon links (with coupons) in the stated skein algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}^{\mathrm{st}}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ,\bullet})
.

6.4.
Remarks on semisimplicity

If the category
\displaystyle H\text{-}\mathrm{mod}
is not semisimple:

•

the matrix coefficients of irreducible modules do not form a generating family of
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
,

•

the matrix coefficients of indecomposable modules form a generating family of
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
but not a free family (even if one restricts to projective modules).

Hence
\displaystyle\textstyle H^{\circ}=\sum_{X\in\mathrm{Ind}(H)}C(X)
, where
\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}(H)
denotes the set of indecomposable
\displaystyle H
-modules up to isomorphisms.

Consequently the decomposition (
87
) is no longer true, and instead one has

\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)=\sum_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathrm{Ind}(H)^{2g+n}}C(X_{1})\otimes C(X_{2})\otimes\ldots\otimes C(X_{2g+n}).

So there is a surjection
\displaystyle\textstyle\bigoplus_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathrm{Ind}(H)^{2g+n}}\mathrm{Hom}_{k}\!\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2g+n}X_{i}\otimes X_{i}^{*},k\right)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
and a commutative diagram

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bigoplus_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathrm{Ind}(H)^{2g+n}}\mathrm{Hom}_{H}\!\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2g+n}X_{i}\otimes X_{i}^{*},k\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{W}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)}

Moreover
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
is still surjective, thus
\displaystyle W
takes values in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
. But in general nothing can be said about the surjectivity of
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
. So when
\displaystyle H
is not semisimple, Theorem
6.9
will in general not be true:

\mathrm{im}(W)\subsetneq\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H).

Non-semisimplicity arises notably at roots of unity. For instance take
\displaystyle(g,n)=(0,1)
and
\displaystyle H=\overline{U}_{\epsilon}=\overline{U}_{\epsilon}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
, the restricted quantum group of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{sl}(2)
at
\displaystyle\epsilon=e^{i\pi/p}
with
\displaystyle p\geq 2
(see e.g.
[
FGST06
, §3]
). We have
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0,1}^{\overline{U}_{\epsilon}}\cong\mathcal{Z}(\overline{U}_{\epsilon})
(see e.g.
[
Fai19a
, Th. 3.7]
) and it is known that
\displaystyle\dim\bigl(\mathcal{Z}(\overline{U}_{\epsilon})\bigr)=3p-1

[
FGST06
, Prop. 4.4.4]
. On the other hand one can check that
\displaystyle\dim\bigl(\mathrm{im}(W)\bigr)=2p
, showing that the inclusion is indeed strict.

In
[
Fai20b
, §4.4]
a generalization of Wilson loops was defined in order to produce more
\displaystyle H
-invariant elements. The idea is roughly to use other symmetric linear forms than the traces; for
\displaystyle\Sigma^{\circ}_{0,1}
these generalized Wilson loops recover the whole of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{H}_{0,1}(H)
.

Another interesting situation where non-semisimplicity arises is when working over the ring
\displaystyle A=\mathbb{C}[q^{1/D},q^{-1/D}]
and specializing
\displaystyle q
to a root of unity. To define an integral version
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A}
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
defined over
\displaystyle A
, such that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A}\otimes_{A}\mathbb{C}(q^{1/D})=\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
, one considers Lusztig’s restricted quantum group
\displaystyle U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{res}}(\mathfrak{g})\subset U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
, which is defined over
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}:=\mathbb{C}[q,q^{-1}]
, and its restricted dual
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{A}}(G)
, associated to the category
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{A}}
of finite rank
\displaystyle U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{res}}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules of type
\displaystyle 1
. The algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{A}
in the case
\displaystyle g=0
is described in
[
BR22
]
.

7.
The case of surfaces without boundary

By Theorem
6.9
, we know that if the ribbon Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H
has semisimple category
\displaystyle H\text{-}\mathrm{mod}
, the Wilson loop map provides an isomorphism of algebras
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{L}^{H}_{g,n}(H)
. Here we address the question of defining algebras which are related to the surface
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}
obtained by closing
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
, and derived from
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{H}_{g,n}(H)
respectively. We stress that the construction and results presented below works for any
\displaystyle n\geq 0
, including in particular the closed surface of genus
\displaystyle g
.

7.1.
Quantum moment maps and quantum reduction

Let
\displaystyle H
be a Hopf algebra over a field
\displaystyle k
, and
\displaystyle H^{\prime}\subset H
a subalgebra and right
\displaystyle H
-coideal (so
\displaystyle\Delta(H^{\prime})\subset H^{\prime}\otimes H
). On
\displaystyle H
we have the right adjoint action,

\mathrm{ad}^{r}(h)(g)=\sum_{(h)}S(h_{(1)})gh_{(2)},\quad(h,g\in H),

and we assume that
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
is a stable subspace under this action. Let
\displaystyle A
be a left
\displaystyle H
-module algebra. We denote by

\displaystyle\blacktriangleright

the action of
\displaystyle H
on
\displaystyle A
.

Definition 7.1
.

A morphism of algebras
\displaystyle\mu\colon H^{\prime}\rightarrow A
is a quantum moment map
(QMM)
if for every
\displaystyle a\in A
and
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
it satisfies

\sum_{(h^{\prime})}(h_{(2)}^{\prime}\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}a)\mu(h_{(1)}^{\prime})=\mu(h^{\prime})a.

This definition is implicit in
[
Lu93
]
, and explicit in
[
VV10
, Section 1.5]
(we use an opposite coproduct, which is suited to our conventions). The following facts follow readily from the definitions and are properties we can expect of a quantum analog of moment map (see
[
Lu93
]
, Theorem 3.10).

Lemma 7.2
.

Assume we are given a QMM
\displaystyle\mu\colon H^{\prime}\rightarrow A
. Then:

\displaystyle(i)

(Equivariance)
For every
\displaystyle h\in H
and
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
we have

\mu\bigl(\mathrm{ad}^{r}(h)(h^{\prime})\bigr)=S(h)\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}\mu(h^{\prime}).

\displaystyle(ii)

(Recovering the action)
For every
\displaystyle a\in A
and
\displaystyle h\in H
such that
\displaystyle(S\otimes id)\Delta(h)\in H^{\prime}\otimes H^{\prime}
(eg. when
\displaystyle H^{\prime}=H
), we have

\textstyle\sum_{(h)}\mu(S(h_{(1)}))a\mu(h_{(2)})=S(h)\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}a.

Given a morphism of algebras
\displaystyle\chi\colon H^{\prime}\rightarrow k
, put

(90)

I_{\chi}:=A\mu({\rm Ker}(\chi)).

This is a left ideal of
\displaystyle A
(but in general not a two-sided ideal). Since
\displaystyle{\rm Ker}(\chi)=\{h^{\prime}-\chi(h^{\prime})1,h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}\}
, and
\displaystyle A
is a
\displaystyle H
-module algebra and
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
is stable under
\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}^{r}
,
\displaystyle(i)
in the lemma implies that
\displaystyle I_{\chi}
is
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
-stable. Therefore we can consider the left
\displaystyle(A/I_{\chi})
-submodule
\displaystyle(A/I_{\chi})^{H^{\prime}}\subset A/I_{\chi}
formed by the
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
-invariant elements. Define the map
\displaystyle\varphi_{\chi}\colon H^{\prime}\rightarrow H
,
\displaystyle\textstyle h^{\prime}\mapsto\sum_{(h^{\prime})}\chi(h_{(1)}^{\prime})h_{(2)}^{\prime}
, and denote by
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{|H^{\prime}}\colon H^{\prime}\rightarrow k
the restriction of the counit of
\displaystyle H
.

Proposition 7.3
.

The product of
\displaystyle A
descends to the space
\displaystyle(A/I_{\chi})^{H^{\prime}}
and gives it a structure of algebra. Moreover, if
\displaystyle{\rm Im}(\varphi_{\chi})\subset H^{\prime}
and
\displaystyle\varphi_{\chi}({\rm Ker}(\chi))={\rm Ker}(\varepsilon_{|H^{\prime}})
, then the algebra
\displaystyle(A/I_{\chi})^{H^{\prime}}
is isomorphic to
\displaystyle End_{A}(A/I_{\chi})^{op}
.

Both claims follow from arguments in Proposition 1.5.2 of
[
VV10
]
, to which we refer. The first claim is also detailed in Proposition 3.12 of
[
GJS19
]
. For completeness let us explain it. The point is to observe that a coset
\displaystyle a+I_{\chi}\in A/I_{\chi}
is
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
-invariant if and only if
\displaystyle{\rm Ker}(\varepsilon_{|H^{\prime}})\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}a\subset I_{\chi}
. But
\displaystyle{\rm Ker}(\varepsilon_{|H^{\prime}})
obviously contains the elements
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{(h^{\prime})}\chi(h_{(1)}^{\prime})h_{(2)}^{\prime}\in H
, where
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\in{\rm Ker}(\chi)
, and

\displaystyle\displaystyle\left(\sum_{(h^{\prime})}\chi(h_{(1)}^{\prime})h_{(2)}^{\prime}\right)\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}a

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{(h^{\prime})}(h_{(2)}^{\prime}\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}a)(\mu(h^{\prime}_{(1)}))+\sum_{(h^{\prime})}(h_{(2)}^{\prime}\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}a)(\chi(h^{\prime}_{(1)})-\mu(h_{(1)}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{(h^{\prime})}(h_{(2)}^{\prime}\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}a)\mu(h_{(1)}^{\prime})\quad{\rm modulo}\ I_{\chi}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mu(h^{\prime})a\quad{\rm modulo}\ I_{\chi}.

The third equality follows from the QMM equation in Definition
7.1
. Therefore, for every
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\in{\rm Ker}(\chi)
,
\displaystyle\mu(h^{\prime})a\in{\rm Ker}(\varepsilon_{|H^{\prime}})\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}a+I_{\chi}\subset I_{\chi}
, whence
\displaystyle I_{\chi}a\subset I_{\chi}
, which shows
\displaystyle I_{\chi}
is stable under right multiplication by elements which are
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
-invariant modulo
\displaystyle I_{\chi}
. It follows that the product of
\displaystyle A
descends to
\displaystyle(A/I_{\chi})^{H^{\prime}}
.

From
[
VV10
]
we take:

Definition 7.4
.

The algebra
\displaystyle A/\!\!/_{\!\chi}H^{\prime}:=(A/I_{\chi})^{H^{\prime}}
is the quantum reduction of
\displaystyle A
by the character
\displaystyle\chi
.

By the defining property of
\displaystyle\mu
, it is immediate that given an
\displaystyle A
-module
\displaystyle V
the subspace

V^{H^{\prime},\chi}:=\{v\in V,\mu(h^{\prime})\cdot v=\chi(h^{\prime})v,\forall h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}\}

is a
\displaystyle(A/\!\!/_{\!\chi}H^{\prime})
-module. Therefore we have a functor
\displaystyle A
-Mod
\displaystyle\rightarrow(A/\!\!/_{\!\chi}H^{\prime})
-Mod.

Let us observe the following fact in the case of the quantum reduction by the counit
\displaystyle\varepsilon
, which will be important in Section
7.3
. Consider the canonical quotient map of
\displaystyle H
-modules,
\displaystyle p\colon A\rightarrow A/I_{\varepsilon}
. The restriction
\displaystyle\pi
of
\displaystyle p
to
\displaystyle A^{H^{\prime}}
has image contained in
\displaystyle A/\!\!/_{\!\varepsilon}H^{\prime}
, and as explained after Proposition
7.3
it is a morphism of algebras

(91)

\pi\colon A^{H^{\prime}}\rightarrow A/\!\!/_{\!\varepsilon}H^{\prime}.

Denote by
\displaystyle Z(H^{\prime})
the center of
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
.

Definition 7.5
.

We will say that
\displaystyle Z(H^{\prime})
separates the simple types if the following property holds: for any finite collection of non isomorphic and non trivial simple
\displaystyle H
-modules
\displaystyle X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}
, there exists an element
\displaystyle z\in Z(H^{\prime})
such that
\displaystyle\varepsilon(z)=0
and
\displaystyle z\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}v=z_{i}v
for every
\displaystyle v\in X_{i}
, where
\displaystyle z_{i}\in k
satisfy
\displaystyle z_{i}\neq z_{j}
for
\displaystyle i\neq j
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Assume that the
\displaystyle H
-module
\displaystyle A
is completely reducible and
\displaystyle Z(H^{\prime})
separates the simple types. Then the map
\displaystyle\pi
is surjective.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle a+I_{\varepsilon}\in A/\!\!/_{\!\varepsilon}H^{\prime}
. By definition we have
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}a-\varepsilon(h^{\prime})a\in I_{\varepsilon}
, for every
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
. Since
\displaystyle A
is completely reducible, we can decompose
\displaystyle a
in the form
\displaystyle a=\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{k}a_{i}
with
\displaystyle a_{i}
the isotypical component of
\displaystyle a
of type
\displaystyle X_{i}
,
\displaystyle i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, and
\displaystyle a_{0}
the isotypical component of
\displaystyle a
of trivial type, ie. the
\displaystyle H
-invariant component of
\displaystyle a
. Then

h^{\prime}\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}a-\varepsilon(h^{\prime})a=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(h^{\prime}\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}a_{i}-\varepsilon(h^{\prime})a_{i}).

Let us apply this to an element
\displaystyle z\in Z(H^{\prime})
, separating the components of type
\displaystyle X_{i}
as in Definition
7.5
. For every integer
\displaystyle s\geq 1
we have

z^{s}\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}a-\varepsilon(z^{s})a=\sum_{i=1}^{k}z_{i}^{s}a_{i}.

Therefore
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}z_{i}^{s}a_{i}\in I_{\varepsilon}
for every
\displaystyle s\geq 1
, with
\displaystyle z_{i}\neq z_{j}
for
\displaystyle i\neq j
. Because the Vandermonde matrix
\displaystyle(z_{i}^{s})
is invertible for
\displaystyle 1\leq s\leq k
, we find
\displaystyle a_{i}\in I_{\varepsilon}
for every
\displaystyle i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
. Therefore
\displaystyle a+I_{\varepsilon}=a_{0}+I_{\varepsilon}
, showing that
\displaystyle\pi
is surjective. ∎

7.2.
A quantum moment map for
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)

Let
\displaystyle H
be a ribbon Hopf algebra with an invertible antipode; we denote the ribbon element of
\displaystyle H
by
\displaystyle v
.

Assumption: the morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to H
from (
37
) is injective.

When
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
is an isomorphism,
\displaystyle H
is called
factorizable
. In general, let
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
be the image of the morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
. Then
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
is a subalgebra of
\displaystyle H
such that
\displaystyle H^{\prime}\subset H^{\mathrm{lf}}
. The quantum group
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
satisfies the above assumption, up to a slight adaptation: in that case
\displaystyle H^{\prime}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathrm{lf}}
by
[
Bau00
, Th. 3]
.

Let us quote the following well-known fact:

Lemma 7.7
.

\displaystyle H^{\prime}
is a right coideal (i.e.
\displaystyle\Delta(H^{\prime})\subset H^{\prime}\otimes H
) and is stable by the right adjoint action
\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}^{r}
of
\displaystyle H
.

Proof.

By definition an element of
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
has the form
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2})}\varphi\bigl(R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(2)}\bigr)R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}
for some
\displaystyle\varphi\in H^{\circ}
. Let us compute the coproduct of such an element:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2}),(R^{1}_{(2)}),(R^{2}_{(1)})}\varphi\bigl(R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(2)}\bigr)\,(R^{1}_{(2)})_{(1)}\,(R^{2}_{(1)})_{(1)}\otimes(R^{1}_{(2)})_{(2)}\,(R^{2}_{(1)})_{(2)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),\ldots,(R^{4})}\varphi\bigl(R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(1)}R^{3}_{(2)}R^{4}_{(2)}\bigr)\,R^{2}_{(2)}R^{3}_{(1)}\otimes R^{1}_{(2)}R^{4}_{(1)}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{4}),(\varphi)}\varphi_{(1)}\bigl(R^{1}_{(1)}\bigr)\,\varphi_{(3)}\bigl(R^{4}_{(2)}\bigr)\,\Phi_{0,1}(\varphi_{(2)})\otimes R^{1}_{(2)}R^{4}_{(1)}\in H^{\prime}\otimes H.

The first equality is by quasi-triangularity of
\displaystyle H
while the second is by definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
and of the coproduct in
\displaystyle H^{\circ}
. The last claim of the lemma is obvious, since
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}:(\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H),\mathrm{coad}^{r})\to(H,\mathrm{ad}^{r})
is
\displaystyle H
-linear.
∎

Remark 7.8
.

For any Hopf algebra
\displaystyle H
, the subalgebra
\displaystyle H^{\mathrm{lf}}
is always a right coideal subalgebra of
\displaystyle H
(see
[
KLNY20
]
, Theorem 1).

Recall the matrices
\displaystyle\overset{V}{B}(i),\overset{V}{A}(i),\overset{V}{M}(j)\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\otimes\mathrm{End}(V)
from (
61
), where
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq g
and
\displaystyle g+1\leq j\leq g+n
and
\displaystyle V
is any finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-module. Let

(92)

\overset{V}{C}(i)=v^{2}_{V}\,\overset{V}{B}(i)\overset{V}{A}(i){{}^{-1}}\overset{V}{B}(i){{}^{-1}}\overset{V}{A}(i)

where
\displaystyle v^{2}_{V}\in\mathrm{End}(V)
is the representation of
\displaystyle v^{2}
on
\displaystyle V
. It is well-known that the matrices
\displaystyle\overset{V}{B}(i),\overset{V}{A}(i),\overset{V}{M}(j)
are invertible (see e.g.
[
Fai19b
, Lem. 6.1.2]
), so this formula makes sense.

Denote by
{}_{V}\phi^{k}_{l}\in H^{\circ}
the matrix coefficients of
\displaystyle V
and by
\displaystyle\overset{V}{C}(i){{}^{k}_{l}}
the coefficients of
\displaystyle\overset{V}{C}(i)
, both taken in some basis of
\displaystyle V
. Consider the linear map given by

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mu^{(i)}:&H^{\prime}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\\
&\Phi_{0,1}\bigl(_{V}\phi^{k}_{l}\bigr)&\longmapsto&\overset{V}{C}(i){{}^{k}_{l}}\end{array}

for all
\displaystyle V
,
\displaystyle k
and
\displaystyle l
. It is well-defined since by assumption
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
is injective.

Lemma 7.9
.

\displaystyle\mu^{(i)}
is a morphism of algebras.

Proof.

This follows from the definition of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{0,1}(H)
(see (
30
)), the fact that
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
is a morphism of algebras, and finally Lemma
3.5
. ∎

Remark 7.10
.

By its proof, Lemma
7.9
is equivalent to the set of fusion relations

(93)

\overset{V\otimes W}{C(i)}=\overset{V}{C(i)}_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{C(i)}_{2}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}^{-1}.

Similar relations are satisfied for any collection of matrices which like
\displaystyle C(i)
“lifts” in a suitable sense a simple closed curve in
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}\!\setminus\!D
. For complete details on this we refer to
[
Fai19b
, Prop. 5.3.14]
(see also the seminal work
[
AS96a
, §9.1]
, but with different conventions). As shows the proof of Lemma
3.5
, the definition of such a lift needs a normalization factor which is a suitable power of
\displaystyle v_{V}
.

Definition 7.11
.

For any finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-module
\displaystyle V
, we define

\displaystyle\displaystyle\overset{V}{C}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\overset{V}{C}(1)\ldots\overset{V}{C}(g)\overset{V}{M}(g+1)\ldots\overset{V}{M}(g+n)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=v^{2g}_{V}\ \prod_{i=1}^{g}\ [\overset{V}{B}(i),\overset{V}{A}(i){{}^{-1}}]\ \prod_{j=1}^{n}\ \overset{V}{M}(g+j)\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\otimes\mathrm{End}(V).

Note that if
\displaystyle b_{i},a_{i},m_{j}
(
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq g
,
\displaystyle g+1\leq j\leq g+n
) are the standard generators of
\displaystyle\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n}\!\setminus\!D)
, then
\displaystyle\partial(\Sigma_{g,n}\!\setminus\!D)=b_{1}a_{1}^{-1}b_{1}^{-1}a_{1}\ldots b_{g}a_{g}^{-1}b_{g}^{-1}a_{g}\,m_{g+1}\ldots m_{g+n}
. So the matrices
\displaystyle\overset{V}{C}
are in some sense a “lift” of
\displaystyle\partial(\Sigma_{g,n}\!\setminus\!D)
.

Consider the linear map given by

(94)

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mu:&H^{\prime}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\\
&\Phi_{0,1}\bigl(_{V}\phi^{k}_{l}\bigr)&\longmapsto&\overset{V}{C}{{}^{k}_{l}}\end{array}

for all
\displaystyle V
,
\displaystyle k
and
\displaystyle l
. In a component-free form,
\displaystyle\mu
is given by

(95)

\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2})}\mu\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(2)}\bigr)_{V}=\overset{V}{C}.

Lemma 7.12
.

\displaystyle\mu
is a morphism of algebras.

Proof.

Since
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
is a morphism of algebras it suffices to show that the collection of matrices
\displaystyle C
satisfies the fusion relation (
30
). For ease of notation let us write
\displaystyle\overset{V}{C}(j)=\overset{V}{M}(j)
for
\displaystyle g+1\leq j\leq g+n
, so that
\displaystyle\overset{V}{C}=\overset{V}{C}(1)\ldots\overset{V}{C}(g+n)
. We saw in (
93
) that the collection of matrices
\displaystyle\overset{V}{C}(i)
satisfies the fusion relation for all
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq g+n
. Straightforward computations using Proposition
4.5
reveal that we also have

R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{C}(j)_{1}\,R{{}_{V,W}^{-1}}\,\overset{W}{C}(i)_{2}=\overset{W}{C}(i)_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{C}(j)_{1}\,R{{}_{V,W}^{-1}}\quad\quad\text{for }1\leq j<i\leq g+n.

The fusion relation for
\displaystyle\overset{V}{C}
follows from all these relations, arguing like in Lemma
3.5
. ∎

The following commutation relations (written with the notations introduced before Proposition
3.4
) for the collection of matrices
\displaystyle\overset{V}{C}
will play a key role in the proof of the next theorem:

Lemma 7.13
.

Let
\displaystyle V,W
be finite-dimensional
\displaystyle H
-modules and let
\displaystyle\overset{W}{X}(i)
be
\displaystyle\overset{W}{B}(i)
,
\displaystyle\overset{W}{A}(i)
or
\displaystyle\overset{W}{M}(i)
. We have

R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{C}_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{X}(i)_{2}=\overset{W}{X}(i)_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{C}_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}

in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\otimes\mathrm{End}(V)\otimes\mathrm{End}(W)
.

Proof.

Recall the notation
\displaystyle\overset{V}{C}(j)=\overset{V}{M}(j)
for
\displaystyle g+1\leq j\leq g+n
from the proof of Lemma
7.12
. Straightforward computations using Proposition
4.5
reveal that

\displaystyle\displaystyle(R^{\prime}){{}_{V,W}^{-1}}\,\overset{V}{C}(j)_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{X}(i)_{2}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\overset{W}{X}(i)_{2}\,(R^{\prime}){{}_{V,W}^{-1}}\,\overset{V}{C}(j)_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\quad\text{for }1\leq i<j\leq g+n,

(96)

\displaystyle\displaystyle R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{C}(i)_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\,\overset{W}{X}(i)_{2}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\overset{W}{X}(i)_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{C}(i)_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq g+n

\displaystyle\displaystyle R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{C}(j)_{1}\,R{{}_{V,W}^{-1}}\,\overset{W}{X}(i)_{2}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\overset{W}{X}(i)_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{C}(j)_{1}\,R{{}_{V,W}^{-1}}\quad\quad\text{for }1\leq j<i\leq g+n.

The result easily follows.
∎

Let us define a left action

\displaystyle\blacktriangleright

of
\displaystyle H
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
by

h\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}x=\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(S^{-1}(h)\bigr)(x)

for
\displaystyle h\in H
,
\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
is defined in (
53
). Since
\displaystyle S
is an anti-morphism of bialgebras,

\displaystyle\blacktriangleright

endows
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
with the structure of
\displaystyle H^{\mathrm{cop}}
-module-algebra:
\displaystyle\textstyle h\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}(xy)=\sum_{(h)}(h_{(2)}\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}x)(h_{(1)}\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}y)
. If
\displaystyle\overset{V}{X}(i)
is one of the matrices
\displaystyle\overset{V}{B}(i),\overset{V}{A}(i),\overset{V}{M}(i)
we define the matrix
\displaystyle h\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}\overset{V}{X}(i)
in the obvious way on coefficients:
\displaystyle\bigl(h\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}\overset{V}{X}(i)\bigr)^{k}_{l}=h\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}\bigl(\overset{V}{X}(i){{}^{k}_{l}}\bigr)
for all
\displaystyle 1\leq k,l\leq\dim(V)
. One checks easily that

(97)

h\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}\overset{V}{X}(i)=\sum_{(h)}S^{-1}(h_{(2)})_{V}\,\overset{V}{X}(i)\,(h_{(1)})_{V}.

Clearly, this applies also to the matrix
\displaystyle\overset{V}{C}
, because
\displaystyle h\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}\overset{V}{X}(i)^{-1}
is given by the same formula.

Theorem 7.14
.

The morphism
\displaystyle\mu:H^{\prime}\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is a quantum moment map:

\mu(h^{\prime})\,x=\sum_{(h^{\prime})}\,(h^{\prime}_{(2)}\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}x)\,\mu(h^{\prime}_{(1)})

for all
\displaystyle h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
and
\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
.

Proof.

Note first that, since
\displaystyle\textstyle(R^{\prime})_{V,W}=\sum_{(R)}(R_{(2)})_{V,1}(R_{(1)})_{W,2}
and
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2})}R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(1)}\otimes R^{2}_{(2)}S\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}\bigr)=1\otimes 1
, the commutation relations of Lemma
7.13
can be rewritten as:

(98)

\overset{V}{C}_{1}\,\overset{W}{X}(i)_{2}\,=\,\sum_{(R)}(R_{(1)})_{W,2}\,R{{}^{-1}_{V,W}}\,\overset{W}{X}(i)_{2}\,R_{V,W}\,\overset{V}{C}_{1}\,(R^{\prime})_{V,W}\,S\bigl(R_{(2)}\bigr)_{V,1}\\
=\sum_{(R^{1}),\ldots,(R^{4})}\Bigl(\bigl(R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(2)}\bigr)_{W}\,\overset{W}{X}(i)\,\bigl(R^{3}_{(2)}R^{4}_{(1)}\bigr)_{W}\Bigr)_{2}\Bigl(\bigl(S\bigl(R^{2}_{(1)}\bigr)R^{3}_{(1)}\bigr)_{V}\,\overset{V}{C}_{1}\,\bigl(R^{4}_{(2)}S\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr)_{V}\Bigr)_{1}.

For the second equality we split all the
\displaystyle R
-matrices and we used obvious commutation relations to rearrange the terms; also recall that
\displaystyle R^{-1}=(S\otimes\mathrm{id})(R)
. To prove the theorem we can assume that
\displaystyle h^{\prime}
is a coefficient of some matrix
\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2})}R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}\otimes\bigl(R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(2)}\bigr)_{V}\in H^{\prime}\otimes\mathrm{End}(V)
and that
\displaystyle x
is a coefficient of some matrix
\displaystyle\overset{W}{X}(i)\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\otimes\mathrm{End}(W)
which is
\displaystyle\overset{W}{B}(i),\overset{W}{A}(i)
or
\displaystyle\overset{W}{M}(i)
. This reduces the proof to a matrix computation in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\otimes\mathrm{End}(V)\otimes\mathrm{End}(W)
based on (
95
). Starting from the right-hand side of the desired formula we get:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2}),(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)})}\Bigl(\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}\bigr)_{(2)}\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}\overset{W}{X}(i)\Bigr)_{2}\,\Bigl(\mu\bigl(\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}\bigr)_{(1)}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(2)}\bigr)_{V}\Bigr)_{1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),\ldots,(R^{4})}\Bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{4}_{(1)}\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}\overset{W}{X}(i)\Bigr)_{2}\,\Bigl(\mu\bigl(R^{2}_{(2)}R^{3}_{(1)}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(R^{1}_{(1)}\,R^{2}_{(1)}\,R^{3}_{(2)}\,R^{4}_{(2)}\bigr)_{V}\Bigr)_{1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2})}\Bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}\vbox{\hbox{\,\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle\blacktriangleright$}\,}}\overset{W}{X}(i)\Bigr)_{2}\,\Bigl(\bigl(R^{1}_{(1)}\bigr)_{V}\,\overset{V}{C}\,\bigl(R^{2}_{(2)}\bigr)_{V}\Bigr)_{1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),\ldots,(R^{4})}\Bigl(S^{-1}\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{4}_{(1)}\bigr)_{W}\,\overset{W}{X}(i)\,\bigl(R^{2}_{(2)}R^{3}_{(1)}\bigr)_{W}\Bigr)_{2}\,\Bigl(\bigl(R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(1)}\bigr)_{V}\,\overset{V}{C}\,\bigl(R^{3}_{(2)}R^{4}_{(2)}\bigr)_{V}\Bigr)_{1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{(R^{1}),\ldots,(R^{4})}\Bigl(\bigl(R^{4}_{(1)}R^{1}_{(2)}\bigr)_{W}\,\overset{W}{X}(i)\,\bigl(R^{2}_{(2)}R^{3}_{(1)}\bigr)_{W}\Bigr)_{2}\,\Bigl(\bigl(S\bigl(R^{1}_{(1)}\bigr)R^{2}_{(1)}\bigr)_{V}\,\overset{V}{C}\,\bigl(R^{3}_{(2)}S\bigl(R^{4}_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr)_{V}\Bigr)_{1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle\displaystyle\overset{V}{C}_{1}\,\overset{W}{X}(i)_{2}=\sum_{(R^{1}),(R^{2})}\Bigl(\mu\bigl(R^{1}_{(2)}R^{2}_{(1)}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(R^{1}_{(1)}R^{2}_{(2)}\bigr)_{V}\Bigr)_{1}\,\overset{W}{X}(i)_{2}.

For the first equality we used the quasi-triangularity of
\displaystyle R
, for the second equality we used the definition of
\displaystyle\mu
, for the third equality we used (
97
) and the quasi-triangularity of
\displaystyle R
, for the fourth equality we used that
\displaystyle S
is an anti-morphism and that
\displaystyle(S\otimes S)(R)=R
, for the fifth equality we used (
98
) and for the last equality we used the definition of
\displaystyle\mu
in (
95
).
∎

Remark 7.15
.

In the case
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and
\displaystyle(g,n)=(1,0)
, quantum moment maps have been obtained in
[
VV10
]
, Section 1.8, and
[
Jor14
]
. In that situation, Theorem
7.14
is Proposition 7.21 of
[
Jor14
]
, and Definition 7.25 of
[
Jor14
]
provides the extension to any
\displaystyle(g,n)
by external tensor product. The proof in that paper uses a matrix
\displaystyle\tilde{R}
, which in our notations is
\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}\otimes S)(R)
evaluated in the fundamental representation of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.

7.3.
The quantum reduction of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}(H)
at the character
\displaystyle\varepsilon

In this section we assume the following hypothesis hold for
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}(H)
:

•

\displaystyle H
is a ribbon Hopf algebra over a field
\displaystyle k
, with an invertible antipode.

•

The algebra morphism
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}:\mathcal{L}_{0,1}(H)\to H
is injective.

Denoting by
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
is the image of
\displaystyle\Phi_{0,1}
, we thus have the quantum moment map defined in (
94
),

\mu\colon H^{\prime}\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}(H).

•

The
\displaystyle H
-module
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}(H)
is completely reducible.

•

\displaystyle Z(H^{\prime})
separates the simple types.

•

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}^{H^{\prime}}(H)={\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
.

In particular, Lemma
7.6
and the results of Section
7.2
apply. Denote by
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{qr}}(H)
the quantum reduction
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)/\!\!/_{\varepsilon}\,H^{\prime}=(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)/I_{\varepsilon})^{H^{\prime}}
associated to the counit
\displaystyle\varepsilon
(see Definition
7.4
). Denote

\mathfrak{C}_{g,n}(H):={\rm Vect}_{k}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{the matrix coefficients of the matrices }\overset{W}{C}-{\rm id}_{W}\\
\quad\>\>\text{for all finite dimensional }H\text{-modules\ }W\end{array}\right\}.

From (
90
) and (
94
) we see that
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}
is the left ideal of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}(H)
of the form

(99)

I_{\varepsilon}={\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}(H)\mathfrak{C}_{g,n}(H).

By Lemma
7.6
and the hypothesis
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}^{H^{\prime}}(H)={\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
, we have a surjective morphism of algebras

\pi\colon\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{qr}}(H).

It follows:

Proposition 7.16
.

Under the above hypothesis, if
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
is a Noetherian algebra, then
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{qr}}(H)
is a Noetherian algebra, and if
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
is a finitely generated algebra over
\displaystyle k
, then
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{qr}}(H)
is a finitely generated algebra over
\displaystyle k
.

Recall the Reynolds operator
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}(H)\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
. Note that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(I_{\varepsilon})
is a two-sided ideal of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
, thanks to Lemma
4.14
.

Proposition 7.17
.

We have
\displaystyle{\rm Ker}(\pi)=I_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)=\mathfrak{R}(I_{\varepsilon})
. Therefore
\displaystyle\pi
factors to an isomorphism
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}^{H}(H)/\mathfrak{R}(I_{\varepsilon})\cong(\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)/I_{\varepsilon})^{H^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

The first equality and the inclusion
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)\subset\mathfrak{R}(I_{\varepsilon})
are immediate, by the definitions of
\displaystyle\pi
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}
respectively. For the converse inclusion, it is enough to show that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(I_{\varepsilon})\subset I_{\varepsilon}
. It follows from the remark below (
97
) that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{g,n}(H)
is an
\displaystyle H
-submodule. Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is an
\displaystyle H
-module algebra,
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}={\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}(H)\mathfrak{C}_{g,n}(H)
is an
\displaystyle H
-submodule too. Because
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
is completely reducible,
\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}
is also completely reducible. Because
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}
is the projection onto the isotypical components of trivial type, we obtain
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(I_{\varepsilon})\subset I_{\varepsilon}
.
∎

Before continuing, we begin with a few simple observations. Let
\displaystyle V
be a finite dimensional
\displaystyle H
-module. We say that a tensor

t=\sum_{i=1}^{\dim V}a_{i}\otimes v_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\otimes V

is
equivariant
if for every
\displaystyle h\in H
we have

\sum_{i=1}^{\dim V}\mathrm{coad}^{r}(h)(a_{i})\otimes v_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{\dim V}a_{i}\otimes h_{V}(v_{i})

where we recall that
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
is defined in (
53
) and
\displaystyle h_{V}
is the representation of
\displaystyle h
on
\displaystyle V
. A main example of equivariant tensor is provided by the matrix
\displaystyle\overset{W}{X}\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\otimes\mathrm{End}(W)
which is
\displaystyle\overset{W}{B}(i),\overset{W}{A}(i)
,
\displaystyle\overset{W}{M}(i)
or any product of them and their inverses, like
\displaystyle\overset{W}{C}
, taking
\displaystyle V=\mathrm{End}(W)\cong W\otimes W^{*}
. Indeed, expanding
\displaystyle\overset{W}{X}=\textstyle\sum_{i,j}\overset{W}{X}{{}^{i}_{j}}\otimes w_{i}\otimes w^{j}
where
\displaystyle(w_{i})
is a basis of
\displaystyle V
with dual basis
\displaystyle(w^{j})
, we have by (
89
),

\sum_{i,j}\mathrm{coad}^{r}\bigl(\overset{W}{X}{{}^{i}_{j}}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(w_{i}\otimes w^{j}\bigr)=\sum_{(h),i,j}\overset{W}{X}{{}^{i}_{j}}\otimes\bigl(h_{(1)}\cdot w_{i}\otimes h_{(2)}\cdot w^{j}\bigr).

Note also the following easy but crucial fact. Assume the
\displaystyle H
-module
\displaystyle A
is completely reducible, and denote by
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}\colon A\rightarrow A^{H}
the Reynolds operator (
i.e.
the projection on the submodule of
\displaystyle H
-invariant elements). With
\displaystyle V
and an equivariant tensor
\displaystyle t
as above, we have

(100)

\big(\mathfrak{R}\otimes{\rm id}_{V}\big)(t)=\big({\rm id}\otimes P\big)(t)

where
\displaystyle P\colon V\rightarrow V
is the projector onto the isotypical component of trivial type, so
\displaystyle{\rm Im}(P)=V^{H}
. The proof is immediate by taking a basis
\displaystyle(v_{i})
adapted to the decomposition
\displaystyle V={\rm Im}(P)\oplus{\rm Ker}(P)
. Also, given another finite dimensional
\displaystyle H
-module
\displaystyle V^{\prime}
with a basis
\displaystyle(v^{\prime}_{j})
, and an equivariant tensor
\displaystyle\textstyle t^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{\dim V^{\prime}}a_{i}^{\prime}\otimes v_{i}^{\prime}\in A\otimes V^{\prime}
, the tensor

t_{1}t^{\prime}_{2}=\sum_{i,j}a_{i}a_{j}^{\prime}\otimes v_{i}\otimes v^{\prime}_{j}\in A\otimes V\otimes V^{\prime}

is equivariant.

In the next statement we use the graphical calculus of
[
Fai20b
, §3]
. It proves the definition of the ideal
\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{C}
in
[
BNR02
, p. 10]
in the case of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
.

Proposition 7.18
.

The vector space
\displaystyle{\rm Ker}(\pi)
is generated by the elements of the form

for all
\displaystyle X_{i},Y\in\mathrm{Irr}(H)
and
\displaystyle f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{H}\textstyle\!\left((\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2g+n}X_{i}\otimes X_{i}^{*})\otimes Y\otimes Y^{*},k\right)
.

Proof.

To simplify notations and diagrams we give the details in the case
\displaystyle(g,n)=(1,1)
. Because of (
99
) and
\displaystyle{\rm Ker}(\pi)=\mathfrak{R}(I_{\varepsilon})
, it is enough to show that if
\displaystyle x(\overset{Y}{C}-{\rm id}_{Y})^{i}_{j}
with
\displaystyle x\in{\mathcal{L}}_{1,1}(H)
satisfies
\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(x(\overset{Y}{C}-{\rm id}_{Y})^{i}_{j})=x(\overset{Y}{C}-{\rm id}_{Y})^{i}_{j}
, then it is of the form required. Without loss of generality, by linearity we can assume
\displaystyle x\in C(X_{1})\otimes C(X_{2})\otimes C(X_{3})
for some simple modules
\displaystyle X_{1}
,
\displaystyle X_{2}
,
\displaystyle X_{3}
. Let
\displaystyle S\subset C(X_{1})\otimes C(X_{2})\otimes C(X_{3})
be the submodule generated by
\displaystyle x
under
\displaystyle\mathrm{coad}^{r}
. We thus have an isomorphism
\displaystyle f\colon\mathrm{coad}^{r}(H)(x)\rightarrow S
. We can view
\displaystyle S
as a submodule (a direct summand) of
\displaystyle\textstyle\bigotimes_{i=1}^{3}X_{i}\otimes X_{i}^{*}
. Denote by
\displaystyle p_{S}\colon\textstyle\bigotimes_{i=1}^{3}X_{i}\otimes X_{i}^{*}\rightarrow S
the associated projection. Define the tensor
\displaystyle t_{x}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{1,1}(H)\otimes S
by

Letting
\displaystyle({}_{k}x_{r})
and
\displaystyle({}_{k}x^{r})
be dual basis of
\displaystyle X_{k}
and
\displaystyle X_{k}^{*}
, this reads

t_{x}=\sum_{a,b,k,l,m,n}\overset{X_{1}}{B(1)}{}^{a}_{b}\otimes\overset{X_{2}}{A(1)}{}^{k}_{l}\otimes\overset{X_{3}}{M(2)}{}^{m}_{n}\otimes p_{S}\big({}_{1}x_{a}\otimes{}_{1}x^{b}\otimes{}_{2}x_{k}\otimes{}_{2}x^{l}\otimes{}_{3}x_{m}\otimes{}_{3}x^{n}\big).

By construction
\displaystyle t_{x}
is an equivariant tensor. Let
\displaystyle(s_{r})
be a basis of
\displaystyle S
, chosen so that
\displaystyle f(x)=s_{1}
. Then it is immediate that
\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes s^{1})(t_{x})=x
. It follows that
\displaystyle(t_{x})_{1}(\overset{Y}{C}-{\rm id}_{Y})_{2}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{1,1}(H)\otimes S\otimes Y\otimes Y^{*}
is equivariant, and therefore

(\mathfrak{R}\otimes{\rm id})\big((t_{x})_{1}(\overset{Y}{C}-{\rm id}_{Y})_{2}\big)=({\rm id}\otimes P)\big((t_{x})_{1}(\overset{Y}{C}-{\rm id}_{Y})_{2}\big)

where
\displaystyle P\colon S\otimes Y\otimes Y^{*}\rightarrow S\otimes Y\otimes Y^{*}
is the projection onto the subspace of invariant elements. Letting
\displaystyle(y_{s})
and
\displaystyle(y^{t})
be dual basis of
\displaystyle Y
and
\displaystyle Y^{*}
, denote by
\displaystyle\eta\colon S\otimes Y\otimes Y^{*}\rightarrow k
the linear form such that
\displaystyle\eta(s_{r}\otimes y_{s}\otimes y^{t})=\delta_{r,1}\delta_{s,i}\delta_{t,j}
. Then we have

x(\overset{Y}{C}-{\rm id}_{Y})^{i}_{j}=({\rm id}\otimes\eta)\big((t_{x})_{1}(\overset{Y}{C}-{\rm id}_{Y})_{2}\big),

and

\displaystyle\displaystyle({\rm id}\otimes(\eta\circ P))\big((t_{x})_{1}(\overset{Y}{C}-{\rm id}_{Y})_{2}\big)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{R}\otimes\eta)\big((t_{x})_{1}(\overset{Y}{C}-{\rm id}_{Y})_{2}\big)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}\big(x(\overset{Y}{C}-{\rm id}_{Y})^{i}_{j}\big)=x(\overset{Y}{C}-{\rm id}_{Y})^{i}_{j}.

The map
\displaystyle\eta\circ P\colon S\otimes Y\otimes Y^{*}\rightarrow k
is
\displaystyle H
-linear, and the left-hand side of the identity is the element of the form

This concludes the proof.
∎

Finally, note that the hypothesis stated at the beginning of the section are satisfied by
\displaystyle H=U^{\mathrm{ad}}_{q}=U^{\mathrm{ad}}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. Indeed, the only ones that remain to be checked are the last two. As already mentioned, we have
\displaystyle H^{\prime}=U_{q}^{{\rm lf}}
by
[
Bau00
, Th. 3]
. Also we have the following decomposition
[
JL92
]
:

U_{q}=T_{2-}^{-1}U_{q}^{\rm lf}[T/T_{2}]

where
\displaystyle T\subset U_{q}
is the multiplicative Abelian group formed by the elements
\displaystyle K_{\lambda}
,
\displaystyle\lambda\in P
, and we denote by
\displaystyle T_{2}\subset T
the subgroup formed by the elements
\displaystyle K_{\lambda}
,
\displaystyle\lambda\in 2P
, and by
\displaystyle T_{2-}\subset T_{2}
the set formed by the elements
\displaystyle K_{-\lambda}
,
\displaystyle\lambda\in 2P_{+}
. As a result

Z(U_{q}^{{\rm lf}})=Z(U_{q})\quad{\rm and}\quad{\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}^{U_{q}^{{\rm lf}}}={\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}^{U_{q}}.

Finally, there is an algebra isomorphism
\displaystyle\Xi\colon Z(U_{q})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2\varpi_{1}}^{\pm 1},\ldots,K_{2\varpi_{N}}^{\pm 1}]^{W}
, where
\displaystyle W
is acting by the shifted Weyl group action (see eg.
[
VY20
]
, Section 3.13). Then let
\displaystyle X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}
be non trivial and non isomorphic simple type
\displaystyle 1
finite dimensional
\displaystyle U_{q}
-modules. They are highest weight modules, of non-zero dominant weights
\displaystyle\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}
lying in disjoint
\displaystyle W
-orbits. Therefore, there exists an element
\displaystyle P\in\mathbb{C}(q)[K_{2\varpi_{1}}^{\pm 1},\ldots,K_{2\varpi_{N}}^{\pm 1}]^{W}
such that
\displaystyle P(0)=0
and
\displaystyle P(\lambda_{i})\neq P(\lambda_{j})
for
\displaystyle i\neq j
. Taking
\displaystyle z=\Xi^{-1}(P)
provides an element satisfying the separation property of Definition
7.5
.

By Proposition
7.16
and Theorem
4.17
, we get:

Corollary 7.19
.

The algebra
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{g,n}^{{\rm qr}}(\mathfrak{g})
is Noetherian and finitely generated.

7.4.
Topological interpretation of the quantum reduction
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{qr}}(H)

Let us call
\displaystyle\partial
-move
the transformation shown in the picture below; this picture represents a neighborhood of the boundary of
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
, and the strand represents the projection on
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
of a portion of some
\displaystyle H
-colored ribbon link with coupons in
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}\times[0,1]
.

As usual we consider such links up to isotopy. We say that two linear combinations of links
\displaystyle L_{1}
and
\displaystyle L_{2}
are
\displaystyle\partial
-equivalent, denoted by
\displaystyle L_{1}\sim_{\partial}L_{2}
, if their diagrams on
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
can be related by a finite sequence of
\displaystyle\partial
-moves. Because the skein relations are local, the relation
\displaystyle\sim_{\partial}
is compatible with skein equivalence, in the sense that if
\displaystyle L_{1}\sim_{\partial}L_{2}
and if
\displaystyle L_{1}
(resp.
\displaystyle L_{2}
) is skein equivalent to
\displaystyle L^{\prime}_{1}
(resp. to
\displaystyle L^{\prime}_{2}
), then
\displaystyle L^{\prime}_{1}\sim_{\partial}L^{\prime}_{2}
. Also,
\displaystyle\sim_{\partial}
is compatible with the product in
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})
, which we recall is given by stacking. Hence
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})/\sim_{\partial}
is an algebra.

For the same reason the inclusion
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}\subset\Sigma_{g,n}
yields a well-defined, surjective morphism of algebras
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n})
, and skein classes of
\displaystyle\partial
-equivalent
\displaystyle H
-colored ribbon links have the same image. Since (diagrams of) isotopic links in
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}
can be related by finite sequences of
\displaystyle\partial
-moves and isotopies in the subsurface
\displaystyle\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ}
, it factors into an isomorphism

\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})/\sim_{\partial}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}).

Below we will identify
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})/\sim_{\partial}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n})
by using this isomorphism.

Recall the Wilson loop morphism
\displaystyle W:\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
(Definition
6.8
; as explained thereafter,
\displaystyle W
takes values in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
), and recall that
\displaystyle\pi\colon\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\rm qr}(H)
denotes the restriction to
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
of the canonical projection
\displaystyle p:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)/I_{\epsilon}
(see (
91
)).

Proposition 7.20
.

If
\displaystyle L_{1}\sim_{\partial}L_{2}
then
\displaystyle\pi\circ W(L_{1})=\pi\circ W(L_{2})
. It follows that there is a morphism of algebras
\displaystyle W^{\mathrm{qr}}:\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{qr}}(H)
such that the diagram

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\sim_{\partial}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{W}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\pi}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{W^{\mathrm{qr}}}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{qr}}(H)}

commutes.

We do the proof for
\displaystyle(g,n)=(1,1)
. The general case is similar except that it requires to draw more cumbersome diagrams. The key-point is the following lemma, which uses the graphical calculus from

, §3]
.

Proof of Proposition
7.20
.

Consider the following
\displaystyle H
-colored ribbon links in
\displaystyle\Sigma_{1,1}^{\circ}\times[0,1]
which are related by a
\displaystyle\partial
-move:

where
\displaystyle T
is some ribbon graph in
\displaystyle[0,1]^{3}
. We denote them by
\displaystyle L_{T}
and
\displaystyle L^{\partial}_{T}
respectively and we want to show that
\displaystyle\pi\circ W(L^{\partial}_{T})=\pi\circ W(L_{T})
. Recall that
\displaystyle p:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)/I_{\varepsilon}
denotes the canonical projection. If
\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbf{x}=\sum_{i}x_{i}\otimes v_{1,i}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{k,i}\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)\otimes V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{k}
is some tensor, define
\displaystyle\textstyle p(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i}p(x_{i})\otimes v_{1,i}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{k,i}
. Note that
\displaystyle W(L^{\partial}_{T})
is equal to the diagram obtained by plugging the tangle
\displaystyle T
atop the diagram at the left-hand side of Lemma
7.21
. By (
99
) we have
\displaystyle p\bigl(a\overset{X}{C}\bigr)=p(a)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X}
for all
\displaystyle a\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}(H)
, and hence

Since the map
\displaystyle\pi
is just the restriction of
\displaystyle p
to
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)
, applying this equality to the right-hand side of Lemma
7.21
we recover
\displaystyle\pi\circ W(L_{T})
, as desired.
∎

Theorem 7.22
.

Assume the hypothesis of §
7.3
hold true. Then
\displaystyle W^{\rm qr}\colon\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{qr}}(H)
is an isomorphism of algebras.

Note that in particular this result applies to
\displaystyle H=U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
, where as usual the ribbon links are colored by the objects and morphisms from the category of finite-dimensional
\displaystyle U_{q}^{\mathrm{ad}}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules of type
\displaystyle 1
.

Proof.

Under the hypothesis,
\displaystyle W\colon\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{g,n}^{\circ})\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{L}^{H}_{g,n}(H)
is an isomorphism of algebras (Theorem
6.9
) and
\displaystyle\pi:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{H}(H)\to\mathcal{L}_{g,n}^{\rm qr}(H)
is surjective (Lemma
7.6
). It follows from its definition in (
101
) that
\displaystyle W^{\mathrm{qr}}
is surjective. To have diagrams with a reasonable size, we prove injectivity for
\displaystyle(g,n)=(1,1)
; this is completely representative of the general situation. So let
\displaystyle L\in\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{1,1}^{\circ})
and assume that
\displaystyle\pi\circ W(L)=0
. Then by Proposition
7.18
we can write

with
\displaystyle X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},Y\in\mathrm{Irr}(H)
, only a finite number of coefficients
\displaystyle\lambda_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},Y}\in\mathbb{C}
are non-zero and
\displaystyle f_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},Y}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{H}\bigl(X_{1}\otimes X_{1}^{*}\otimes\ldots\otimes X_{3}\otimes X_{3}^{*}\otimes Y\otimes Y^{*},k\bigr)
where
\displaystyle k
is the trivial
\displaystyle H
-module. Consider the following ribbon graphs in
\displaystyle[0,1]^{3}
:

and using the notations
\displaystyle L_{T}
,
\displaystyle L^{\partial}_{T}
introduced in the proof of Proposition
7.20
define

L^{\prime}=\sum_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},Y}\lambda_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},Y}\bigl(L_{T(f_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},Y})}^{\partial}-L_{T(f_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},Y})}\bigr)\in\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{1,1}^{\circ}).

Note that
\displaystyle L^{\prime}\sim_{\partial}0
, simply because
\displaystyle L^{\partial}_{T}\sim_{\partial}L_{T}
for any ribbon graph
\displaystyle T
. Moreover, by definition of
\displaystyle W
and by Lemma
7.21
, we have
\displaystyle W(L^{\prime})=W(L)
. It follows that
\displaystyle L^{\prime}=L
since
\displaystyle W
is an isomorphism (Theorem
6.9
). Thus
\displaystyle L\sim_{\partial}0
, which means that
\displaystyle L=0
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{H}(\Sigma_{1,1})
.
∎
